# Paper Set Evidence Digest

Number of papers in this paper set: 357

Each item below is a compact paper-level analysis digest. Use only this digest.

- source: Wu, Z. W., Xu, Z. J., & Zhang, L. Z. (2014). Punishment Mechanism with Self-Adjusting Rules in Spatial Voluntary Public Goods Games. *Communications in Theoretical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The introduction of a punishment mechanism in a spatial voluntary PGG, where players only know their own payoff and update strategies via a self-adjusting rule, can significantly increase average payoff (efficiency) when the cost of punishment is low. This effect is especially pronounced at low values of the multiplication factor r (mpcr), where, without punishment, cooperation and payoff would otherwise be low. The model demonstrates that as punishment cost decreases, the region of high average payoff expands. Memory length also affects outcomes: moderate memory can further improve average payoff, but very high memory may reduce it. The results are robust across initial conditions and show that punishment can transform low-efficiency environments into high-efficiency ones under the right parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial voluntary PGGs, enabling punishment (especially with low punishment cost and fixed punishment effectiveness) can substantially increase average efficiency compared to the control (no punishment) case, particularly when the mpcr is low. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the cost of punishment and the value of mpcr. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that lowering punishment cost or enabling punishment in low-mpcr environments will yield large efficiency gains. The model also highlights the importance of memory length as a moderator. The results are most directly applicable to spatial, voluntary, mixed-strategy PGGs with local information and self-adjusting update rules.

- source: Levine, D. K., & Modica, S. (2016). Peer discipline and incentives within groups. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that peer punishment, when feasible and not prohibitively costly, enables groups to sustain full contribution to public goods and achieve high efficiency, even in large groups. The model provides conditions under which peer discipline is feasible and optimal, and shows that the efficiency gains from punishment depend on the cost and effectiveness of the punishment and audit technology. The model also shows that, in the absence of peer punishment, only small groups can achieve efficient outcomes, while large groups fail to contribute. The results are robust to various extensions, including renegotiation, spillover costs, and generalized auditing procedures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling peer punishment in public goods games increases efficiency, especially as group size increases, provided that punishment and audit costs are not too high. The model gives explicit formulas for group utility as a function of game parameters, including player count, marginal per-capita return, punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency (without punishment), enabling punishment will increase efficiency, particularly in larger groups and when the punishment technology is effective and not too costly. The model also highlights the importance of the cost structure of punishment and auditing, and the quality of monitoring signals, for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment.

- source: Perc, M., Jordan, J. J., Rand, D. G., Wang, Z., Boccaletti, S., & Szolnoki, A. (2017). Statistical physics of human cooperation. *Physics Reports-review Section of Physics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that enabling punishment in public goods games can increase group efficiency and sustain cooperation, especially when punishment is not too costly and is effective at deterring defectors. However, the effect is not universally positive: high punishment costs, the presence of antisocial punishment, or poorly tuned parameters can lead to lower efficiency or even the collapse of cooperation. Adaptive and probabilistic punishment mechanisms can improve efficiency by reducing unnecessary costs. The spatial structure of interactions (e.g., lattices, networks) is critical: it allows for clustering of cooperators and punishers, which can support higher efficiency at lower multiplication factors (mpcr) than in well-mixed populations. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams showing the regions where punishment increases, decreases, or has no effect on efficiency, depending on the interplay of punishment cost, effectiveness, group size, and synergy factor.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly sensitive to game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, mpcr, punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control only when punishment is not too costly and is sufficiently effective, and when the spatial structure allows for clustering of cooperators/punishers. If punishment is costly or antisocial punishment is possible, efficiency gains may be lost or reversed. The paper's phase diagrams and model descriptions can be used to map specific game designs to expected efficiency outcomes, given the control efficiency. However, the results are based on theoretical models and simulations, not direct empirical measurement, so predictions should be interpreted with caution and in the context of the model's assumptions.

- source: Cong, R., Li, K., Wang, L., & Zhao, Q. C. (2016). Cooperation induced by wise incentive allocation in spontaneous institution. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with optional institution membership, the optimal regime for cooperation and group efficiency is achieved when the institution balances punishment and reward. Pure punishment or pure reward is suboptimal: too much punishment collapses the institution, while too much reward allows institutional defectors to dominate. The efficiency of punishment and reward (i.e., their impact per unit cost) increases the range of conditions under which cooperation prevails. The model provides explicit conditions for when institutional cooperators outcompete defectors, and shows that the stationary distribution of strategies (and thus group efficiency) is maximized at intermediate values of the punishment/reward split.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the balance between punishment and reward, as well as the effectiveness of each. It suggests that enabling punishment alone may not always increase efficiency; the presence and strength of reward, and the cost-effectiveness of both, are crucial moderators. For prediction tasks, the model implies that intermediate punishment (with some reward) is optimal for efficiency, and that both the cost and impact of punishment and reward should be considered. The findings are directly relevant for settings where institutional incentives are endogenous and both punishment and reward are possible.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2013). Correlation of Positive and Negative Reciprocity Fails to Confer an Evolutionary Advantage: Phase Transitions to Elementary Strategies. *Physical Review X*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game, punishment is generally the most effective strategy for promoting cooperation and group efficiency, outperforming both reward and the combined punishment+reward strategy except in very narrow parameter regions. The combined strategy (B) can survive and even outperform elementary strategies only when the cost of punishment/reward is extremely low relative to their impact, and/or when the synergy factor is low (i.e., cooperation is otherwise hard to sustain). However, these conditions are considered unrealistic for most practical or experimental settings. The results suggest that adding reward to punishment does not generally increase group efficiency beyond what punishment alone achieves, and that the evolutionary advantage of combining positive and negative reciprocity is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial public goods games, enabling punishment (with or without reward) generally increases group efficiency compared to no punishment, especially when the cost of punishment is not prohibitively high. However, adding reward to punishment does not further increase efficiency except in very narrow, low-cost parameter regions. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (with reasonable cost and effectiveness) should be expected to increase efficiency, but the marginal benefit of also enabling reward is likely negligible except under harsh conditions (low MPCR, low cost). The results are most directly applicable to spatial (networked) PGGs with group size 5, continuous contributions, and symmetric cost/impact for punishment and reward.

- source: Chen, X. J., Sasaki, T., Brännström, & Dieckmann, U. (2015). First carrot, then stick: how the adaptive hybridization of incentives promotes cooperation. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with institutional incentives, an adaptive hybrid policy that rewards when cooperation is rare and punishes when cooperation is common ('first carrot, then stick') minimizes the cost of establishing and maintaining cooperation. This policy outperforms pure reward or pure punishment in both effectiveness (range of conditions for full cooperation) and efficiency (cumulative cost to reach/restore cooperation). The findings hold for both well-mixed and spatial populations, and are robust to changes in group size, incentive leverage, spatial structure, and other model details. The hybrid policy is especially efficient at converting a population of defectors to cooperators, requiring less total incentive expenditure than either reward or punishment alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, for a given public goods game structure, the efficiency gain from enabling punishment depends on how punishment is combined with reward. Pure punishment is effective at recovering cooperation but costly for establishing it, while pure reward is the opposite. The adaptive hybrid policy achieves both at lower cost. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is maximized when punishment is used adaptively after cooperation is established, and that the efficiency benefit is greater when the punishment technology is strong relative to reward. The results are robust across a range of game design dimensions, including player count, group size, and spatial structure. However, the results are theoretical and assume institutional (not peer-to-peer) sanctioning, with rational agents and no implementation errors.

- source: Yao, Y., & Chen, S. S. (2014). Multiplicative noise enhances spatial reciprocity. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game, both strong altruistic punishment and reward are unable to sustain cooperation when implemented in the traditional (non-tax-based) way, due to their high cost. When a tax-based mechanism is introduced—where all players pay a tax that is redistributed to punishers or rewarders—these strategies become viable, leading to stable coexistence of cooperators and punishers/rewarders and the elimination of defectors. This results in higher group efficiency and welfare. Tax-based strong altruistic reward leads to even higher cooperation and efficiency than tax-based strong altruistic punishment under equivalent tax conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the introduction of a tax-based institutional mechanism for funding punishment or reward can transform the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games from negative or neutral to strongly positive. The results suggest that, for a given control efficiency (without punishment), enabling tax-based punishment or reward will substantially increase efficiency, with reward being even more effective than punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with strong institutional support and compulsory tax collection, and less so to voluntary or peer-based punishment. The model is deterministic and assumes infinite, well-mixed populations, so predictions should be adjusted for finite or noisy environments.

- source: Sasaki, T. (2014). The Evolution of Cooperation Through Institutional Incentives and Optional Participation. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with optional participation, institutional punishment (penalties) is highly effective at promoting full cooperation and maximizing group welfare, even when the same level of punishment would only produce bi-stability (coordination problem) under compulsory participation. Optional participation allows the population to escape from the inefficient defection equilibrium, making cooperation globally stable for a wide range of punishment strengths. In contrast, institutional rewards combined with optional participation do not robustly promote cooperation or welfare: they often lead to non-participation and only marginally improve group welfare over the baseline. The model provides explicit analytical thresholds for when each regime occurs, and shows that the minimal punishment needed to achieve full cooperation decreases as group size increases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public goods games with optional participation and institutional punishment, enabling punishment will substantially increase efficiency (group welfare) compared to the control (no punishment) condition. The effect is robust across a wide range of group sizes, rounds, and incentive strengths, provided the punishment is above a threshold that decreases with group size. The model also predicts that institutional rewards are much less effective in this context. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially when optional participation and institutional punishment are present. However, the results are theoretical and assume infinite populations and well-mixed groups, so empirical generalizability may be limited.

- source: Wolitzky, A. (2013). Cooperation with Network Monitoring. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games with network monitoring, the maximum sustainable level of cooperation (and thus efficiency) is achieved by grim trigger (punishment) strategies. The effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation depends on the monitoring network's structure (who observes whom), group size, and the type of public good. For pure public goods, efficiency is typically increasing in group size; for divisible public goods, it is typically decreasing. More connected and less uncertain monitoring networks support higher efficiency. The paper provides explicit formulas for the maximum sustainable efficiency as a function of these design dimensions. Rewards are not necessary for sustaining maximum cooperation; punishment alone suffices.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of repeated public goods games with punishment, as a function of game design dimensions. It shows that, with punishment enabled (grim trigger strategies), the maximum sustainable efficiency is determined by the network structure, group size, monitoring technology, and the marginal per-capita return (mpcr). The results are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and design dimensions: enabling punishment (grim trigger) increases efficiency up to the robust maximum, which is explicitly characterized. The paper also provides comparative statics for how efficiency changes with group size, network connectivity, and monitoring certainty, all of which are key design dimensions.

- source: Sasaki, T., Uchida, S., & Chen, X. J. (2015). Voluntary rewards mediate the evolution of pool punishment for maintaining public goods in large populations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary public goods games with large populations, pool punishment cannot emerge from rare initial conditions due to the second-order free rider problem. However, if a voluntary reward fund is available and especially if it rewards those who contribute to pool punishment, then pool punishment can emerge and become stable, leading to high group efficiency (i.e., the population converges to all-punisher state). The reward mechanism acts as a mediator, facilitating the transition from free riding to stable punishment-supported cooperation. Once established, pool punishment is self-sustaining and the reward fund is no longer needed. The findings are robust to variations in group size, benefit multipliers, and cost parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence of a reward mechanism, especially one that rewards contributors to punishment, is a key moderator for the emergence and stability of efficient outcomes in public goods games with punishment. In the absence of such a reward, punishment alone cannot overcome the second-order free rider problem in large populations, and efficiency remains low. When predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, the presence or absence of a reward mechanism (and its design) is critical. Other dimensions such as group size, MPCR, and cost parameters are explored and found not to qualitatively change the main result: without reward, punishment does not increase efficiency from a free-rider baseline; with reward, punishment can emerge and efficiency increases dramatically.

- source: Hetzer, M., & Sornette, D. (2013). An Evolutionary Model of Cooperation, Fairness and Altruistic Punishment in Public Good Games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with punishment, evolutionary dynamics lead to two regimes: (1) a defection regime with low cooperation and low group payoff when punishment is weak or absent, and (2) a coordination regime with higher cooperation and higher group payoff when punishment is sufficiently strong and driven by disadvantageous inequity aversion. The model predicts the empirically observed propensity to punish in lab experiments. The presence of punishment transforms the social dilemma into a coordination problem, enabling higher efficiency. Disadvantageous inequity aversion is shown to be evolutionarily dominant, and populations with this trait invade and outcompete purely self-regarding populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in public goods games increases efficiency (group payoff) when punishment is sufficiently strong and is motivated by disadvantageous inequity aversion. The model gives explicit formulas for the threshold propensity to punish and shows that, above this threshold, populations coordinate on higher contributions and higher efficiency. The findings suggest that, for games with similar design parameters (e.g., n=4, r=3, MPCR=0.4), enabling punishment will shift the group from low to high efficiency, provided the punishment regime is effective. The paper also predicts how efficiency and punishment propensity depend on group size and punishment effectiveness, which can inform predictions for other parameterizations. However, the paper does not provide direct empirical estimates of efficiency change, so predictions should be interpreted as strong theoretical expectations rather than empirical effect sizes.

- source: Roberts, G. (2013). When Punishment Pays. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games, punishment can be self-interested and increase group efficiency when the cost to punishers is outweighed by the benefits of increased cooperation. The key condition is that the cost to the punisher (p) is less than the product of the punishment impact (q), the benefit per cooperative act (b), divided by the net cost of cooperation (c): p < q*b/c. Simulations confirm that with typical lab parameters (e.g., group size 3, k=2, p=1, q=3), punishment leads to high stable cooperation and high group payoffs. The effect is robust to group size and number of rounds, provided the parameter condition holds. The paper also explains why punishment sometimes fails to increase payoffs (e.g., when p is too high or q is too low), and why punishment is more effective in repeated than one-shot games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based support for predicting that, in repeated public goods games with punishment enabled and with favorable punishment parameters (low p, high q), efficiency will be substantially higher than in control games without punishment. The key moderators are the punishment cost (p), punishment effectiveness (q), group size (g), and the number of rounds (m). The paper gives explicit formulas for when punishment will increase efficiency, and shows that the effect is robust across a range of typical lab parameters. If the control game has low efficiency and the punishment parameters satisfy p < q*b/c, enabling punishment should lead to a large increase in efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2017). Second-Order Free-Riding on Antisocial Punishment Restores the Effectiveness of Prosocial Punishment. *Physical Review X*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games with both prosocial and antisocial punishment, the negative effects of antisocial punishment on cooperation and efficiency are largely neutralized by second-order free-riding among defectors. When the synergy factor (multiplication factor) is high, cooperation is sustained by network reciprocity alone, and antisocial punishment is negligible. When the synergy factor is low, prosocial punishment can still restore cooperation and efficiency, because nonpunishing defectors (second-order free-riders) undermine antisocial punishers, allowing punishing cooperators to dominate. The result is that, across a wide range of parameters, the introduction of punishment increases group efficiency, even in the presence of antisocial punishment. The paper also identifies parameter regions with stable coexistence of strategies, but the main result is that prosocial punishment is robustly effective in structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in spatially structured public goods games, enabling punishment (even when antisocial punishment is possible) will increase group efficiency compared to the control (no punishment) condition, except in cases where punishment is extremely costly or the synergy factor is very low. The results suggest that the presence of antisocial punishment does not undermine the efficiency gains from prosocial punishment in structured populations, due to the protective effect of second-order free-riding. The findings are most directly applicable to games with local interactions, fixed group size (G=5), and continuous contribution choices. The mapping from strategy composition to efficiency is direct, so the phase diagrams and stationary states can be used to predict efficiency outcomes under different parameter settings (e.g., player count, mpcr, punishment cost, punishment effectiveness). The paper does not address chat, communication, or information dimensions, so predictions for those features are not informed.

- source: Oya, G., & Ohtsuki, H. (2017). Stable polymorphism of cooperators and punishers in a public goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in well-mixed populations, punishment does not maintain cooperation or improve efficiency; free riders dominate unless initial conditions favor punishers, and even then, cooperation is lost. In spatially structured populations (Wright's island model and stepping stone model), if punishment is sufficiently efficient and the cost of cooperation is less than the cost of punishment, a stable coexistence of cooperators, punishers, and free riders can emerge, either as a limit cycle or a stable equilibrium (especially with mutation). In these cases, the presence of punishment can support higher average payoffs and group welfare than in the absence of punishment. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent: it can be positive in structured populations with the right parameters, but is neutral or negative in well-mixed populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on population structure, the relative costs of cooperation and punishment, and the efficiency of punishment. In well-mixed (random matching) environments, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency or maintain cooperation. In spatially structured environments, punishment can support higher efficiency and stable coexistence of strategies, but only if punishment is efficient and costly enough relative to cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive only in structured populations with high punishment efficiency and when cooperation is less costly than punishment. The model also highlights the importance of mutation and the inability to both cooperate and punish in the same round. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in theoretical or agent-based models, and provide mechanistic insight for interpreting empirical results.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Nowak, M. A., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Punishment does not promote cooperation under exploration dynamics when anti-social punishment is possible. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in both compulsory and voluntary public goods games, when all forms of punishment are allowed (including anti-social punishment), the introduction of punishment does not increase the average payoff or efficiency of the group under high mutation rates. Analytical results and agent-based simulations show that the average payoff of cooperators is not increased by punishment when anti-social punishment is possible; in fact, the outcome is equivalent to the no-punishment baseline. Only when punishment is restricted to pro-social forms (punishing defectors only) does punishment increase efficiency. These findings are robust to changes in mutation rate and selection intensity.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that in PGG-like environments with high exploration/mutation and the possibility of anti-social punishment, enabling punishment will not increase group efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect of punishment on efficiency is neutral or negative when anti-social punishment is possible, regardless of other game parameters such as player count, rounds, or selection intensity. If the game design allows anti-social punishment, do not expect efficiency gains from enabling punishment.

- source: Vasconcelos, V. V., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2015). Cooperation dynamics of polycentric climate governance. *Mathematical Models & Methods in Applied Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in threshold public goods games relevant to climate governance, the presence of local (polycentric) sanctioning institutions leads to higher efficiency and more robust cooperation than global (centralized) institutions. Efficiency is measured by the average group payoff relative to the maximum possible. The model shows that local punishment institutions are more effective at sustaining cooperation, especially under high risk, and can prevent the collapse into full defection. Larger group sizes, higher threshold uncertainty, and high homophily among wealth classes reduce efficiency. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and simulation of evolutionary dynamics, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling local (polycentric) punishment institutions in public goods games will increase efficiency relative to global punishment or no punishment. The effect is especially pronounced in smaller groups and under high risk perception. The model directly analyzes efficiency and group payoff, making its results highly relevant for predicting the impact of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions. However, the results are theoretical and may need empirical validation for specific parameterizations.

- source: Barrett, S. (2016). Coordination vs. voluntarism and enforcement in sustaining international environmental cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods and prisoners' dilemma games, voluntary cooperation and self-enforcing treaties generally fail to achieve efficient outcomes, especially as the number of players increases. Costly punishment can increase contributions but does not necessarily improve group efficiency or payoff, and may even reduce it if punishment is inefficient or leads to counter-punishment. Coordination mechanisms, such as trade restrictions or weakest-link structures, are more effective at achieving efficient outcomes. The paper also notes that the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) affects cooperation rates in experiments, even though theory predicts it should not. Theoretical models are supported by references to experimental findings from other studies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the cost and effectiveness of punishment. If punishment is costly and not highly effective, it may increase contributions but reduce or fail to improve efficiency due to the cost imposed on both punishers and punished. Coordination mechanisms or institutional changes that alter incentives (e.g., trade bans, weakest-link structures) are more likely to improve efficiency. For prediction, the presence of punishment should not be assumed to increase efficiency unless punishment is highly effective and not subject to costly retaliation. The marginal per-capita return (mpcr) and group size are also important moderators.

- source: Hwang, S. H. (2017). Conflict technology in cooperation: The group size paradox revisited. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that coordinated punishment in public goods games can lead to a concentration effect, making it easier to sustain cooperation and achieve high group efficiency as group size increases. When punishers are even slightly better coordinated than defectors, the set of states in which it is advantageous to be a punisher expands with group size, and the all-punisher (fully cooperative) equilibrium becomes stochastically stable in the long run. This result is robust to several variations, including the presence of unconditional punishment costs and some decay in coordination as group size increases. The model provides a theoretical explanation for empirical findings that larger groups can sometimes achieve higher cooperation and efficiency, contrary to standard free-rider logic.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public goods games with coordinated punishment, increasing group size can increase the efficiency of the punishment-enabled game, even when the control (no-punishment) game would predict lower efficiency in larger groups. The key moderator is the degree of coordination among punishers relative to defectors: as long as punishers are at least slightly better coordinated, the efficiency benefit of punishment increases with group size. The model also shows that this result is robust to moderate punishment costs and some decay in coordination. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not necessarily diminished in larger groups, and may in fact be enhanced, provided coordinated punishment is feasible.

- source: Ye, H., Chen, S., Luo, J., Tan, F., Jia, Y. M., & Chen, Y. F. (2016). Increasing returns to scale: The solution to the second-order social dilemma. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a PGG with increasing returns to scale, the presence of punishment leads to a dramatic increase in group efficiency and average payoff, provided the IRS parameter (alpha) is above a threshold (about 1.2–1.3). In standard PGGs (alpha=1), punishment does not stably increase efficiency, as defectors can invade and dominate. With strong IRS (alpha > 1.3), punishers can resist invasion by both defectors and second-order free-riders, leading to stable high cooperation and high group payoff. The effect of punishment is thus highly contingent on the returns-to-scale structure of the public good. The results are supported by both analytical evolutionary models and agent-based simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment in a PGG depends critically on the degree of increasing returns to scale in the production function. In standard linear PGGs, punishment may not improve efficiency, but in games with strong IRS, punishment can lead to near-maximal efficiency by stabilizing cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence and strength of IRS (or analogous nonlinear production functions) should be considered a key moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency. Other game design dimensions (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR) are also discussed, but the IRS effect is dominant.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Chu, T. G., & Weissing, F. J. (2013). Does insurance against punishment undermine cooperation in the evolution of public goods games?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper models a public goods game with punishment and an additional option for defectors to buy insurance that covers their punishment costs. It shows that if insurance is cheaper than the expected punishment, cooperation can be destabilized, leading to lower group efficiency and welfare. However, if insurance is realistically priced (at or above the expected fine), cooperation remains stable and efficient. The results are presented as a set of scenarios, with the stability of cooperation, defection, or speculation depending on the relative costs of cooperation, punishment, and insurance. The main implication is that punishment remains an effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency unless insurance is unrealistically cheap.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the efficiency gains from punishment in public goods games are robust to the introduction of insurance against punishment, as long as the insurance is not priced below the expected punishment cost. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in standard public goods games with punishment, efficiency should remain high unless there are additional mechanisms (like cheap insurance) that allow defectors to avoid punishment at a lower cost. The model also clarifies that the effectiveness of punishment (and thus its impact on efficiency) depends on the relationship between the cost of cooperation, the expected punishment, and any available insurance. If the control game (without punishment) is inefficient, introducing punishment should increase efficiency unless insurance is unrealistically cheap.

- source: Eldakar, O. T., Gallup, A. C., & Driscoll, W. W. (2013). WHEN HAWKS GIVE RISE TO DOVES: THE EVOLUTION AND TRANSITION OF ENFORCEMENT STRATEGIES. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can increase group efficiency (fitness) in public goods game environments, but the pathway depends on the type of punisher. Selfish punishers can invade selfish populations and reduce cheating, thereby increasing group payoff and paving the way for altruistic punishers, who further increase group efficiency. The effectiveness of punishment in raising group payoff is higher when punishment costs are low, group size is small, and the number of rounds (iterations) is high. The model directly analyzes how these design dimensions affect the ability of punishment to increase group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment in a public goods game can increase group efficiency, especially when punishment costs are low, group size is small, and the number of rounds is high. The model is directly based on a standard PGG and manipulates key design dimensions, making its findings highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that the efficiency gain from punishment is larger when punishment is less costly and when group structure allows for stronger group selection (smaller groups, longer association). The findings are most applicable to settings where punishment leads to exclusion or strong consequences for defectors.

- source: Sui, X. K., Wu, B., & Wang, L. (2017). Multiple tolerances dilute the second order cooperative dilemma. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper provides a unified theoretical framework for public goods games with multiple tolerant punishment strategies. It shows that: (1) Less tolerant punishers (those who punish with fewer defectors present) are always more abundant and thus more evolutionarily successful than more tolerant ones. (2) The presence of punishment strategies increases group efficiency (measured by the abundance of cooperation and punishers) when the fine for defectors exceeds a threshold determined by group size, enhancement factor, and punishment cost. (3) Larger group sizes can facilitate the evolution of punishment and thus higher group efficiency, but only when the enhancement factor and punishment cost are small. (4) The paper provides explicit analytical conditions for when punishment prevails, and these are validated by simulations. (5) The main outcome is the stationary abundance of strategies, which is directly linked to average payoffs and group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. It provides explicit analytical conditions for when punishment increases the abundance of cooperation and group efficiency, as a function of group size, enhancement factor (mpcr), punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness. The results show that punishment can increase efficiency, especially in larger groups when costs are low, and that less tolerant (more responsive) punishment is more effective. The findings can be used to predict the efficiency of games with and without punishment, given the game design parameters. However, the results are derived under evolutionary dynamics with weak selection and large populations, so care should be taken when applying them to small groups or different update rules.

- source: Boyd, R., Schonmann, R. H., & Vicente, R. (2014). Hunter-Gatherer population structure and the evolution of contingent cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods and threshold punishment games with realistic hunter-gatherer population structure, the efficiency gains from punishment or contingent cooperation depend critically on group size (player count), relatedness (assortment), and the threshold for cooperation or punishment. When groups are small (e.g., n=10) and relatedness is moderate to high (r_e > 0.05), contingent cooperation and punishment can increase expected fitness and invade, especially if the threshold for cooperation/punishment is low (i.e., only a minority need to cooperate/punish). In larger groups (e.g., n=40) or with low relatedness (r_e ~ 0.01), efficiency gains are much harder to achieve, requiring implausibly high repetition or relatedness. The model shows that punishment can support widespread cooperation and higher efficiency if a minority of punishers is sufficient to induce cooperation, but contingent cooperation alone is unlikely to sustain high efficiency in large, low-relatedness groups. Equilibrium outcomes are typically mixed, with both cooperators and non-cooperators present, and the equilibrium frequency of cooperators (and thus efficiency) depends on the tolerance threshold.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments as a function of player count, relatedness (assortment), and the threshold for cooperation or punishment. It suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, but only under specific conditions: small group size, high relatedness, and low thresholds for action. In large groups or with low relatedness, punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency unless the punishment mechanism is highly effective and requires only a minority to act. The model also highlights that equilibrium efficiency may be limited by the coexistence of cooperators and non-cooperators, especially for tolerant strategies. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on these structural parameters, and that simply enabling punishment is not sufficient for efficiency gains in all settings.

- source: Sasaki, T., & Uchida, S. (2013). The evolution of cooperation by social exclusion. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that social exclusion is a more effective and robust mechanism than costly punishment for sustaining cooperation and high efficiency in public goods games. When excluders can effectively and cheaply exclude defectors, the group can reach and maintain the efficient outcome where all contribute and defectors are eliminated. Costly punishment, even when effective, is vulnerable to invasion by non-punishing cooperators (second-order freeriders), leading to a decline in punishers and eventual dominance by defectors, which collapses efficiency. The model demonstrates that social exclusion can solve both the emergence and maintenance problems of cooperation, provided the cost of exclusion is not too high. The results are robust to changes in group size and to stochastic effects. The paper also notes that if monitoring costs are high, or if exclusion is not self-serving, the advantage of social exclusion disappears.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public goods games, enabling a punishment mechanism that takes the form of social exclusion (where defectors are excluded from benefit sharing and the cost of exclusion is not too high) will lead to a large increase in group efficiency compared to the control (no punishment) condition. The effect is robust across group sizes and does not require repeated interaction or reputation. In contrast, enabling costly punishment (where punishers pay to reduce defectors' payoffs) is less likely to increase efficiency, especially in the long run, due to vulnerability to second-order freeriders. For prediction tasks, the presence of a social exclusion mechanism with low cost and high effectiveness should be treated as a strong positive moderator of the efficiency effect of punishment. The specific model parameters (group size, multiplication factor, cost of exclusion/punishment, effectiveness) are all explicitly analyzed, allowing for quantitative predictions if mapped to empirical settings.

- source: Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2017). Competitions between prosocial exclusions and punishments in finite populations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in finite-population public goods games, exclusion strategies (both peer and pool) consistently outperform punishment strategies (peer and pool) in terms of evolutionary advantage, which is measured by stationary frequencies and average payoffs. Peer exclusion dominates in the absence of second-order sanctioning, while pool exclusion dominates when second-order sanctioning is present. The results hold for both optional and compulsory PGGs and are robust to variations in group size and punishment fine (unless the fine is extremely high). The findings imply that, for a given control efficiency, enabling exclusion (especially peer exclusion) will yield higher group efficiency than enabling punishment, and that the presence of second-order sanctioning shifts the advantage to pool exclusion.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in standard PGGs, enabling exclusion (especially peer exclusion) will increase group efficiency more than enabling punishment, across a wide range of parameter settings. The effect is robust to group size, number of rounds (since the model is evolutionary and not round-based), and the relative costs of punishment and exclusion (as long as they are not extremely imbalanced). The presence of second-order sanctioning is a key moderator: without it, peer exclusion is optimal; with it, pool exclusion is optimal. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment (vs. exclusion), one should expect a positive but smaller efficiency gain than with exclusion, and that the details of sanctioning (peer vs pool, second-order) are critical.

- source: Adami, C., Schossau, J., & Hintze, A. (2016). Evolutionary game theory using agent-based methods. *Physics of Life Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based simulations of the public goods game, the introduction of punishment (where cooperators can punish defectors at a cost) shifts the critical synergy parameter (r) required for cooperation to be stable, thus expanding the parameter region where cooperation (and thus higher group efficiency) is observed. The effect is quantitatively predicted by mean-field theory and confirmed by simulations: higher probability or density of punishment lowers the threshold for cooperation. However, because punishment is costly, it creates a second-order dilemma, and the evolutionary dynamics can lead to meta-stable states and hysteresis. When punishment is allowed to evolve, its effect is strongest near the critical point, and in highly cooperative or highly defective regimes, punishment is not maintained by selection. The findings are robust across different group sizes and mutation rates, and the paper provides explicit equations and simulation results for the effect of punishment on group payoff and cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public goods games, enabling punishment increases group efficiency (payoff) in the dilemma region by shifting the critical point for cooperation to lower values of the synergy parameter (r). The effect size and direction depend on the punishment parameters (cost, effectiveness), group size, and whether punishment is fixed or can evolve. For the downstream prediction task, the paper supports the prediction that, controlling for game design dimensions and baseline efficiency, enabling punishment will generally increase efficiency, especially near the cooperation/defection threshold. The paper also highlights that the effect of punishment is context-dependent: it is most effective near the critical point, and less so when cooperation or defection is already dominant. The explicit equations and simulation results can inform quantitative predictions.

- source: Schoenmakers, S., Hilbe, C., Blasius, B., & Traulsen, A. (2014). Sanctions as honest signals - The evolution of pool punishment by public sanctioning institutions. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that public sanctioning institutions (pool punishment) can evolve and sustain high levels of cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games, provided their existence is publicly visible. The signaling effect of such institutions deters free-riding and enables opportunistic cooperation, leading to a stable equilibrium with a mix of pool punishers and opportunists. This equilibrium achieves higher average payoffs than regimes without visible punishment or without the option to abstain. Diminishing taxes (cost-sharing among tax payers) further increase efficiency, while second-order punishment eliminates tax evasion but does not increase average payoffs beyond the baseline with only first-order punishment. The efficiency of the punishment technology and the level of public information about the institution are critical for the emergence and stability of efficient cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and public visibility of a punishment institution in a PGG-like environment increases group efficiency (average payoff) compared to control conditions without punishment. The model allows direct mapping to game design dimensions such as player count, punishment cost, punishment technology, and information structure. It predicts that enabling punishment (with public visibility) will increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is sufficiently deterrent (fine > cost of cooperation + tax), and when the institution is visible to most or all players. Diminishing the per-person cost of punishment (e.g., via cost-sharing) further increases efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, given knowledge of the control efficiency and the game design parameters.

- source: Dercole, F., De Carli, M., Della Rossa, F., & Papadopoulos, A. V. (2013). Overpunishing is not necessary to fix cooperation in voluntary public goods games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both peer-punishment (overpunishing) and shared-punishment (fixed sanction per defector, cost shared among punishers) in voluntary PGGs can lead to the fixation of cooperation and high group efficiency, provided antisocial punishment is absent. The main difference is quantitative: overpunishing increases the fraction of initial conditions leading to full cooperation, but shared-punishment is sufficient to achieve efficient outcomes in most cases, especially when stochasticity (finite populations) is considered. The likelihood of efficient cooperation increases with group size, sanction severity, and the public goods multiplier. The paper provides explicit payoff formulas and analyzes the basins of attraction for efficient (cooperative) equilibria under a wide range of parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in voluntary PGGs increases group efficiency, even when punishment is mild (shared-punishment). The effect is robust across a wide range of group sizes, round numbers, and parameter values. The paper gives explicit formulas for payoffs and the conditions under which cooperation (and thus efficiency) is achieved. For prediction tasks, the presence of voluntary participation, absence of antisocial punishment, and the ability to punish defectors (even with fixed, shared-cost sanctions) are sufficient to expect a large increase in efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline. The effect is stronger with larger groups, higher punishment severity, and higher public goods multipliers. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in PGG-like games with similar design features.

- source: Archetti, M., & Scheuring, I. (2013). Trading public goods stabilizes interspecific mutualism. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with non-linear (sigmoid or step) benefit functions, stable mutualism and high group efficiency can be achieved without punishment, even in large groups and with high costs. Punishment is only necessary to maintain mutualism and efficiency in the special case of linear benefits (the N-person prisoner's dilemma). When punishment is introduced in the linear case, it drives the system to full cooperation and maximal efficiency. In non-linear cases, punishment is not required for high efficiency, as the non-linearity itself stabilizes cooperation at high levels. The paper provides analytical and numerical results for the equilibrium frequency of cooperators and the conditions for stable mutualism.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the shape of the benefit function. If the game is linear (standard PGG), enabling punishment will have a strong positive effect on efficiency, driving the group to full cooperation. If the game is non-linear (e.g., threshold, sigmoid, or step), high efficiency can be achieved without punishment, and enabling punishment adds little or no additional benefit. Therefore, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, it is crucial to know whether the benefit function is linear or non-linear. Other game design dimensions (group size, cost/benefit ratio) interact with this, but the linearity of the benefit is the key moderator identified by this paper.

- source: Kroupa, S. (2014). Love or Fear: Can Punishment Promote Cooperation?. *Evolutionary Anthropology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that punishment in public goods games increases cooperation but does not always increase efficiency; in fact, in many standard lab designs, the costs of punishment can outweigh the benefits, leading to lower group payoffs. However, when games are run for more rounds, or when communication, reputation, or endogenous group formation are allowed, the frequency and cost of punishment decline over time, and efficiency improves—sometimes surpassing the no-punishment baseline. The paper also notes that antisocial punishment and second-order free-riding can undermine efficiency, but these problems are less severe in real-world or more naturalistic experimental settings where communication and reputation are present. The review suggests that the negative effects of punishment on efficiency have been exaggerated by restrictive lab designs, and that in real-world settings, punishment is often less costly and more effective due to social mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper suggests that the impact depends strongly on game design dimensions: short, anonymous, communication-free games with fixed groups and no reputation or coordination tend to show punishment as inefficient (lower group payoff), while longer games, or those with communication, reputation, or endogenous group formation, show punishment as both effective and efficient. The presence of antisocial punishment and high punishment costs can reduce efficiency, but these are mitigated by communication and coordination. Thus, when predicting treatment efficiency, one should adjust expectations upward if the design includes communication, reputation, or longer time horizons, and downward if the design is short, anonymous, and lacks these features.

- source: Kurzban, R., Burton-Chellew, M. N., & West, S. A. (2015). The Evolution of Altruism in Humans. *Annual Review of Psychology, Vol 66*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: This review synthesizes theory and empirical evidence on the evolution of altruism, with a focus on public goods games and the role of punishment. It highlights that punishment mechanisms increase contributions to the public good, but typically reduce overall efficiency or group payoffs because the costs of punishing and being punished are not fully offset by increased cooperation. Exceptions exist, such as when punishment is cheap or the game is played for many rounds, but these are not the norm. The review also notes the existence of 'perverse' punishment (punishing high contributors) and the importance of contextual factors (e.g., possibility of counter-punishment, cultural variation).
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this review suggests that, in most standard lab PGGs, enabling punishment increases cooperation but reduces efficiency due to the direct costs of punishment. However, if punishment is relatively cheap or the game is played for many rounds, efficiency losses may be mitigated or reversed. The review provides strong theoretical and empirical support for the importance of punishment cost, number of rounds, and the structure of punishment opportunities as moderators of the efficiency effect. It also cautions that the presence of counter-punishment or 'perverse' punishment can further reduce efficiency.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2014). Optimal distribution of incentives for public cooperation in heterogeneous interaction environments. *Frontiers in Behavioral Neuroscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games on heterogeneous (scale-free) networks, the optimal way to distribute limited institutional punishment (and reward) depends on the network structure, the enhancement factor (r), and whether payoffs are absolute or degree-normalized. For low enhancement factors, distributing punishment/reward equally is optimal for maximizing cooperation. For high enhancement factors, targeting high-degree nodes (punishing or rewarding them more) is optimal, but the direction depends on whether payoffs are absolute or normalized. The main outcome is the stationary fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, especially in heterogeneous network settings. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) depends on the distribution of punishment, the enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR), and the network structure. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to inferring that higher cooperation rates (under optimal punishment schemes) may translate to higher efficiency, especially when the enhancement factor is high. The findings are most relevant for settings with network heterogeneity and institutional (not peer) punishment.

- source: Perc, M. (2016). Phase transitions in models of human cooperation. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment into spatial public goods games can enable the survival and dominance of cooperative strategies under harsher conditions (lower multiplication factors) than in the absence of punishment. The effectiveness of punishment depends on the fine and cost parameters, with phase transitions (sometimes discontinuous) between different strategy compositions. The addition of reward and the interplay of positive and negative reciprocity further enrich the dynamics, leading to complex patterns such as cyclic dominance. However, the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes; instead, it focuses on the prevalence of cooperation and the conditions under which cooperation can persist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that punishment can promote cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when the punishment fine is sufficiently large and the cost is not prohibitive. The models specify how game design parameters (e.g., group size, multiplication factor, punishment fine and cost) affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, because the main outcomes are strategy frequencies and phase transitions rather than explicit efficiency or group payoff, the paper offers only indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. It is most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of punishment-related parameters and for mapping the conditions under which cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) is likely to emerge, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data for direct prediction.

- source: Hetzer, M., & Sornette, D. (2013). The Co-Evolution of Fairness Preferences and Costly Punishment. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulation model, based on the structure of standard public goods games with punishment (n=4, mpcr=0.4, punishment cost: 1 MU spent removes 3 MUs from target), shows that when agents evolve under disadvantageous inequity aversion, they develop a stable, empirically-matched propensity to punish defectors (k≈0.25). This level of punishment is sufficient to sustain high cooperation and maximize group fitness/wealth. The model finds that disadvantageous inequity aversion is evolutionarily stable and dominates other preference types, leading to higher average wealth, lower volatility, and longer agent lifetimes. While the model does not report efficiency in the strict sense (group payoff as a fraction of maximum possible), it provides strong evidence that punishment under disadvantageous inequity aversion increases group welfare and evolutionary success compared to self-regarding or non-punishing populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in a standard public goods game with punishment (n=4, mpcr=0.4, punishment cost 1:3), enabling punishment leads to higher group welfare and evolutionary success, provided agents have disadvantageous inequity aversion. The model is calibrated to match empirical punishment levels and shows that these levels are sufficient to sustain high cooperation and maximize group fitness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar game designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency (group payoff/welfare) relative to the control. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative efficiency ratios, so the evidence is most useful for qualitative or directional prediction, especially in settings matching the model's design parameters.

- source: Abdallah, S., Sayed, R., Rahwan, I., LeVeck, B. L., Cebrian, M., Rutherford, A., & Fowler, J. H. (2014). Corruption drives the emergence of civil society. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with both peer and centralized punishment, the presence of corruption undermines the effectiveness of strong centralized punishment. When centralized punishment is severe and corruption is possible, corruptors exploit the system, leading to lower cooperation and more defectors. Weaker centralized punishment allows peer-punishers to maintain cooperation even in the presence of corruption. Introducing a hybrid strategy (paying both the pool and peer-punishing) can maintain high cooperation across all levels of centralized punishment. The model suggests that a mix of centralized and decentralized enforcement is necessary for robust cooperation, especially in the face of corruption.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency (or cooperation) in public goods games depends critically on the structure of punishment (peer, pool, hybrid), the possibility of corruption, and the severity of centralized punishment. In environments where only centralized punishment is available and corruption is possible, increasing punishment severity can reduce efficiency by enabling corruptors to exploit the system. Allowing peer or hybrid punishment can mitigate this effect and restore cooperation. For prediction, the presence of corruption, the type of punishment available, and the severity of punishment are key moderators. The findings suggest that simply enabling (strong) punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency; the institutional context and the possibility of corruption must be considered.

- source: Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2014). Probabilistic sharing solves the problem of costly punishment. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that probabilistic sharing of punishment duties in a public goods game can solve the problem of costly punishment and promote cooperation, even when punishment is expensive. In well-mixed populations, probabilistic sanctioning transforms the game into a coordination game with two stable equilibria (full cooperation and full defection). In structured populations, sharing punishment duties leads to high cooperation rates, but only if the severity and frequency of punishment are carefully balanced; both too little and too much punishment can reduce cooperation. The main reported outcome is the stationary fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. Theoretical payoff expressions are provided, and the analysis explains why intermediate punishment is optimal for cooperation, but efficiency is not directly measured or reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the way punishment is implemented (shared probabilistically or periodically) can have a major impact on cooperation rates in public goods games. While the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency, the derived payoff expressions and analysis of invasion dynamics suggest that intermediate levels of punishment severity and frequency can maximize group welfare by supporting high cooperation at lower cost. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that both the cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as the structure of the population and the sharing of punishment duties, are important moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, because efficiency is not directly reported, quantitative predictions about efficiency must be inferred indirectly from the cooperation rates and payoff formulas.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2013). Effectiveness of conditional punishment for the evolution of public cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that conditional punishment, where the severity of punishment is proportional to the number of punishers in a group, is more effective than unconditional punishment in promoting cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when punishment is costly. Conditional punishers can maintain cooperation at lower fines and costs, and are more efficient in terms of cooperation per unit cost. Unconditional punishers only outperform conditional punishers when punishment is very cheap. The results are robust across different values of the multiplication factor (mpcr), and the findings are explained through the mechanism of indirect territorial competition and pattern formation on the spatial grid.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial public goods games, the design of the punishment mechanism (conditional vs. unconditional) and the cost-to-fine ratio are critical moderators of the effect of punishment on cooperation and cost-effectiveness. When predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, the results suggest that conditional punishment is more likely to yield higher cooperation per unit cost, especially when punishment is not cheap. However, the paper does not directly report group efficiency as a fraction of the maximum possible payoff, so predictions about absolute efficiency should be made with caution. The findings are most applicable to spatially structured games with local interactions and no communication.

- source: Pacheco, J. M., Vasconcelos, V. V., & Santos, F. C. (2014). Climate change governance, cooperation and self-organization. *Physics of Life Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing local (group-level) sanctioning institutions (punishment) in threshold public goods games with risk of collective loss significantly increases the fraction of groups that successfully achieve the public good, especially under low risk perception and high contribution thresholds. Global (population-wide) sanctioning institutions provide only marginal improvement. Smaller group sizes and higher risk perception also promote higher group achievement. Threshold uncertainty (uncertainty about the required contribution level) can sharply reduce group achievement. The model suggests that decentralized, polycentric punishment is more effective than centralized punishment for promoting cooperation and group success.
