  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment (specifically, local/group-level sanctioning institutions) in threshold public goods games with risk of collective loss will increase group achievement (a close proxy for efficiency), especially when group size is small, risk perception is high, and contribution thresholds are stringent. The effect of punishment is much stronger when implemented locally rather than globally. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of local punishment should be expected to yield a more positive effect on efficiency-related outcomes than global punishment or no punishment, particularly in small groups and under high risk. However, the main outcome is group achievement, not exact efficiency, so predictions should be interpreted accordingly.

- source: Noussair, C. N., & van Soest, D. P. (2014). Economic Experiments and Environmental Policy. *Annual Review of Resource Economics, Vol 6*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: chat, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews laboratory experimental evidence on public goods games and related social dilemmas, focusing on mechanisms to promote cooperation and efficiency. It finds that allowing peer punishment and/or rewards generally increases cooperation and can improve group payoffs, but the effect on efficiency is not universal and depends on implementation details (e.g., possibility of counter-punishment, cultural context). Communication between players robustly increases efficiency. Top-down interventions (such as fines or minimum contributions) can crowd out voluntary cooperation, sometimes reducing overall welfare. In market-based resource allocation, auction and trading mechanisms can achieve high efficiency, but are affected by strategic behavior, market power, and the presence or absence of endowment effects. The review highlights that the design of the game and institutions (e.g., whether players can undo others' contributions, whether the game is framed as giving or taking) strongly moderates the effect of punishment and other interventions on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides broad, high-level evidence that punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency in public goods games, but the effect is highly context-dependent. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when (a) group composition and identity are stable, (b) counter-punishment is not possible, and (c) the cultural context supports prosocial punishment. However, the review also warns that punishment can fail to improve efficiency or even reduce it if retaliation is possible or if the strategic environment allows for undoing others' contributions. Communication is a more robust positive moderator of efficiency. The review does not provide quantitative effect sizes but offers qualitative guidance on when punishment is likely to be effective. For prediction, the presence of punishment should not be assumed to always increase efficiency; moderators such as communication, game framing, and the possibility of counter-punishment must be considered.

- source: Parks, C. D., Joireman, J., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Cooperation, Trust, and Antagonism: How Public Goods Are Promoted. *Psychological Science in the Public Interest*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward mechanisms are effective at promoting cooperation in public goods games and similar social dilemmas, with meta-analytic evidence indicating that their effects on cooperation are roughly equal and of medium size. The presence of punishment opportunities leads to higher contributions to public goods over time, and thus to higher group efficiency and collective welfare. The effectiveness of punishment is moderated by factors such as trust (more effective in high-trust societies), group size (less effective in large groups), and the iterated nature of the game (more effective in repeated interactions). The review also notes potential downsides of punishment, such as undermining intrinsic motivation or evoking retaliation, but concludes that, overall, structural solutions like punishment are robust tools for increasing cooperation and efficiency in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper should inform prediction by reinforcing that, across a wide range of public goods game designs, enabling punishment (or reward) mechanisms generally increases group efficiency and collective welfare compared to control conditions without such mechanisms. The effect is robust across many studies and is supported by meta-analytic evidence. However, the magnitude of the effect may depend on moderators such as group size, trust, and the iterated nature of the game. The review provides indirect evidence for the predictive value of design dimensions such as punishment cost, identifiability, communication, and group size. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency, especially in high-trust, small-group, iterated settings.

- source: Milinski, M. (2016). Reputation, a universal currency for human social interactions. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews evidence that punishment in public goods games can maintain high levels of cooperation, but often at a net cost due to the expenses of punishing and being punished. Reputation mechanisms, such as indirect reciprocity, can also sustain cooperation and do so more efficiently by avoiding these costs. When both punishment and reputation mechanisms are available, punishment is used less frequently and more selectively, and overall group efficiency is increased. The review also notes that reward mechanisms are generally less effective than punishment in sustaining cooperation. The findings are based on a synthesis of multiple experimental studies, with particular emphasis on the interaction between punishment and reputation in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games generally increases cooperation and can improve group efficiency, especially when combined with reputation mechanisms. However, punishment alone may not always increase efficiency due to its costs. The review provides indirect evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the presence of reputation systems and the ability to observe or communicate about others' actions. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the efficiency gain from punishment is context-dependent and is maximized when reputation mechanisms are also present. The review does not provide new quantitative estimates but synthesizes qualitative and comparative findings from the literature.

- source: dos Santos, M., & Wedekind, C. (2015). Reputation based on punishment rather than generosity allows for evolution of cooperation in sizable groups. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper shows that in simulated public goods games, a reputation system based on punitive actions (punishment scoring) allows for the evolution and maintenance of high cooperation frequencies in large groups, even when errors in reputation assessment are present. In contrast, reputation based on generosity (contributions) or rewards is much less effective: generosity-based reputation rarely leads to cooperation in large groups, and reward-based systems are unstable due to invasion by non-rewarders. The model assumes binary contribution and punishment choices, with payoffs used for evolutionary selection, but does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that punishment-based reputation mechanisms are more effective than generosity- or reward-based reputation for sustaining cooperation in public goods games, especially as group size increases and errors in reputation assessment become more likely. However, because the main outcome is cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be interpreted as supporting the likely positive effect of punishment on efficiency in similar game designs, especially in large groups and error-prone environments. The model assumes all-or-nothing contribution and punishment choices, no communication, and full observability of actions (except for errors in reputation memory). The results are most informative for predicting the effect of punishment in large, anonymous, repeated PGGs with reputation tracking, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency.

- source: Toriumi, F., Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2016). Exploring an Effective Incentive System on a Groupware. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based simulations of public-goods-like meta-sanction games, systems with first-order punishment and appropriate second-order sanctions (such as punishing non-punishers or rewarding punishers) can achieve and robustly sustain high cooperation rates. In contrast, systems that allow first-order rewards without second-order incentives for rewarding behavior can actually impede the formation of cooperative regimes. The cooperation rate is highly sensitive to the cost/benefit ratio of sanctions and the discount factor for higher-order sanctions. The results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the structure of the punishment and reward system (including the presence or absence of second-order incentives) strongly affects the cooperation rate in public-goods-like environments. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that enabling punishment (with appropriate cost/benefit ratios and possibly second-order sanctions) can increase cooperation rates, but the translation to efficiency is not quantified. The results also warn that adding rewards without second-order incentives can reduce cooperation, which may imply lower efficiency, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Roos, P., Gelfand, M., Nau, D., & Lun, J. (2015). Societal threat and cultural variation in the strength of social norms: An evolutionary basis. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary simulations of the PGG with punishment, higher societal threat leads to higher rates of cooperation and a greater proportion of norm-enforcing punishers. Under low threat, cooperation and norm enforcement are weaker. The presence of punishment is necessary to achieve high cooperation under threat; spatial structure alone is insufficient. The model also shows that norm strength (adherence and enforcement) increases rapidly in response to increased threat and declines gradually when threat is removed. While payoffs are modeled, the main reported outcomes are behavioral, not efficiency or group payoff per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that punishment increases cooperation and norm enforcement in PGG-like environments, especially under higher threat. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment enabled/disabled, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The model parameters (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR) are specified, and the model is robust to parameter changes, but the main outcomes are behavioral. The paper supports the qualitative prediction that enabling punishment increases cooperation and norm enforcement, which is likely to increase efficiency, but does not provide direct efficiency data for prediction tasks.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Rong, Z. H. (2015). Mutual punishment promotes cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study uses agent-based simulations of a spatial public goods game on a square lattice, where each player participates in five overlapping groups. The key manipulation is the introduction of a symmetric, mutual punishment: whenever two neighbors have different strategies, both pay a punishment fine. The main result is that higher punishment fines lead to higher steady-state cooperation rates, and for sufficiently high fines, the population becomes fully cooperative. The mechanism is robust to changes in system size and noise. The paper provides a formula for the payoff difference between cooperators and defectors at the interface between clusters, showing that this difference increases with the punishment fine, favoring the spread of cooperation. However, the paper does not report group-level efficiency or total payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mutual punishment increases cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, especially as the punishment fine increases. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not efficiency. The model is theoretical and does not include communication, reward, or other institutional features. The punishment is symmetric and always applied when strategies differ, which is a specific variant of punishment not always present in experimental or real-world settings.

- source: Thöni, C. (2014). Inequality aversion and antisocial punishment. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that, while inequality aversion can theoretically motivate antisocial punishment under certain conditions, empirical data from several public goods game experiments indicate that most antisocial punishment acts do not fit this explanation. Instead, antisocial punishment is often carried out by the richest group members and is not typically accompanied by punishment of free riders, contrary to the predictions of the inequality aversion model. The findings suggest that other motives, such as revenge or a desire to harm others, are more likely explanations for antisocial punishment. The paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, but notes that antisocial punishment is detrimental to the efficiency of informal punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms behind antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment, but it does not provide direct quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. It does, however, reinforce the point that the presence and frequency of antisocial punishment can undermine the positive effects of punishment on cooperation and efficiency, especially in environments where antisocial punishment is prevalent. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that game design features or subject pool characteristics that foster antisocial punishment may reduce or even reverse the efficiency gains from enabling punishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, so it cannot be used to calibrate quantitative predictions of efficiency changes.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2015). Group Cooperation without Group Selection: Modest Punishment Can Recruit Much Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulations demonstrate that when punishment is possible and can recruit labor (i.e., induce free riders to cooperate in the future), even modest levels of punishment are sufficient to sustain high levels of cooperation in public goods games. The model relaxes several assumptions of prior models, allowing for probabilistic punishment, continuous cooperation probabilities, individual recognition, and group mixing. The results show that both punishment and cooperation can evolve by individual selection, even in large groups, and that second-order free riders do not prevent the evolution of cooperation. However, the outcomes reported are behavioral (cooperation and punishment rates), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in public goods games can substantially increase cooperation rates, especially when punishment is probabilistic and can recruit labor. The findings suggest that game designs allowing for individual recognition and repeated interactions (mixing ecologies) will see higher cooperation supported by lower punishment rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. Its value is in supporting the mechanism by which punishment increases cooperation, which is likely to translate into higher efficiency in similar empirical designs, but this is not directly quantified here.

- source: Li, Y. X., & Shen, B. (2013). The coevolution of partner switching and strategy updating in non-excludable public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in non-excludable public goods games, the ability to switch partners asymmetrically (i.e., only being able to sever outgoing ties) reduces the equilibrium level of cooperation compared to excludable (symmetric) PGGs. The addition of costly punishment, where cooperators pay a cost to reduce defectors' payoffs, substantially increases the equilibrium fraction of cooperators. The effect of punishment intensity (amplification factor) is also positive for cooperation. However, all outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that costly punishment increases cooperation rates in non-excludable PGGs with dynamic networks, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates typically imply higher efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this relationship or report actual payoffs. The model details (e.g., group size, network structure, punishment cost and effectiveness) are described, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes limits direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Shimao, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2013). Strict or Graduated Punishment? Effect of Punishment Strictness on the Evolution of Cooperation in Continuous Public Goods Games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the effectiveness of punishment strictness in promoting cooperation depends on the population structure. In spatially structured populations (lattice), strict punishment (high strictness parameter) leads to higher evolved cooperation levels than graduated punishment. In random-matching (well-mixed) populations, graduated punishment (low strictness parameter) is more effective at increasing cooperation. The results are robust across a range of parameters for public goods efficiency and punishment effectiveness. However, the paper does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the average cooperation level as the main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, efficiency) is moderated by the population structure: strict punishment is more effective in spatially structured settings, while graduated punishment is more effective in well-mixed settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design dimension corresponding to population structure (e.g., fixed groups vs. random rematching) is a key moderator of punishment's effect. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as informative about contribution/cooperation rates, which are closely related but not identical to efficiency.

- source: Kaiping, G. A., Jacobs, G. S., Cox, S. J., & Sluckin, T. J. (2014). Nonequivalence of updating rules in evolutionary games under high mutation rates. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with punishment, the choice of updating rule (BP, DP, LD) can dramatically change the long-run frequencies of defectors, cooperators, and punishers. For the same parameter values, the birth-death process leads to a population dominated by defectors, while the death-birth process results in a majority of cooperative strategies (cooperators and punishers). Link dynamics yields intermediate results. These differences arise even in well-mixed populations and are especially pronounced when mutation rates are not negligible and when more than two strategies interact. The paper does not report group efficiency or payoff, but the shift from defector to cooperator/punisher dominance implies a likely increase in group welfare under some updating rules.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the updating rule (a feature of evolutionary dynamics, not typically manipulated in lab experiments) can be a major moderator of the effect of punishment on cooperation and, by implication, on efficiency. In public goods games with punishment, the same game design (including punishment parameters) can yield very different long-run outcomes depending on the updating rule: punishment may be ineffective (defector-dominated) under one rule and highly effective (cooperator/punisher-dominated) under another. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as indicating that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the evolutionary dynamics assumed, especially in theoretical or agent-based models. For empirical prediction, this suggests caution in generalizing across studies with different evolutionary assumptions.

- source: Hintze, A., & Adami, C. (2015). Punishment in public goods games leads to meta-stable phase transitions and hysteresis. *Physical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing punishment in public goods games shifts the critical synergy parameter (r) required for cooperation to emerge to lower values, making cooperation possible under less favorable conditions. Punishment acts as a catalyst for cooperation, even if it is used sparingly, and can stabilize cooperation below the critical point. However, punishment can also stabilize defection above the critical point if defectors reach a critical density. The system exhibits meta-stable phase transitions and hysteresis: once cooperation is established, it can persist even as conditions worsen, and vice versa. The main reported outcomes are the probabilities of cooperation and punishment, and the critical values of the synergy parameter, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the presence and parameters of punishment (cost, effectiveness) in a public goods game can substantially lower the threshold (synergy r) at which cooperation emerges. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the probability of cooperation is a strong proxy for efficiency in this context, as full cooperation would maximize group payoff. The findings suggest that, for a given game design, enabling punishment (especially if it is effective and not too costly) will increase the likelihood of high cooperation and thus higher efficiency, particularly near the critical synergy threshold. However, the paper does not provide quantitative efficiency values, so its support is indirect and best used for qualitative prediction or mechanistic understanding.

- source: Kaiping, G. A., Cox, S. J., & Sluckin, T. J. (2016). Cooperation and punishment in community-structured populations with migration. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in community-structured populations playing a linear PGG with punishment, the presence of group competition (where entire communities can replace each other based on average fitness) or fitness-based migration (where individuals are more likely to migrate if they are fitter) can sustain high levels of cooperation and punishment, suppressing defection in parameter regions where defection would otherwise dominate. Constant (random) migration does not have this effect. The transition between cooperative and defective regimes is sharper with migration, but only group competition and fitness-based migration expand the parameter space where cooperation and punishment are stable. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of strategies, not efficiency or group payoff, but higher frequencies of cooperation and punishment imply higher group payoffs relative to defection-dominated regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task: in PGG-like environments with punishment, the presence of group competition or fitness-based migration mechanisms can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation and punishment, and thus are likely to increase efficiency or group payoff compared to control games without punishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so the evidence is based on the frequencies of cooperative and punishing strategies. The results suggest that, for the same game design dimensions, enabling punishment in a context with group competition or fitness-based migration will likely lead to higher efficiency than in well-mixed or constant migration settings. The findings are most relevant for models with large group sizes, high mutation and migration rates, and community structure.

- source: Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2015). Competition and cooperation among different punishing strategies in the spatial public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial public goods game with diverse punishing strategies, the highest level of public cooperation is attainable through a selection mechanism among punishers. The evolutionary success of a punishing strategy depends on its invasion velocity relative to defectors and other punishers. At low multiplication factors (difficult environments), cooperation among punishing strategies can allow otherwise weak punishers to survive. At higher multiplication factors, competition among punishers can lead to a single dominant strategy. The impact of punishment fine is nonmonotonic: too high fines can be detrimental due to excessive costs. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so its findings are most relevant for predicting the prevalence of cooperation and the dynamics of punishment strategies, not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure and diversity of punishment strategies, as well as the multiplication factor and punishment fine, are critical moderators of cooperation in spatial public goods games. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it informs the prediction task by highlighting that the relationship between punishment and cooperation is nonmonotonic and context-dependent, and that the presence of multiple punishing strategies can alter the evolutionary outcome. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment may depend on the costliness of punishment and the synergy factor, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Li, K., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2016). Cooperation induced by random sequential exclusion. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the random sequential exclusion (R) mechanism for social exclusion in public goods games can sustain higher levels of cooperation (measured as the stationary frequency of cooperators and excluders) than the standard peer exclusion (P) mechanism, especially under adverse conditions such as high exclusion cost, low exclusion success probability, large group size, and low synergy factor. The advantage of the R mechanism is lost if the scheduling cost for coordination among excluders becomes too high. The results hold in both well-mixed and spatially structured populations. However, the paper does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff, so the findings are about the prevalence of cooperative strategies rather than explicit welfare or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that more efficient or coordinated punishment mechanisms (like random sequential exclusion) can increase the prevalence of cooperative strategies in public goods games, especially when punishment costs are high or group size is large. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it suggests that under certain design dimensions (low scheduling cost, high group size, high exclusion cost), mechanisms that reduce redundant punishment may be more effective at sustaining cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the structure and cost of punishment mechanisms are important moderators, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Chen, X. J., Sasaki, T., & Perc, M. (2015). Evolution of public cooperation in a monitored society with implicated punishment and within-group enforcement. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of implicated punishment (where all group members are fined if a defector is detected) can transform the public goods game into a coordination game, making both full cooperation and full defection stable equilibria. Increasing the monitoring probability, the implicated punishment fine, or the within-group enforcement probability all enlarge the basin of attraction for full cooperation, thus promoting higher cooperation rates. Group size has a nonlinear effect: intermediate group sizes are least conducive to cooperation, while both small and large group sizes favor cooperation. The addition of within-group enforcement (peer punishment) further relaxes the conditions for cooperation to thrive. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but higher cooperation rates in standard PGGs are typically associated with higher efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that implicated punishment and within-group enforcement can substantially increase cooperation rates in public goods games, especially when monitoring probability and punishment fines are sufficiently high. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the close relationship between cooperation rate and efficiency in standard PGGs suggests that these mechanisms would also increase efficiency. The nonlinear effect of group size is important: intermediate group sizes may be less effective for promoting cooperation than either small or large groups. For prediction tasks, the presence of implicated punishment and within-group enforcement, as well as the values of monitoring probability, punishment fine, and group size, should be considered as key moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Quan, J., Liu, W., Chu, Y. Q., & Wang, X. J. (2017). Stochastic evolutionary voluntary public goods game with punishment in a Quasi-birth-and-death process. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment and non-participation strategies into a stochastic evolutionary public goods game changes the evolutionary dynamics and the likelihood of the system reaching cooperative, defecting, or non-participation equilibria. Higher punishment probability (alpha) and lower punishment cost (gamma) both increase the parameter regions where the system is likely to reach cooperative/punishing (C+P) equilibria. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; instead, it reports the probability of the system selecting each type of equilibrium as a function of the parameters. The results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under a wide range of conditions, but the effect is moderated by the cost and probability of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the likelihood of cooperative/punishing equilibria increases with higher punishment probability and lower punishment cost, and that these effects are sensitive to the marginal per-capita return (r/N) and the fixed income for non-participants. However, because the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, its findings must be mapped from the probability of cooperative equilibria to expected efficiency. The results suggest that, in parameter regions where the C+P equilibrium is likely, efficiency would be higher than in regions dominated by defection or non-participation. Thus, the paper supports the idea that punishment can increase efficiency, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Cong, R., Zhao, Q. C., Li, K., & Wang, L. (2017). Individual mobility promotes punishment in evolutionary public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the optimal mobility (migration tendency) of punishers depends on the cost of punishment. For high punishment costs, a medium tendency to migrate (rather than always punishing or always running) maximizes the prevalence of punishers. For low punishment costs, always punishing is optimal. The presence of cooperators (second-order free-riders) changes the dynamics: in harsh environments (low enhancement factor), punishers benefit from moderate migration, but in more favorable environments, cooperators can dominate, and the advantage of punishment diminishes. The model shows that mobility can help punishers survive, but only under certain cost and environmental conditions. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but higher prevalence of punishers and cooperators is interpreted as beneficial for cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for how punishment, its cost, and the mobility of punishers affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under certain conditions, especially when the cost is not too high and punishers have a moderate migration tendency. For prediction tasks, the findings imply that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive when punishment is not too costly and when the game environment allows for spatial structure and mobility. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Spitzer, M. L. (2016). Preferences over Punishment and Reward Mechanisms in Social Dilemmas. *Journal of Institutional and Theoretical Economics-zeitschrift Fur Die Gesamte Staatswissenschaft*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper does not present new empirical findings but critiques the interpretability of Kocher and Matzat's experimental results. It notes that subjects' preferences over punishment and reward mechanisms are difficult to interpret due to the multiplicity of possible utility functions and equilibria. The comment also observes that the effects of punishment on contribution are sensitive to the details of the punishment structure, and that the presence of a constitutional convention stage (where subjects choose the institutional mechanism) adds further complexity. The author suggests that simpler experimental designs would make it easier to interpret the effects of institutional choices on cooperation and contributions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-good games. However, it offers important cautionary notes: (1) the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency is highly sensitive to the details of the punishment mechanism (e.g., cost, impact, available choices); (2) subject beliefs and the complexity of institutional choice can obscure the relationship between mechanism and outcome; (3) the presence of multiple equilibria under different utility functions makes it difficult to infer efficiency effects from observed preferences or voting patterns. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that careful attention must be paid to the specific design dimensions of the game, as small changes can have large effects on outcomes.

- source: Sylwester, K., Hellmann, B., & Bryson, J. J. (2013). Homo Homini Lupus? Explaining Antisocial Punishment. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews and interprets evidence that antisocial punishment—punishing cooperators at a cost to oneself—is a widespread phenomenon in public goods games, especially in certain cultural and ecological contexts. It argues that antisocial punishment is often driven by status competition, aggression, or negative reciprocity, and is more prevalent where the cost of punishment is low, legal institutions are weak, and social competition is high. The review notes that antisocial punishment can undermine the cooperation-enhancing effects of punishment, and that its prevalence varies widely across cultures. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment; instead, it focuses on behavioral mechanisms and cross-cultural patterns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical and contextual background for understanding when and why punishment may fail to increase efficiency in public goods games, especially due to antisocial punishment. It highlights that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent and can be negative in environments with high antisocial punishment. However, because the paper is a review and does not report new or meta-analyzed efficiency or payoff data, it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. It is most useful for identifying moderators (e.g., punishment cost, anonymity, cultural context) that should be considered when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, and for warning that punishment can sometimes reduce, rather than increase, group welfare.

- source: Perc, M., Gómez-Gardeñes, J., Szolnoki, A., Floría, L. M., & Moreno, Y. (2013). Evolutionary dynamics of group interactions on structured populations: a review. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The review summarizes that structured populations (lattices, networks, bipartite graphs) and coevolutionary rules can strongly affect the evolution of cooperation in public goods games. Punishment and reward mechanisms, as well as strategic complexity (e.g., conditional cooperation, pool/peer punishment, rewarding), can promote cooperation and solve second-order free-rider problems in structured settings. The review highlights that the effectiveness and stability of punishment and reward depend on the interplay between game parameters (e.g., group size, benefit functions, network structure) and evolutionary dynamics. However, the main outcomes discussed are cooperation rates and the survival/extinction of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This review provides high-level theoretical and simulation-based context for how punishment and other mechanisms affect cooperation in public goods games on structured populations. It is useful for understanding which game design dimensions (e.g., group size, network structure, strategic complexity) are likely to moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report control vs. punishment-enabled efficiency rates. For the downstream prediction task, this review can inform which dimensions are likely to be important moderators, but cannot be used for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Strang, S., & Park, S. Q. (2017). Human Cooperation and Its Underlying Mechanisms. *Social Behavior from Rodents to Humans: Neural Foundations and Clinical Implications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper synthesizes evidence that the threat or presence of punishment in public goods games and similar economic games leads to higher and more stable cooperation rates. It highlights that when punishment is available, cooperation is maintained, and when it is removed, cooperation declines. The review also discusses the emotional and neural correlates of cooperation and punishment, such as the role of negative emotions in driving punishment and the involvement of brain regions like the insula, striatum, and DLPFC. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions moderate these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation in public goods games and related settings, and that this effect is robust across cultures and contexts. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates for the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze how specific game design dimensions (such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost) moderate these effects. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is best used as background for the general mechanism that punishment increases cooperation, but not for precise quantitative prediction or for understanding the impact of specific design features.

- source: Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Human cooperation. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical and experimental literature on the evolution and maintenance of cooperation in humans, with a focus on public goods games and related social dilemmas. It finds that: (1) Repetition and targeted reciprocity (including punishment and reward) can promote cooperation in public goods games; (2) Both punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but reward is generally more efficient (i.e., produces higher group payoffs) than punishment, which can destroy value and provoke retaliation or antisocial punishment; (3) The effectiveness of punishment and reward depends on their implementation and the broader social context; (4) Cooperation in one-shot anonymous games is explained as an overgeneralization of strategies learned in repeated or reputation-based settings. The paper does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical and qualitative evidence that adding punishment to public goods games can increase cooperation, but may not always increase efficiency or group payoff, especially if punishment is costly, provokes retaliation, or is used antisocially. Reward mechanisms are generally more efficient than punishment. The review does not provide quantitative data or direct predictions about efficiency changes, so it should be used for qualitative guidance: expect that punishment can promote cooperation, but its effect on efficiency is context-dependent and may be negative if punishment is misapplied. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the design of punishment (cost, effectiveness, possibility of antisocial use) and the presence of reward options are important moderators.

- source: Ogaki, M., & Tanaka, S. C. (2017). Social Preferences. *Behavioral Economics: Toward a New Economics by Integration with Traditional Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The chapter reviews evidence that, in public goods games, the introduction of costly punishment opportunities (as in Fehr and Gächter 2000) leads to higher contribution rates and prevents the typical decay of cooperation seen in repeated games without punishment. It explains these results using models of social preferences, especially inequality aversion. However, the chapter does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly. The main findings are that punishment increases cooperation (contribution rates) and that social preference models can explain both altruistic and selfish behaviors depending on the context (e.g., presence of competition).
  prediction_guidance: This chapter provides indirect support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation, which is often associated with higher efficiency, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, the chapter supports the qualitative expectation that punishment increases cooperation, but does not allow for precise prediction of efficiency changes. The evidence is most useful for understanding mechanisms and for qualitative directionality, not for quantitative prediction.

- source: Baumard, N., André, J. B., & Sperber, D. (2013). A mutualistic approach to morality: The evolution of fairness by partner choice. *Behavioral and Brain Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation and punishment behaviors in economic games, including public goods games, are guided by evolved fairness norms shaped by partner choice. It reviews evidence that people punish both low and high contributors in PGGs, often to restore fairness rather than to maximize group efficiency. The mutualistic approach predicts that individuals seek proportionality between contributions and rewards, and that punishment is used to rebalance unfair distributions. The paper notes that in real-world small-scale societies, exclusion and partner switching are more common than costly punishment. It also highlights that punishment in PGGs is sometimes antisocial (targeting high contributors) and is strongly shaped by local fairness norms. However, the paper does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes as a function of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and interpretive context for understanding why and how punishment may affect cooperation and fairness in PGG-like environments, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether punishment is perceived as fair and proportional, and that exclusion or partner choice may be more effective than costly punishment in sustaining cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper is useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., fairness norms, entitlement, partner choice), but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

- source: Vincent, A. (2017). Punishment and status in collective action: How status hierarchies foster optimal punishment use. *Sociology Compass*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that status hierarchies can endogenously coordinate punishment in public goods games, leading to more optimal (i.e., less costly and more effective) use of punishment. High status actors are more likely to be expected to punish when punishment is seen as group-oriented, and their punishment is more likely to promote subsequent cooperation and reduce the need for further punishment. The author suggests that these dynamics can help groups realize public goods more efficiently, but this is a theoretical claim rather than an empirically demonstrated effect. The paper also discusses why typical experimental designs may fail to observe these effects, due to their suppression of status emergence and communication.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical arguments and literature synthesis suggesting that the presence of status hierarchies and the assignment of punishment responsibility to high status actors could improve the efficiency of punishment in public goods games by reducing unnecessary or counterproductive punishment and fostering cooperation. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that game designs allowing status differentiation, communication, or leadership roles may see more efficient punishment use, but this is not quantified. The paper is less useful for direct prediction of efficiency from game design dimensions in the absence of empirical data.

- source: dos Santos, M. (2015). The evolution of anti-social rewarding and its countermeasures in public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in public goods games with pool reward funds, allowing defectors to form their own reward funds (anti-social rewarding) causes a breakdown of cooperation and group efficiency, even when mechanisms to exclude non-rewarders (second-order sanctions) are present. Theoretical analysis shows that anti-social rewarding leads to stable equilibria with low or no cooperation, unless additional countermeasures are implemented. These include making pro-social reward funds more productive than anti-social ones, or making reward funds contingent on the actual production of the public good. Only under these conditions, and with sufficiently strong exclusion of non-rewarders, can high cooperation and efficiency be restored.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence of anti-social rewarding mechanisms in public goods games with institutional rewards can dramatically reduce the efficiency gains from positive incentives, even when second-order punishment or exclusion is available. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling anti-social rewarding (or failing to prevent it) will likely result in much lower efficiency in the punishment/reward-enabled condition compared to control, unless the game design includes mechanisms that make pro-social rewards more effective or contingent on public good production. The findings are directly relevant for settings with institutional rewards, but less so for standard punishment-only designs. The results highlight the importance of considering the possibility of anti-social incentives and the structure of reward and punishment technologies when predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, K., Cong, R., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2015). Social exclusion in finite populations. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that social exclusion mechanisms (peer and pool) can allow excluders to dominate over defectors in finite-population public goods games, depending on exclusion cost, group size, and exclusion effectiveness. Peer exclusion is more effective at low costs, while pool exclusion is more robust at high costs and larger group sizes. Both mechanisms can sustain cooperation and prevent the dominance of defectors, which would otherwise reduce group payoff. The analysis is based on evolutionary game theory, using payoff functions and fixation probabilities, rather than direct simulation of group efficiency. The results suggest that enabling exclusion mechanisms can improve group outcomes compared to no-punishment baselines, especially under the right parameter conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support that enabling exclusion-based punishment in public goods games can increase the prevalence of cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency, especially when exclusion is effective and/or costs are not too high. The results are parameterized by group size, exclusion cost, and exclusion probability, and show that pool exclusion is more robust in large groups or at high costs. While the paper does not report efficiency as a direct outcome, the dominance of excluders and cooperators over defectors implies higher group payoff. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency will be more positive when exclusion is effective, costs are moderate, and group size is not too large for peer exclusion (but pool exclusion can handle larger groups). However, the lack of direct efficiency reporting and the theoretical nature of the results mean that quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Yokoyama, A. (2014). The Effect of Ostracism and Optional Participation on the Evolution of Cooperation in the Voluntary Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in voluntary public goods games, allowing group members to exclude (ostracize) candidates with low reputation (defectors) leads to the evolution and maintenance of high cooperation rates. Optional participation (where individuals can choose whether to join a group based on group reputation) does not sustain cooperation. The strictest exclusion rule (requiring unanimous acceptance for membership) is most effective at promoting cooperation. The model is evolutionary and simulation-based, and outcomes are reported as the proportion of cooperators, not as group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that costless ostracism (exclusion of defectors) is a highly effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation in public goods games, more so than optional participation alone. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the implication is that group efficiency would be higher when ostracism is enabled, since the share of cooperators is much higher. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is costless and takes the form of exclusion, rather than costly point-based punishment. The results are less directly applicable to predicting the quantitative effect of standard costly punishment on efficiency, but they support the qualitative prediction that effective exclusion or ostracism can greatly increase group efficiency if measured.

- source: Sell, J., & Reese, B. (2014). Social Dilemma Experiments in Sociology, Psychology, Political Science, and Economics. *Laboratory Experiments in the Social Sciences, 2nd Edition*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The chapter synthesizes findings from experimental studies on social dilemmas, emphasizing that cooperation is more common than predicted by strong free-riding hypotheses, and that structural features such as group size, information, communication, and institutional rules (including punishment and reward) shape cooperation. Punishment is discussed as a mechanism that can increase cooperation, but its effectiveness depends on design features such as who can punish, the cost, and the identification of punishers and recipients. The chapter does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but notes that higher cooperation is often associated with institutional features like communication and targeted punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This chapter provides useful theoretical and qualitative evidence about the mechanisms by which punishment, communication, and information affect cooperation in public goods games and related social dilemmas. It does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence about efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it report experimental results with efficiency as a dependent variable. For prediction tasks, the chapter supports the idea that punishment can increase cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency), but also warns that unconstrained punishment may reduce cooperation. The review is most useful for understanding which design dimensions are likely to moderate the effect of punishment, rather than for direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Greiff, M. (2013). Rewards and the private provision of public goods on dynamic networks. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The model demonstrates that in a repeated public goods game with rewards (where giving rewards is costly but beneficial to the receiver), the emergence of a social institution prescribing prosocial behavior (contribute and reward contributors) can lead to efficient public good provision, provided that rewards are only given to contributors and the network structure evolves quickly enough to allow exclusion of non-contributors. If rewards are also given to defectors, or if players update strategies too quickly, the system converges to low contribution and low efficiency. The model does not analyze punishment directly, but exclusion from rewards via network dynamics acts as an endogenous sanction. The findings are theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with reward mechanisms, especially in dynamic network settings. It provides strong theoretical evidence that reward-based institutions can achieve high efficiency if rewards are targeted only at contributors and if the network allows for exclusion of non-contributors. However, it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment, nor does it compare reward to punishment directly. For prediction tasks focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that endogenous exclusion (a form of sanction) can substitute for explicit punishment, but does not quantify the effect of punishment per se. The model's results are most applicable to games with dynamic partner selection and reward, not to standard fixed-group punishment treatments.

- source: Gavrilets, S. (2015). Collective action problem in heterogeneous groups. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that group effort and efficiency in public goods games and group contests depend strongly on group size, within-group heterogeneity, and the nonlinearity of cost and benefit functions. In linear models, group effort typically decreases with group size and increases with heterogeneity, while in nonlinear models, these patterns can reverse. The largest contributors are those with the highest stake or lowest cost, but under some conditions, these individuals may end up with lower net payoffs than free-riders. The review also summarizes experimental findings, noting that punishment can increase contributions but may not always increase total earnings or efficiency, especially in heterogeneous groups. Theoretical models predict that group selection, between-group competition, and synergicity can all increase group effort and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and synthetic evidence on how game design dimensions such as player count, group heterogeneity, cost/benefit structure, and group selection affect efficiency in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency, as punishment is only discussed in the context of prior literature and experiments. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper is mainly useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the moderating role of group structure, but not for estimating the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2015). Antisocial pool rewarding does not deter public cooperation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the introduction of both antisocial and prosocial pool rewarding does not deter the evolution of cooperation or reduce group efficiency. Instead, prosocial rewarding continues to promote cooperation, and the aggregation required for rewards to be effective leads to higher group payoff and efficiency. The critical threshold for cooperation to dominate is lowered as the effectiveness of rewarding increases, even when defectors can also reward each other. In well-mixed populations, antisocial rewarding can prevent cooperation unless cooperators are initially predominant, but in structured populations, local aggregation allows cooperation to thrive. The results are robust to increased randomness in the interaction network.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public goods games with pool rewarding, the presence of antisocial rewarding does not reduce the efficiency gains from prosocial rewarding in structured populations. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling reward mechanisms (even if both cooperators and defectors can use them) will generally increase efficiency compared to control, provided the population structure allows for aggregation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings with spatial or network structure, group size of 5, all-or-nothing contributions, and no punishment. The results are less applicable to well-mixed populations or to games with punishment rather than reward.

- source: Kurokawa, S., & Ihara, Y. (2017). Evolution of group-wise cooperation: Is direct reciprocity insufficient?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper shows that in n-player repeated prisoners' dilemma games with non-rivalrous public goods, direct reciprocity alone can support the evolution of cooperation (and thus high group efficiency), especially as group size increases. This holds even when individuals sometimes make mistakes or have imperfect information, provided they use sufficiently optimistic conditional strategies. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcome is the condition under which cooperative strategies are selectively favored over defection, which is directly tied to group payoff and efficiency. The paper does not provide empirical or simulated efficiency values, but gives analytic thresholds for when cooperation is favored.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with non-rivalrous benefits, increasing group size can make cooperation (and thus high efficiency) more likely, even in the presence of mistakes or imperfect information, as long as players use optimistic conditional strategies. However, the paper does not model punishment, so it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions as a function of group size, information, and error, but not for the effect of punishment. The paper is useful for understanding baseline efficiency in repeated public goods games with direct reciprocity, but not for predicting the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Smaldino, P. E., & Lubell, M. (2014). Institutions and Cooperation in an Ecology of Games. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model demonstrates that reputational exclusion, when information about behavior is reliable and flows quickly, allows cooperators to achieve much higher payoffs relative to defectors than capacity constraints. However, if reputation is slow to converge or cooperators are rare, capacity constraints are more effective. The ability of cooperators to join many games under reputation is key to their advantage. The model also finds that increasing the productivity of the public goods game (higher multiplier) increases the relative payoff to cooperators under reputation, but not necessarily under capacity constraints. Limiting the number of games an agent can play reduces the effectiveness of reputation-based exclusion. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning (costly punishment), so direct evidence on punishment is not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it models only exclusion-based institutions (capacity constraints and reputation-based entry) rather than costly punishment. However, it is highly informative about the effects of group entry rules and reputation mechanisms on efficiency and cooperation. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only adjacent, but it provides strong evidence that exclusion and reputation can be powerful alternatives to punishment for promoting efficiency, especially when information is reliable and cooperators are common. The findings suggest that the design of group entry and information flow can be as important as punishment in determining efficiency outcomes.

- source: Stewart, A. J., Parsons, T. L., & Plotkin, J. B. (2016). Evolutionary consequences of behavioral diversity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in iterated public goods games, increasing the number of available contribution levels (behavioral diversity) can either increase or decrease group efficiency, depending on the ratio of public benefit to individual cost (mpcr). With low mpcr, more choices allow for more robust cooperative strategies and higher mean payoffs. With high mpcr, the abundance of suboptimal robust strategies causes populations to get stuck at lower efficiency. The ability to punish (by reducing one's own contribution in response to defection) is necessary for two-choice strategies to be robust, but explicit costly punishment is not modeled. The paper provides analytic conditions for when simple strategies can resist invasion by more complex ones, and uses evolutionary simulations to show the impact of choice set size on mean payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of the number of contribution options (all_or_nothing, player_count, mpcr, etc.), but it does not model explicit costly punishment as a separate mechanism. Instead, 'punishment' is operationalized as the ability to reduce one's own contribution in response to others' defection. The findings suggest that increasing the number of contribution options (behavioral diversity) can either help or hurt efficiency, depending on mpcr. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that more behavioral options do not always increase efficiency, and that the effect depends on the underlying game parameters. However, because explicit costly punishment is not present, the results are only adjacent to the core prediction task regarding punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled games.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2016). Competition of tolerant strategies in the spatial public goods game. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing diverse tolerant strategies into the spatial public goods game can significantly increase group efficiency, sometimes leading to defector-free, fully cooperative states. The effect depends on the synergy factor (r), the cost of monitoring (gamma), and the system size. In structured populations, coexistence of multiple tolerant strategies can create synergistic effects that outperform single-strategy or uniform-tolerance scenarios. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams showing which strategy combinations are stable under different parameter regimes. The main efficiency gains occur when tolerant strategies with different thresholds form alliances, crowding out defectors and maximizing group payoff. The results highlight the importance of diversity in tolerance and the structure of interactions for achieving high efficiency in public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial public goods games, the introduction of conditional strategies (tolerant players) that abstain from cooperation when defection is high can substantially increase efficiency, especially when multiple tolerance thresholds are present. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of such conditional strategies (even in the absence of explicit costly punishment) can be as or more effective than standard punishment in raising efficiency, particularly in structured populations. The results are most relevant for games with spatial structure, optional participation, and where monitoring costs are not prohibitive. The paper does not directly manipulate or analyze explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, so its direct applicability to punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled prediction is limited, but it provides a strong adjacent signal about the power of conditional sanctioning and abstention. For prediction, the key moderators are the synergy factor (mpcr), the cost of monitoring (analogous to punishment cost), and the structure of the population.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhang, C. Y., & Cao, M. (2015). How insurance affects altruistic provision in threshold public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing insurance for contributors in threshold public goods games increases the provision of the public good and can improve the efficiency (group payoff) of the system. The effect is strongest when the insurance compensation is high and the required threshold for provision is high.
