The model shows that insurance can shift the evolutionary dynamics to favor contributors over defectors, depending on parameter values. The allocation rule for surplus contributions (parameter omega) also moderates the effect. The paper does not analyze punishment or sanctions, but provides a theoretical basis for how risk-mitigation mechanisms (like insurance) can promote cooperation and efficiency in threshold public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that risk-mitigation mechanisms (insurance) for contributors can increase efficiency in threshold public goods games, especially when the risk of loss is high and insurance compensation is generous. While it does not study punishment, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in games where contributors face risk and can be protected from loss. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, adding insurance or similar mechanisms can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency. The model is most applicable to threshold public goods games with all-or-nothing contributions, no communication, and no explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The results are parameter-dependent and highlight the importance of the threshold, insurance compensation, and allocation rules for surplus contributions.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhang, J. L., & Weissing, F. J. (2014). The role of emotions in the maintenance of cooperative behaviors. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a stress-based cost for defectors in a public goods game can make cooperation evolutionarily stable if the stress intensity exceeds a calculable threshold. This threshold is lower for larger populations and higher public goods multipliers, and higher for larger group sizes. The model provides explicit formulas for the fixation probability of cooperation and the conditions under which cooperation can invade and dominate a population of defectors. The findings are directly relevant to efficiency, as they show under what conditions the group can achieve higher payoffs through sustained cooperation, even in the absence of explicit external punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that internalized costs for defection (modeled as stress or moral emotions) can substitute for explicit punishment in promoting cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games. While it does not study explicit punishment, it shows that any mechanism that imposes a cost on defection (whether internal or external) can shift the evolutionary dynamics toward cooperation, provided the cost is above a threshold determined by game parameters (group size, population size, and MPCR). For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of any effective cost for defection—whether via punishment or internalized emotion—can increase efficiency, and that the required cost decreases with larger populations and higher MPCR. However, the model does not address explicit punishment technology, cost, or identification, so its direct applicability to those dimensions is limited.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2017). Evolution of commitment and level of participation in public goods games. *Autonomous Agents and Multi-agent Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a commitment mechanism—where players can propose binding agreements before the PGG, with compensation for defection—substantially increases both cooperation and average payoffs, provided the cost of arranging commitment is not too high. The effect is robust across group sizes and compensation levels, as long as compensation is above a threshold. The optimal participation threshold (number of committers required) depends on the severity of the dilemma and the cost of commitment. The model shows that commitment mechanisms can transform the efficiency of the PGG, making group payoffs much closer to the fully cooperative optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling credible commitment mechanisms in PGG-like environments can substantially increase efficiency, especially when the cost of arranging commitment is low relative to the benefit of cooperation. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of a commitment mechanism (even if not a classic punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency, with the effect size moderated by the cost of commitment, the mpcr, and the participation threshold. The results are robust across group sizes and compensation levels (above a threshold). However, the model does not directly analyze classic punishment (costly peer punishment), so direct transfer to punishment-enabled PGGs should be done with caution.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2015). Benefits of tolerance in public goods games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The presence of tolerant players, who monitor and respond to defection by switching to a loner strategy only when defection exceeds a threshold, can substantially increase the average payoff of the group in public goods games. This effect is strongest at intermediate levels of tolerance and is robust across both well-mixed and spatially structured populations. The model shows that neither unconditional cooperation nor immediate withdrawal (loner) is optimal; instead, a moderate tolerance threshold allows the group to maintain high efficiency, even under conditions where cooperation would otherwise be unsustainable. The paper does not include punishment or reward as explicit mechanisms, but positions tolerance as an alternative to these approaches.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games, the introduction of a tolerance-based strategy (where players cooperate unless defection exceeds a threshold) can dramatically improve group efficiency compared to standard models with only cooperators, defectors, and loners. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that mechanisms allowing conditional withdrawal (tolerance) can substitute for or outperform punishment in raising efficiency, especially when the cost of monitoring is not too high and the tolerance threshold is moderate. However, because the paper does not include explicit punishment or reward treatments, its direct applicability to predicting the effect of punishment is limited. Instead, it provides a benchmark for the potential of alternative mechanisms to improve efficiency.

- source: Moreira, J. A., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2013). Evolution of collective action in adaptive social structures. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can adapt their social ties—specifically, break links with defectors more quickly than with cooperators—cooperation can be sustained at higher levels than in well-mixed or static networks. The evolutionary dynamics shift from defection dominance to a scenario with stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, depending on the group size, enhancement factor, and network adaptation rates. Stochastic effects (e.g., mutation, exploration) further help populations escape defection traps and maintain cooperation. However, the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how endogenous partner selection and adaptive network structure can promote cooperation in PGGs, which may indirectly inform predictions about efficiency in environments where such mechanisms are present. However, it does not address punishment or sanctions, nor does it provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that adaptive partner selection can increase cooperation rates, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or other interventions.

- source: Zhang, H. R., & Perc, M. (2016). Evolution of conditional cooperation under multilevel selection. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in public goods games with continuous contribution and multilevel selection, the presence of conditional strategies (modeled as piecewise linear response functions) enables the emergence and stability of high cooperation levels. The dominant strategy is robust to initial conditions and parameter changes. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on average contribution and strategy prevalence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional cooperation strategies can sustain high contribution levels in public goods games under multilevel selection, even without punishment or reward. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment-related dimensions. Its findings are most relevant for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of explicit sanctioning mechanisms, and for interpreting the role of conditional strategies as an alternative to punishment. It is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2017). Alliance formation with exclusion in the spatial public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games, the introduction of an excluder strategy (which monitors and excludes defectors at a cost) can lead to the elimination of defectors when excluders form alliances with other cooperative strategies (cooperators or punishers). This effect is robust even when the cost of exclusion is extremely high, provided the population structure allows for the formation of sufficiently large clusters of excluders and cooperators/punishers. The main outcomes are the extinction probabilities of defectors and the conditions for the survival of excluders, not direct measures of group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that exclusion-based sanctions (a form of punishment) can be highly effective in structured populations, especially when combined with other cooperative strategies. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it suggests that under certain spatial and strategic conditions, sanctioning mechanisms can eliminate defection, which would likely increase efficiency, but this is not quantified. The findings are most relevant for understanding the qualitative impact of exclusion and alliances in spatially structured games, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Wang, Z., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2014). Rewarding evolutionary fitness with links between populations promotes cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that rewarding players whose utility exceeds a threshold by granting them external links to another population can significantly promote cooperation in both public goods and prisoner's dilemma games on structured networks. The effect is maximized at intermediate threshold values, where rewarded players are rare but able to percolate, acting as catalysts for cooperation. If the threshold is too low or too high, the effect diminishes. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. The results are robust across network types and game details. Punishment is not modeled, only discussed as background.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It models a reward mechanism (external links for high-fitness players) and analyzes its effect on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or payoff. While it discusses the relative merits of reward and punishment in the introduction, all results pertain to reward. Therefore, it is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency, except as context for how reward mechanisms can promote cooperation under certain network and threshold conditions.

- source: Zhou, T. W., Ding, S., Fan, W. J., & Wang, H. (2016). An improved public goods game model with reputation effect on the spatial lattices. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that embedding a reputation effect into the utility function of agents in a spatial PGG model leads to a substantial increase in the stationary fraction of cooperators, especially as the reputation factor increases. The critical synergy factor required for cooperation to emerge is reduced, and the region of parameter space supporting full cooperation is expanded. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are based on Monte Carlo simulations and are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation in spatial PGGs, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. For the downstream prediction task—predicting efficiency effects of punishment—this paper is only weakly informative. It suggests that non-monetary social mechanisms (like reputation) can increase cooperation, but does not provide data or analysis on how punishment affects efficiency, nor does it report group payoff or welfare. The game design dimensions are described in detail for the reputation mechanism, but not for punishment or efficiency.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2017). Publishing the donation list incompletely promotes the emergence of cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: Publishing the donation list incompletely (only for those who contribute above a threshold) leads to higher total contributions and more equal contributions among players in a public goods game on a network. There is an optimal threshold for publication that maximizes these effects. The per capita number of neighbors and the amount of historical data players consider also moderate the effect, but the main result is robust: partial transparency outperforms full transparency or secrecy. The model is agent-based and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on social reputation and face loss as motivators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that social information mechanisms (such as partial publication of contribution lists) can increase cooperation in public goods games, but it does not study monetary punishment or its effect on efficiency. The findings are relevant for predicting the effect of information disclosure or reputation-based interventions, not for predicting the effect of adding costly punishment. The sum of contributions is a proxy for group welfare, but efficiency (as defined by payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly reported. Use this paper as informative for the effect of social/reputational incentives, not for monetary punishment treatments.

- source: Yang, Z. H., Li, Z., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2013). Effects of adaptive dynamical linking in networked games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that adaptive dynamical linking—where agents lengthen or shorten social ties based on whether their partners cooperate or defect—can dramatically increase the prevalence of cooperation in both the prisoner's dilemma and public goods game. The effect is stronger when agents adjust their links more rapidly (higher adjustment rate, Δ), and under weak selection. The mechanism does not involve any explicit punishment, reward, or reputation system. The outcome measured is the frequency of full cooperation (all-cooperator absorbing state) in repeated simulations. There is no direct measurement or discussion of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task of how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant as a demonstration that network adaptation (partner switching) can promote cooperation, but it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be contextually useful for understanding alternative mechanisms (partner choice) that can promote cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

  findings: The study finds that introducing conditional participation (interpreted as a form of punishment) in a spatial public goods game can promote cooperation, but only within an optimal range of aspiration levels. If aspiration is too low, the effect is minimal; if too high, cooperation is suppressed. The transition from defection to cooperation can be abrupt or gradual depending on aspiration and synergy factor. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff. The model does not include standard costly punishment or direct payoff reduction, and does not report efficiency or welfare outcomes.

The conditional participation mechanism is adjacent to punishment, but differs in implementation and effect. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The findings may be useful for understanding how conditional participation or abstention can affect cooperation, but should not be directly used to predict efficiency effects of standard punishment interventions.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., & Chen, Z. Q. (2016). The public goods game with a new form of shared reward. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a shared reward, proportional to the number of cooperators, can enable cooperation to invade and become stable in a population of defectors if the reward parameter exceeds a critical threshold. This threshold depends negatively on the population size and the public goods multiplier, and positively on the group size. Larger populations and higher public goods multipliers make it easier for cooperation to be favored, while larger group sizes make it harder. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, and thus does not directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. However, it does provide theoretical insight into how reward mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can affect the evolution of cooperation and group payoff. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but may be useful for understanding the comparative role of reward-based interventions.

- source: Reeves, T., Ohtsuki, H., & Fukui, S. (2017). Asymmetric public goods game cooperation through pest control. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric public goods games (modeled as pest control among farmers), the Nash equilibrium investment and resulting efficiency depend strongly on the discount factor and the number of players. There are threshold effects: only farmers close to the risk (frontier) contribute when the discount factor is low, while more distant farmers contribute only if the discount factor is high enough. Increasing the number of players does not always increase efficiency; in some parameter regions, adding more players can paradoxically reduce total investment and group welfare. Asymmetry leads to a disproportionate burden on those at greatest risk, making the system more vulnerable to shocks. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for how efficiency in asymmetric public goods games depends on player count, discount factor, and the structure of risk, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is highly informative for baseline (control) efficiency in asymmetric, no-punishment settings, especially regarding the effects of group size and discounting. However, it does not inform the effect of enabling punishment, as no such mechanism is present or analyzed. Its findings suggest that efficiency can be non-monotonic in group size and is sensitive to asymmetry and time preferences, which should be considered when predicting control efficiency, but it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment.

- source: Henrich, J., Chudek, M., & Boyd, R. (2015). The Big Man Mechanism: how prestige fosters cooperation and creates prosocial leaders. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that prestige-based leadership, via cultural transmission, can generate and sustain cooperation in public goods game environments, leading to higher group efficiency and payoffs. The effect depends on the probability that followers copy the leader (prestige effect, p), the stickiness of acquired behaviors (s), and group size (n). Cooperation is more likely to evolve and persist in small groups, with high prestige effects and high stickiness. The model predicts that prestigious leaders will be more prosocial in small groups, but this effect diminishes in large groups. The paper does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in its models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games without punishment, the presence of prestige-biased cultural transmission and prosocial leaders can increase group efficiency, especially in small groups and when followers are likely to copy and retain the leader's cooperative behavior. However, because the models do not include punishment or sanctions, the findings do not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The results are most relevant for understanding baseline efficiency in games with prestige-based leadership and social learning, not for predicting the marginal effect of punishment. If a game design includes prestige or leading-by-example mechanisms, this paper suggests that efficiency may already be elevated, and the incremental effect of punishment may be smaller or interact with these mechanisms.

- source: Hilbe, C., Wu, B., Traulsen, A., & Nowak, M. A. (2015). Evolutionary performance of zero-determinant strategies in multiplayer games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that repeated interactions (direct reciprocity) can sustain cooperation and high efficiency in small groups, but not in large groups. As group size increases, the evolutionary dynamics favor defectors and extortioners, leading to a sharp drop in average group payoff. The results are robust across both restricted (ZD strategies only) and general (all memory-one strategies) strategy spaces. The paper provides explicit formulas for payoffs and demonstrates, both analytically and via simulation, that group size is a critical moderator of efficiency in repeated public goods games without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, increasing group size in repeated public goods games leads to lower efficiency, even when direct reciprocity is possible. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that the baseline efficiency in control games without punishment will be high for small groups but will decline as group size increases. The paper does not provide evidence about the effect of enabling punishment, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency change when punishment is enabled. However, it does highlight that, without additional mechanisms (like punishment), large groups are unlikely to sustain cooperation or high efficiency.

- source: Meloni, S., Xia, C. Y., & Moreno, Y. (2017). Heterogeneous resource allocation can change social hierarchy in public goods games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when players in a networked PGG can allocate their contributions unevenly—favoring games where they previously earned higher payoffs—this leads to increased cooperation, a more heterogeneous distribution of investments, and a shift in the structure of wealth production. Specifically, small clusters of cooperators become highly productive, and the overall wealth distribution approaches the Pareto law (80% of wealth produced by 20% of nodes) for sufficiently strong payoff-driven allocation. The study also shows that the classical PGG (equal allocation) does not reproduce these features. The model is robust to different evolutionary update rules and network structures. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions in PGGs or their impact on efficiency. It is highly relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in resource allocation affects efficiency and wealth distribution in PGGs, but it cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task—estimating the efficiency impact of punishment given game design dimensions—this paper is not directly informative, as punishment is not present in the model.

  findings: The study finds that heterogeneity in both investment and payoff allocation, especially under the mixed-preference mechanism (where both previous payoff and social status influence decisions), promotes higher average payoff and cooperation rates compared to mechanisms based solely on wealth or social status. Increasing the enhancement factor r or reducing the heterogeneity parameter alpha further increases cooperation and average payoff. - source: Sun, C. H., & Duan, H. B. (2015). Impact of shill intervention on the evolution of cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The introduction of shill agents with a Fixed-Cost-per-Player contribution rule into a PGG on a scale-free network can substantially increase both cooperation rates and average payoffs (social welfare), particularly when shills are preferentially placed on high-degree nodes and the dilemma is severe (low enhancement factor). The effect is strongest for high-degree-preference shill placement (large alpha) and in harsh environments. As the environment becomes more favorable (higher enhancement factor), local cooperators reappear and can coexist with shill cooperators, and distributing shills on low-degree nodes (small alpha) can maximize social welfare. The paper provides detailed simulation evidence on how these effects depend on the enhancement factor, shill fraction, and shill placement, but does not study punishment or sanctioning.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments with external intervention via shill agents, but not for environments with punishment or sanctioning. The findings suggest that introducing agents with alternative contribution rules (partial free-riders) can increase efficiency in harsh environments, especially if they are placed on network hubs. The results are parameter-dependent and highlight the importance of network structure and agent placement. However, since the paper does not involve punishment, its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency is limited. It is most useful for understanding the effects of external intervention and heterogeneity in contribution rules on efficiency.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., Wu, T., Chen, X. J., Xie, G. M., & Wang, L. (2013). Mixed strategy under generalized public goods games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary outcome in generalized public goods games with mixed strategies depends critically on the shape of the production function, the group size, the population size, and the cost-to-benefit ratio. For linear production functions, the outcome is determined by the cost/benefit ratio. For nonlinear functions, concave shapes favor stable coexistence (CESS), convex shapes favor bistability (repeller), and sigmoidal shapes can produce more complex dynamics with multiple equilibria. When the group size equals the population size, cooperation is always lost. The paper provides explicit conditions for the emergence of full cooperation, full defection, or intermediate equilibria, but does not address the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed theoretical predictions about how efficiency (group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) in public goods games depends on the production function, group size, population size, and cost/benefit ratio, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it is highly informative for understanding baseline efficiency in control games without punishment, especially as a function of game design parameters such as group size, population size, and the nonlinearity of the public goods production function.

- source: Olejarz, J. W., & Nowak, M. A. (2014). Evolution of staying together in the context of diffusible public goods. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in public goods games with diffusible goods can evolve if the benefit-to-cost ratio exceeds a threshold determined by the probability of staying together (q) and the internal absorption efficiency (alpha_k). Clustering increases the efficiency of cooperation by allowing cooperators to preferentially benefit from their own public goods. The model also shows that if defectors and cooperators differ in their ability to absorb public goods, coexistence or bistability can occur. Introducing a cost to large clusters (gamma_k) can create an optimal cluster size for cooperation. The model provides explicit analytical conditions for these outcomes, but does not consider punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how structural and spatial factors (such as clustering and internal absorption efficiency) affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games. However, it does not address punishment or sanctioning, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in control games without punishment, or in environments where clustering and spatial structure are the main mechanisms supporting cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how efficiency depends on clustering, internal benefit allocation, and the benefit-to-cost ratio, but offers no evidence about punishment effects.

- source: Liu, P. H., & Liu, J. (2017). Contribution diversity and incremental learning promote cooperation in public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that both contribution diversity (CD)—where individuals reallocate their contributions based on response rates—and incremental learning (IL)—where individuals imitate those with long-term high payoffs—promote cooperation in PGGs on scale-free networks. CD is more effective at higher mpcr (eta), while IL stabilizes cooperation even when CD is less effective. The combination of CD and IL is most effective. The main reported outcomes are cooperation frequency and the average payoff/fitness difference between cooperators and defectors. No group efficiency or total group payoff is reported. The models do not include punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs, as punishment is not modeled. It does provide indirect evidence that mechanisms other than punishment (contribution diversity and incremental learning) can promote cooperation and increase the relative payoff of cooperators, especially at higher mpcr. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative. For tasks considering alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms, the findings suggest that structural and learning modifications can substitute for punishment in promoting cooperation, but the effect on group efficiency is not quantified.

- source: Amado, A., Huang, W. N., Campos, P. R. A., & Ferreira, F. F. (2015). Learning process in public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with bounded rationality and finite memory, both the learning process (dynamic vs static) and the public goods enhancement factor (r) strongly affect cooperation rates. In dynamic learning, increasing r leads to a transition from defection to a neutral regime (cooperation ~50%), while in static learning, cooperation can exceed 50% when r > N. Memory size smooths the transition from defection to cooperation. The expected payoff change is positive when r > 1 and cooperation is present, but the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a main outcome. No punishment or reward mechanisms are included.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide detailed modeling of how learning, memory, and the enhancement factor r affect cooperation and expected payoffs in standard PGGs. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide useful context for baseline (no-punishment) efficiency as a function of r, memory, and learning process.

- source: Li, A. M., Wu, T., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2013). One step memory of group reputation is optimal to promote cooperation in public goods games. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with dynamic group formation based on group reputation (which is determined by individual memory of past cooperation), cooperation is maximized when individuals have short memory (one-step). Moderate levels of expectation about group reputation and sufficient opportunities to change groups (network dynamics) also promote cooperation. Too high or too low expectations, or too long memory, reduce cooperation. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings about memory, reputation, and group switching may be relevant for understanding other mechanisms that promote cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Shi, D. M., & Wang, B. H. (2017). Critical mass of public goods and its coevolution with cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation is maximized at an intermediate value of the critical mass threshold (cm), with both too low and too high thresholds leading to lower cooperation. The nature of the public good (consumable vs. reusable) and the depreciation rate parameter (Gamma) affect the optimal threshold and the dynamics of cooperation. The results are robust across different network structures. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or any sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings about critical mass and cooperation density may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Li, Z. F., Shen, B., & Jiang, Y. C. (2017). The maintenance of cooperation in multiplex networks with limited and partible resources of agents. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in multiplex public goods games with limited and partible resources, cooperation is more likely to be maintained when at least one network layer is scale-free (high degree diversity) and when agents use a 'greedy-first' updating rule (imitating strategies in the higher-payoff layer). Homogeneous network structures and 'generous-first' updating rules lead to higher rates of defection and the collapse of cooperation. The study does not consider punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. However, it is informative about the role of network structure and resource allocation mechanisms in sustaining cooperation in multiplex PGGs. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may provide contextual insight into how structural and behavioral mechanisms (other than punishment) affect cooperation.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneity in cooperative investments (modeled as a uniform distribution around a mean) increases the steady-state fraction of cooperators in a spatial public goods game. This effect is stronger with greater variance in investment. When both strategy and investment coevolve, the promotion of cooperation is weaker because investment heterogeneity is reduced. The main dependent variable is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show that heterogeneity in investment can promote cooperation (as measured by the fraction of cooperators), but do not address payoff-based outcomes or the effect of punishment. It should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Xu, K. Y., Li, K., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2017). Cooperation guided by the coexistence of imitation dynamics and aspiration dynamics in structured populations. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the coexistence of imitation and aspiration dynamics in structured populations can have nontrivial effects on cooperation. For fixed update rules, introducing a moderate fraction of aspiration-driven individuals into an imitation-driven population can cause a 'plunge of cooperation' at intermediate synergy factors. When both strategies and update rules coevolve, only one update rule eventually dominates, and the stationary cooperation level depends on which rule prevails. Aspiration dynamics can promote cooperation when the synergy factor is low (poor synergy), but undermine it when the synergy factor is high. However, the study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings about the effects of update rules (imitation vs aspiration) on cooperation rates may be of indirect interest for understanding behavioral dynamics, but are not directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with or without punishment. It should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Stivala, A., Kashima, Y., & Kirley, M. (2016). Culture and cooperation in a spatial public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model shows that cooperation (measured as the fraction of conditional cooperators) only survives in multicultural states with high cultural diversity and very low or zero noise. In monocultural states or with any significant noise, cooperation dies out. The number of participants in public goods games is inversely related to cultural diversity. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it does provide contextual evidence that group structure, diversity, and noise can strongly affect cooperation rates. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding the role of cultural diversity and conditional cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Wang, Y. J., & Chen, T. (2015). Heuristics guide cooperative behaviors in public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the presence and strength of 'cooperators with independent thought' (CIs) can strongly promote and stabilize cooperation in a spatial PGG. When the heuristic capability of CIs exceeds that of 'defectors with independent thought' (DIs), cooperation dominates; when the reverse is true, defection dominates. If both have equal heuristic capability, coexistence occurs. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is not informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the model. The findings are only indirectly relevant, as they pertain to the role of heuristic-driven strategy updating and independent thought in promoting cooperation, not to institutional design features such as punishment.

- source: Wang, X. F., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2014). Do not aim too high nor too low: Moderate expectation-based group formation promotes public cooperation on networks. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to leave groups that do not meet their expectation for cooperativeness and to form new ties elsewhere can substantially increase the frequency of cooperation in public goods games on networks. The effect is strongest when individuals have a moderate expectation threshold (neither too high nor too low). The time scale ratio of selection to interaction also matters: more frequent interaction relative to selection promotes cooperation. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how endogenous group formation and network rewiring based on individual expectations can affect cooperation rates, but not efficiency or payoff. The findings may be indirectly informative for settings where group structure is endogenous, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The absence of punishment and the lack of payoff-based outcome reporting limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

  findings: The study finds that introducing a weight parameter (alpha) to differentiate the influence of less and more cautious conditional cooperators changes which strategies survive in the population. For low multiplication factor (r), increasing alpha allows more types of conditional cooperators to coexist, while for high r, less cautious cooperators dominate. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing a recommendation mechanism based on group quality in spatial public goods games can significantly increase the frequency of cooperators, especially for intermediate values of the group quality threshold (Q_c). However, the study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.

- source: Zhong, L. X., Xu, W. J., Shi, Y. D., & Qiu, T. (2013). Coupled dynamics of mobility and pattern formation in optional public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that both population density and agent mobility (free-floating probability) have non-monotonic effects on the frequency of cooperation in the OPGG. At low density and low mobility, cooperation is high; increasing either density or mobility generally reduces cooperation by making it easier for defectors to exploit cooperators. There is an optimal population density for cooperation, and the relationship between mobility and cooperation follows a power law. The results are explained in terms of agent collisions and cluster formation. However, the paper does not consider punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how population density and mobility affect cooperation rates in optional participation settings without sanctions. It should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to study how conformity-driven behavior affects cooperation in public goods games with binary heterogeneous endowments (rich and poor). The main finding is a 'double-edged sword' effect: when the multiplication factor (synergy of cooperation) is high, conformity enhances cooperation by strengthening network reciprocity; when the multiplication factor is low, conformity inhibits cooperation, especially among the poor, by allowing defection to infiltrate cooperative clusters. These effects are robust to variations in conformity strength, the proportion of rich, and the extension to multiplayer PGGs. However, the study only reports on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on conformity and cooperation rates in heterogeneous endowment settings may be contextually informative for understanding behavioral dynamics in PGGs, but are not directly transferable to efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor to the effects of punishment. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Liu, A. Z., Zhang, Y. L., Chen, X. J., & Sun, C. Y. (2017). Randomness and diversity matter in the maintenance of the public resources. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that both greater diversity (more available contribution levels) and greater randomness (higher mutation, smaller population, weaker selection) increase the average investment in the public goods game. Under weak selection, average investment is high and insensitive to diversity or randomness. Under strong selection, average investment is low and insensitive to diversity or population size, but increases with mutation. Under intermediate selection, average investment increases with diversity and mutation, and decreases with population size. No payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported, and no punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. It is only relevant for understanding how diversity in contribution options and evolutionary randomness affect average investment in the absence of enforcement mechanisms. It provides no evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency.

  findings: The SEPGG with voluntary participation exhibits distinct stationary states depending on the multiplication factor (r), group size (N), and network density (mean degree). On complete graphs (all-to-all), either defectors or loners dominate depending on r, with no stable cooperator-dominant state. On sparse networks, cooperation can dominate for sufficiently low mean degree. The paper provides scaling laws for the transitions between these states. However, the study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff; it focuses on the prevalence of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how group size and network structure affect cooperation and opting out in voluntary participation PGGs, but not for the effect of punishment. The results may be useful as a baseline for the behavior of voluntary participation PGGs without punishment, but cannot inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.
