  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the degree of rationality (parameter alpha) in a subset of players leads to a higher fraction of cooperators in the population. When more players use rational allocation strategies, cooperation is promoted, and the phase transition points for cooperation occur at lower enhancement factors. The ratio of rational to conventional players also modulates cooperation: more rational players lead to more cooperation. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or direct efficiency/payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in rationality and investment strategies can promote cooperation, but it does not inform predictions about punishment-enabled versus punishment-disabled efficiency. The absence of punishment and the focus on cooperation rates rather than payoffs limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Chen, M. H., Wang, L., Wang, J., Sun, S. W., & Xia, C. Y. (2015). Impact of individual response strategy on the spatial public goods game within mobile agents. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the order in which agents update their strategy and move (strategy update order) can significantly affect the evolution of cooperation and social instability in spatial PGGs with mobile agents. Different aspiration evaluation methods (how agents assess satisfaction based on their own and neighbors' payoffs) have only minor effects on cooperation and agglomeration, but can influence social instability. The model does not include punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how mobility and aspiration-based strategy updating affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based efficiency outcomes or the impact of punishment. It may provide weak contextual information about the role of behavioral updating and mobility, but is not informative for the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Broom, M., Lafaye, C., Pattni, K., & Rychtár, J. (2015). A study of the dynamics of multi-player games on small networks using territorial interactions. *Journal of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the fixation probability of cooperators in the public goods game is generally less than 1/N (where N is the number of players), indicating that cooperation is disfavored under the birth-death process in these small networked populations. The fixation probability is strongly influenced by the mean temperature (a measure of how often individuals are replaced), with higher temperatures amplifying selection and lower temperatures suppressing it. The specific graph structure (network topology) has some effect, but mean temperature is a more important determinant. The results are derived for small graphs (3-4 players), and the framework is generalizable to larger graphs in principle. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide theoretical insights into how network structure and group size affect the evolutionary success of cooperation versus defection in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, but it may provide contextual background on how structural features (such as player count and group formation) influence cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Figuières, C., Masclet, D., & Willinger, M. (2013). Weak Moral Motivation Leads to the Decline of Voluntary Contributions. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals' moral motivation is weak (i.e., sensitive to others' contributions), average contributions in repeated public goods games decline over time. The model predicts that this decay is robust under both myopic and forward-looking behavior, and can explain several empirical regularities observed in experiments, such as the restart effect and slower decay in longer games. The model does not include punishment or sanctions, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not model or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. Its main relevance is in providing a theoretical account of contribution decay in standard linear public goods games without punishment. It may be useful for understanding baseline dynamics in the absence of punishment, but does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Wang, Y. F., Vasilakos, A. V., & Ma, J. H. (2015). VPEF: A Simple and Effective Incentive Mechanism in Community-Based Autonomous Networks. *Ieee Transactions on Network and Service Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: VPEF, an incentive mechanism based on voluntary participation, round-based entry fees, and voluntary costly punishment, leads to high efficiency in community-based collaborative networks. The system spends most of its time in the punisher state, where all participants contribute and punish defectors, minimizing efficiency loss. The efficiency loss is analytically shown to be 4/(8+M), which becomes negligible for large communities. Simulations confirm the theoretical predictions. The mechanism is robust to changes in group size, entry fee (as long as it is not too high), and multiplexing/diversity factor. The model assumes strong selection (users imitate higher-payoff strategies) and does not require tracking long-term behavior or permanent exclusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling voluntary costly punishment in a public-goods-like environment with voluntary participation and a small entry fee can dramatically increase efficiency, driving the system to near-full cooperation (punisher state) and minimizing efficiency loss. The efficiency loss formula (4/(8+M)) can be used to predict the efficiency of the punishment-enabled game, given the number of users. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values, provided the multiplexing/diversity factor is above 1 and defectors can be reliably detected. The model is most applicable to dynamic, anonymous, and voluntary participation settings, and less so to settings with continuous contribution levels or where punishment/reward is not voluntary.

- source: Lee, J. H., Sigmund, K., Dieckmann, U., & Iwasa, Y. (2015). Games of corruption: How to suppress illegal logging. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that punishment (via hired enforcers) can sustain high efficiency (cooperation and group welfare) only if a sufficient fraction of enforcers are honest. If corruption is prevalent among enforcers, punishment fails and efficiency collapses. The system is bistable: both high- and low-efficiency equilibria are possible. Small rates of random exploration (mutation) among enforcers can tip the system toward either high or low efficiency, depending on whether honest or corrupt exploration is more common. Providing information about enforcer honesty (i.e., making corrupt enforcers identifiable) greatly increases the likelihood of high-efficiency outcomes. The model shows that interventions targeting enforcer honesty (education, incentives, transparency) are more effective for improving efficiency than interventions targeting harvesters directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in improving efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the integrity of the punishment institution. If enforcers are honest and/or can be identified as such, punishment can lead to high efficiency. If enforcers are corrupt and cannot be identified, punishment may fail and efficiency may be low. The model suggests that game design dimensions that increase the likelihood of honest enforcement (e.g., transparency, information sharing, enforcer education) will increase the efficiency gain from punishment. The presence of bistability means that initial conditions and small biases (e.g., in enforcer behavior) can have large effects. For prediction, this paper implies that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the effectiveness and honesty of the punishment mechanism, and on whether players can identify corrupt enforcers.

- source: Okada, I., Yamamoto, H., Toriumi, F., & Sasaki, T. (2015). The Effect of Incentives and Meta-incentives on the Evolution of Cooperation. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a theoretical evolutionary game framework, cooperation and high efficiency (group payoff) can only be stably achieved if the incentive system includes second-order incentives for first-order rewards. Systems with only first-order punishment or reward, or with second-order incentives for punishment but not for reward, cannot maintain cooperation or efficiency in the long run due to the second-order free-rider problem. The cost-effect ratio of incentives (how much impact per unit cost) is a critical parameter: higher ratios make it easier for cooperation to be sustained. The model predicts that systems with second-order rewards for first-order incentives are more robust and can achieve high efficiency with fewer incentive-providers than systems with second-order punishments. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using replicator dynamics, and does not provide empirical estimates of efficiency but gives qualitative and parametric conditions for when efficiency is high or low under different incentive structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment and reward systems (including meta-incentives) on efficiency in public-goods-like games. It suggests that simply enabling punishment is not sufficient for high efficiency; the structure of incentives, especially the presence of second-order incentives for rewarding, is crucial. If a game design includes only first-order punishment, efficiency will not improve in the long run. If second-order incentives for first-order rewards are present and the cost-effect ratio is high, efficiency can be high. The model also clarifies that the effect of punishment on efficiency is conditional on the broader incentive structure. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that the presence or absence of second-order incentives, and the cost-effectiveness of punishment/reward, are key moderators of the efficiency effect of punishment.

- source: Farjam, M., Faillo, M., Sprinkhuizen-Kuyper, I., & Haselager, P. (2015). Punishment Mechanisms and Their Effect on Cooperation: A Simulation Study. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment can facilitate higher group efficiency (average energy) in environments where resources are scarce (low MPCR), but only when the punishment mechanism is designed to avoid antisocial punishment and minimize costs (legitimate, zero loss, hired gun). Unrestricted punishment consistently reduces efficiency. When resources are abundant (high MPCR), punishment mechanisms do not improve and may reduce efficiency compared to no-punishment. The results are robust to variations in group size and punishment cost ratio. The study provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by resource availability (analogous to MPCR in PGGs) and by the design of the punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in PGG-like environments depends critically on both the marginal per-capita return (MPCR, here operationalized as resource availability) and the specific punishment mechanism. When MPCR is low (resources scarce), enabling well-designed punishment (legitimate, zero loss, hired gun) increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, but unrestricted punishment reduces efficiency. When MPCR is high (resources abundant), punishment does not improve and may reduce efficiency. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the effect of punishment on efficiency should be modeled as conditional on both MPCR and the punishment mechanism. The results are robust to moderate changes in group size and punishment cost ratio, so these dimensions are less likely to moderate the direction of the effect.

- source: Gavrilets, S., & Richerson, P. J. (2017). Collective action and the evolution of social norm internalization. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to show that norm internalization evolves more readily and has larger effects on cooperation when peer punishment of free riders is promoted. In public-goods-like ('us-vs.-nature') games, the evolution of norm internalization and punishment increases cooperation and material payoffs (efficiency). In intergroup contest ('us-vs.-them') games, norm internalization and punishment increase cooperation but reduce material payoffs due to overproduction and rent dissipation. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore positive in public-goods-like settings and negative in intergroup contest settings. The model also predicts that group size, punishment cost, and the strength of social norms moderate these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the type of collective action problem. In standard public goods (us-vs.-nature) games, enabling punishment increases efficiency by reducing free riding. In intergroup contest (us-vs.-them) games, enabling punishment can reduce efficiency due to excessive effort and resource dissipation. The model also suggests that group size and punishment cost are important moderators. For prediction, the context of the collective action (public goods vs. contest), group size, and punishment parameters should be considered.

- source: Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2016). How to keep punishment to maintain cooperation: Introducing social vaccine. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the meta-norms game, costly punishment and meta-punishment alone are insufficient to robustly maintain cooperation and high group efficiency, due to the vulnerability to second-order free-riding. However, the coercive introduction of a small number of always-defecting agents (the 'social vaccine') stabilizes the punishment system, prevents the drift toward non-punishing cooperators, and maintains a highly cooperative regime. This results in average payoffs for the group that are close to those of a fully cooperative group, even in parameter regions where cooperation would otherwise collapse. The effect is robust to changes in population size, temptation to defect, and punishment cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with meta-norms (second-order punishment), the presence of a small number of persistent defectors can dramatically increase the efficiency of the punishment regime, maintaining high group payoffs where they would otherwise collapse. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the stability and efficiency of punishment-enabled games may depend not only on the formal punishment parameters but also on the presence of persistent norm violators. In the absence of such 'social vaccine' agents, efficiency gains from punishment may be fragile or short-lived, especially in longer games or with low punishment costs. The findings are most directly relevant to games with meta-punishment and compulsory participation, and may be less directly transferable to standard linear PGGs without meta-norms.

- source: Lee, J. H., Jusup, M., & Iwasa, Y. (2017). Games of corruption in preventing the overuse of common-pool resources. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that delegated punishment (enforcement) can support efficient, fully cooperative outcomes in common-pool resource games, but only if a sufficient fraction of enforcers are honest. If corruption is prevalent among enforcers, punishment loses its effectiveness, and the system converges to defection and resource overuse (low efficiency). The model exhibits bistability: depending on initial conditions (notably the initial fraction of honest enforcers), the system ends up either in full cooperation (high efficiency) or full defection (low efficiency). Key parameters moderating the effect of punishment include group size (larger groups make cooperation harder), resource growth rate (higher growth rates favor defection), and the speed of resource dynamics (slower resource response masks the benefits of cooperation and impedes efficiency). The cost of harvesting has a mixed effect. The model provides direct predictions about how these dimensions affect the likelihood of efficient outcomes under punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like resource games depends critically on the honesty of enforcers and several game design dimensions. If punishment is implemented via delegated enforcement and enforcers are honest, punishment can enable high efficiency (full cooperation). However, if enforcers are corrupt, punishment fails and efficiency collapses. The model shows that increasing group size, increasing resource growth rate, and slowing resource dynamics all reduce the effectiveness of punishment in achieving efficiency. These findings should inform predictions about treatment efficiency: punishment will only improve efficiency if the enforcement mechanism is robust to corruption and the group size and ecological parameters are favorable. The model is most directly relevant to settings with delegated (not peer) punishment and where corruption is possible.

- source: Powers, S. T., & Lehmann, L. (2013). The co-evolution of social institutions, demography, and large-scale human cooperation. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the co-evolution of institutions with sanctioning (punishment) and demography enables the stable emergence and maintenance of large-scale cooperation, resulting in a dramatic increase in group carrying capacity and efficiency. In the absence of institutionalized punishment, cooperation cannot be stably maintained, and group size/efficiency remains low. The model demonstrates that the optimal institution invests most, but not all, of the public good into technological improvement (increasing carrying capacity), while retaining enough for sanctioning to deter defectors. Sensitivity analyses show that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to changes in key parameters, though it is diminished by high migration rates or low benefits of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling institutionalized punishment in public-goods-like environments will increase group efficiency, especially in structured populations. The model directly links the presence and effectiveness of punishment to higher group payoffs and larger sustainable group sizes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing or strengthening punishment mechanisms should increase efficiency, provided the cost of punishment is not prohibitive and the benefit from cooperation is sufficient. The model also highlights the importance of population structure (limited migration) and the allocation of resources between sanctioning and productive investment. While the model is not an empirical experiment, its detailed mapping to public goods game structure and explicit focus on efficiency make it highly informative for predicting the impact of punishment on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Iwasa, Y., & Lee, J. H. (2013). Graduated punishment is efficient in resource management if people are heterogeneous. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that graduated punishment (punishment increasing with the harm caused by selfish actions) is only efficient—i.e., maximizes group welfare—when two conditions are met: (1) there is heterogeneity among players in their sensitivity to utility differences, and (2) there is a nonzero probability of false accusation (punishing prosocials by mistake). In standard rational or homogeneous quantal response models, optimal punishment is not graduated but rather jumps from zero to a high level. With heterogeneity and error, the optimal punishment increases smoothly with harm, matching the graduated punishment observed in successful resource management. The paper provides explicit formulas for optimal punishment and shows how efficiency-maximizing punishment depends on model parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games depends critically on the presence of player heterogeneity and error in monitoring. In standard homogeneous or error-free settings, punishment is either absent or severe, with little efficiency gain from graduation. When heterogeneity and error are present, graduated punishment is optimal and can improve efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency will be more positive in environments with player heterogeneity and monitoring errors, and that the optimal punishment level should increase with the harm caused by defection. The paper's analytic results can inform parameterization of efficiency predictions as a function of harm, benefit, error rates, and heterogeneity.

- source: Lee, J. H., & Iwasa, Y. (2014). Modeling socio-economic aspects of ecosystem management and biodiversity conservation. *Population Ecology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: The paper's third model demonstrates that in social dilemma situations (akin to public goods or common-pool resource games), introducing punishment for selfish behavior can increase group welfare, especially when punishment is graduated (i.e., increases with the harm caused). The model shows that with evaluation errors and heterogeneity in player sensitivity to utility, graduated punishment is optimal for maximizing efficiency (group payoff). Severe, non-graduated punishment is only optimal under unrealistic assumptions. The findings support the use of graduated sanctions to improve efficiency in resource management games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in public-goods-like environments with social dilemmas, enabling punishment (especially graduated punishment) increases group efficiency/welfare compared to no-punishment baselines, provided that punishment is not excessively severe and is sensitive to the harm caused. The model is abstract but maps closely to PGG-like games with two actions (cooperate/defect), and the efficiency outcome is directly analyzed. The findings are most applicable to settings where punishment can be graduated and where player heterogeneity and evaluation errors exist. The paper does not provide empirical effect sizes or parameterized predictions, but it gives qualitative guidance that enabling (graduated) punishment should increase efficiency relative to control.

- source: Vlerick, M. (2016). Explaining Universal Social Institutions: A Game-Theoretic Approach. *Topoi-an International Review of Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of institutions—specifically those that include punishment for non-cooperation—transforms the payoff matrix of social dilemmas, such as the tragedy of the commons or property theft, resulting in a shift from inefficient Nash equilibria (low group payoff) to efficient cooperative equilibria (high group payoff). The models show that when punishment is available and used to sanction defectors or freeriders, the group achieves higher total payoffs and resource preservation. The argument is made through theoretical modeling, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments will increase group efficiency and total payoff, relative to control conditions without punishment. The models show that punishment changes the incentives so that cooperation becomes the dominant strategy, leading to higher group welfare. However, the paper does not provide empirical estimates or test specific parameter values, so its guidance is qualitative: punishment mechanisms, when credible and enforced, are predicted to improve efficiency in social dilemmas.

- source: Gao, L., Wang, Z., Pansini, R., Li, Y. T., & Wang, R. W. (2015). Collective punishment is more effective than collective reward for promoting cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that collective punishment is more effective than collective reward in promoting and stabilizing cooperation in multiplayer snowdrift games, especially for small groups or high initial cooperation. When the punishment-to-benefit ratio exceeds a threshold, a stable state of full cooperation emerges for both small and large groups, while such a state does not appear for large groups with only reward. The main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, which is closely related to group payoff but not a direct measure of efficiency. The results suggest that, in environments where individual-level discrimination is not possible, collective punishment can maintain high cooperation and thus higher group fitness or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in multiplayer public-goods-like environments (specifically, snowdrift games), enabling collective punishment (even when it cannot be targeted at individuals) can substantially increase the frequency and stability of cooperation, especially in small groups or when the punishment intensity is high. This implies that, for prediction tasks, the presence and strength of collective punishment should be expected to increase efficiency or group payoff, particularly in small groups. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions of efficiency as defined by total group payoff relative to full cooperation, so the findings should be interpreted as indicating a strong positive effect on efficiency-related outcomes via increased cooperation rates and stable cooperative equilibria.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Liu, Z. X., Sun, Q. L., & Chen, Z. Q. (2015). Altruism in multiplayer snowdrift games with threshold and punishment. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Theoretical results show that introducing punishment for defectors in threshold N-person snowdrift games increases the equilibrium fraction of cooperators, especially when punishment is always applied regardless of group success. Stronger punishment and higher cooperation thresholds further increase cooperation. However, the model does not include costs for administering punishment, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding how punishment rules and thresholds affect cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation in threshold public goods-like games, especially when punishment is always applied and is strong. However, because the model assumes costless punishment and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment regime, threshold, and punishment strength affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency in some settings, but the lack of punishment cost and direct efficiency outcomes limits predictive value for efficiency.

- source: Chung, N. N., Chew, L. Y., & Lai, C. H. (2013). Influence of network structure on cooperative dynamics in coupled socio-ecological systems. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study extends a previous analytical model of social sanction (ostracism) in common pool resource extraction to populations with complex network structures. It finds that, contrary to some prior work, cooperation and resource sustainability are promoted by higher network connectivity when social sanction is present. In networks with fewer social ties, defectors can cluster and avoid sanction, leading to lower cooperation and lower equilibrium resource levels. The model shows that the equilibrium resource level (a proxy for group efficiency) is higher in well-mixed or highly connected networks, and lower in sparsely connected ones. The findings are robust across different network topologies (random, scale-free, community-structured) and network sizes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public-goods-like common pool resource games with social sanction (ostracism), increasing the average number of social ties (network degree) increases the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and sustaining higher resource levels (efficiency). For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, all else equal, games with higher player connectivity or more opportunities for sanctioning will see a greater positive effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper does not provide direct experimental data, but its results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in networked or structured populations.

- source: Janssen, M. A. (2015). A behavioral perspective on the governance of common resources. *Current Opinion in Environmental Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: chat
  findings: The paper finds that, contrary to the predictions of standard economic theory, groups can achieve higher efficiency and avoid the tragedy of the commons when allowed to communicate or implement costly punishment. Both lab and field experiments show that these mechanisms increase group earnings and reduce overuse of resources. The review also notes that centralized sanctioning mechanisms may be more effective and sustainable than peer punishment. However, the effectiveness of punishment and other interventions is context-specific, depending on factors such as group size, communication, trust, and cultural background. The paper also discusses the phenomenon of 'crowding out,' where externally imposed rules can sometimes reduce performance compared to voluntary agreements.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments generally increases group efficiency or earnings, especially when compared to baseline games without punishment or communication. It also highlights that the effect of punishment is moderated by contextual factors such as group size, communication, and the nature of the sanctioning institution (peer vs. centralized). While the review does not provide quantitative estimates, it offers strong qualitative evidence that punishment mechanisms, when available, tend to improve efficiency outcomes, but the magnitude and sustainability of these effects depend on the specific game design and social context.

- source: Tang, T. Z., & Ye, H. (2016). The Blessing of Sexuality: Evolution of Altruism with Mating Preference. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulation results show that altruistic punishment alone does not sustain high levels of cooperation; defectors tend to dominate unless sexual attractiveness (mating preference for altruists) is present. When both punishment and sexual attractiveness are enabled, altruists (cooperators and punishers) become dominant, and the mean altruistic investment approaches its maximum. Variable population size and individual variation in investment further amplify the prevalence of altruism. The model is a close variant of a public goods game, but the main outcomes are population composition and mean investment, not efficiency or group payoff. Thus, the findings are most relevant for predicting the conditions under which punishment can sustain high cooperation, but less directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment, when combined with additional incentives (such as sexual attractiveness or mating preference for altruists), can lead to high levels of cooperation in public-goods-like environments. However, in the absence of such additional incentives, punishment alone may not sustain cooperation. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes from punishment. Instead, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may depend on the presence of complementary mechanisms that reward altruists. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment's positive effect on efficiency is conditional and may be weak or absent without additional incentives.

- source: Zhang, B. Y., & Hofbauer, J. (2016). Quantal response methods for equilibrium selection in 2 x 2 coordination games. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a mini public goods game with punishment, the QRE method selects a cooperative equilibrium if the punishment is sufficiently strong (i.e., if the fine-to-fee ratio is high enough). The selection of cooperation depends on the effectiveness and cost of punishment. For intermediate punishment, cooperation can dominate for some parameter values even if the limiting QRE is defection. The results are derived analytically and illustrated with theoretical examples, but no empirical or simulated efficiency or payoff data are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical conditions for when punishment will lead to the selection of a cooperative equilibrium in a 2x2 mini public goods game. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it implies that strong punishment can shift equilibrium selection from defection to cooperation, which would be expected to increase efficiency in a public goods context. The results are most directly relevant for games with two players, binary choices, and explicit punishment options, and can inform predictions about the qualitative effect of punishment strength and cost on equilibrium selection, but not quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Van Cleve, J. (2016). Cooperation, conformity, and the coevolutionary problem of trait associations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the coevolution of cooperation, punishment, and conformist bias in a metapopulation model requires a strong initial within-group association (high F_ST) between these traits. If this threshold is not met, cooperation and punishment do not evolve to high frequency, and conformist bias does not stabilize cooperation. The threshold is around F_ST = 0.2 for large groups. The model assumes a public-goods-type group interaction with costly cooperation and punishment, and group competition is based on accumulated resources (which are higher with more cooperation). However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, focusing instead on the evolutionary dynamics of the traits.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (and thus, by implication, efficiency) in public-goods-like environments depends critically on initial population structure (within-group association). In well-mixed or unstructured populations, punishment may not evolve or persist, and thus may not improve efficiency. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in a controlled game setting. Its findings are most relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment can support cooperation in evolutionary or long-run settings, rather than for predicting short-run efficiency changes in experimental games.

- source: Vasconcelos, V. V., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2013). A bottom-up institutional approach to cooperative governance of risky commons. *Nature Climate Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that local, group-level sanctioning institutions are much more effective than global, population-level institutions at promoting successful collective action in a threshold public goods game with risk of collective loss. Local institutions increase the fraction of groups that reach the public good threshold (group achievement), especially when the perceived risk of failure is low. Global institutions are unstable and provide only marginal improvements. The effect is stronger in smaller groups. The model does not report efficiency or total payoff, but the results strongly suggest that local punishment institutions increase the likelihood of group success, which is closely related to efficiency in this context.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling local, group-level punishment institutions in threshold public goods games (with risk of collective loss) will increase the rate of successful group outcomes compared to no punishment or global punishment. While the model does not report efficiency or total payoff, the 'group achievement' outcome is a close proxy for efficiency in threshold settings. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency will be more positive when punishment is local, groups are small, and risk perception is low. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, so its value is mainly in qualitative prediction and mechanism understanding.

- source: Sugiarto, H. S., Chung, N. N., Lai, C. H., & Chew, L. Y. (2015). Socioecological regime shifts in the setting of complex social interactions. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that social ostracism (punishment) can maintain cooperation in a common-pool resource setting, but the resilience of cooperation and the occurrence of regime shifts depend strongly on the structure of the social network. Higher network degree (more social ties) increases the width of the hysteresis curve, making the system more resilient to regime shifts (sudden collapse of cooperation and resources). In sparse networks, regime shifts occur more easily and hysteresis is reduced or absent. The model shows that both social and ecological parameters (e.g., ostracism strength, opportunity cost, resource inflow) can drive regime shifts. However, the main outcomes are the fraction of cooperators and resource levels, not direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that punishment (ostracism) can stabilize cooperation in a public-goods-like environment, and that the effect of punishment is moderated by network structure (player connectivity). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective at maintaining cooperation (and thus, by implication, higher group welfare) in denser networks, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its predictive value for the target outcome.

- source: Xu, M., Zheng, D. F., Xu, C., Zhong, L. X., & Hui, P. M. (2015). Cooperative behavior in N-person evolutionary snowdrift games with punishment. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing costly punishment into an N-person snowdrift game (a close variant of the public goods game) can suppress non-cooperators and promote a cooperative population, but only under certain conditions: low cost-to-benefit ratio, large group size, and strong punishment effectiveness. In these cases, the system reaches a 'frozen' phase with only cooperators and punishers. If the punishment is weak, the cost-to-benefit ratio is high, or the group size is small, punishers are eliminated and the system remains a mix of cooperators and non-cooperators. The model is analyzed using replicator dynamics and confirmed by simulation. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the elimination of non-cooperators implies higher group performance in those parameter regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in N-person public-goods-like games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the steady-state elimination of non-cooperators in the presence of strong punishment, large group size, and low cost-to-benefit ratio suggests that efficiency would be higher in these regimes when punishment is enabled. Conversely, when punishment is weak, costly, or the group is small, punishment does not improve cooperation and likely does not improve efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings where the game structure is similar to the N-person snowdrift game (cost shared among cooperators, task completed if at least one cooperator exists), which is close but not identical to the standard public goods game.

- source: Johnson, S. (2015). Escaping the tragedy of the commons through targeted punishment. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in large-N social dilemmas (modeled after public goods games), targeted punishment strategies—where only a small, strategically chosen subset of defectors are punished at a time—can enable a small number of punishers to shift a population from full defection to full cooperation. This is in contrast to standard punishment strategies, which require much higher punishment capacity to achieve the same result. The main outcome is the stationary proportion of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The model is robust to heterogeneity in player predispositions and to some randomness in the ordering of players. The findings are relevant for understanding how to design punishment institutions or strategies to escape the tragedy of the commons, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the structure and targeting of punishment can strongly affect the ability to achieve cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when initial cooperation is low. However, because the model's outcome is the proportion of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. The results suggest that, all else equal, targeted punishment strategies may be more effective at increasing cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) than uniform punishment, but the lack of direct payoff analysis limits quantitative prediction. The model assumes binary (all-or-nothing) contribution choices, no explicit payoff or MPCR parameters, and does not model communication, reward, or information features. Thus, its main value is in highlighting the importance of punishment targeting and heterogeneity, rather than providing parameter-specific efficiency predictions.

- source: Teraji, S. (2016). Why Do People Obey Norms?. *Evolving Norms: Cognitive Perspectives in Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper presents theoretical arguments and formal models showing that costly punishment (including decentralized, altruistic punishment) can sustain norm compliance and cooperation in social dilemmas similar to public goods games. It also models the interaction between punishment and reputation as mechanisms for norm enforcement. The models predict multiple possible equilibria (high or low compliance/cooperation), with path dependence based on initial conditions. While the models are closely related to public goods games, the main outcomes are norm compliance and cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff. The models do not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in public-goods-like environments can increase norm compliance and cooperation, especially when reputation effects are weak. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper suggests that punishment is likely to increase cooperation and norm compliance, which may translate into higher efficiency, but the models do not quantify this effect. The paper is most useful for understanding the mechanisms and conditions under which punishment sustains cooperation, rather than for making direct efficiency predictions.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Bshary, R. (2015). The reputation of punishers. *Trends in Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the reputation consequences of punishment are context-dependent. In forced play games where punishment is possible, a punitive reputation can induce fear and stabilize cooperation, potentially supporting group efficiency. However, when partner choice is available, individuals with a nonpunishing cooperative reputation are typically preferred over punishers, unless punishment is the only available signal of cooperative intent. The efficiency of punishment (fee:fine ratio) also moderates reputational outcomes: 'efficient' punishment (fee:fine > 1) may be viewed as self-serving, while 'inefficient' punishment (fee:fine < 1) is more likely to be seen as genuinely norm-enforcing. The paper synthesizes prior empirical and theoretical work but does not provide new data or direct estimates of efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is most useful for identifying moderators and mechanisms that may affect the impact of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, efficiency in public goods games and similar environments. It highlights the importance of partner choice, the efficiency of punishment, and the availability of alternative signals of cooperation. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence on efficiency outcomes, so its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is primarily in identifying which dimensions are likely to be important moderators (e.g., partner choice, punishment efficiency, information structure).

- source: Declerck, C. H., Boone, C., & Emonds, G. (2013). When do people cooperate? The neuroeconomics of prosocial decision making. *Brain and Cognition*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper proposes a dual-process neural framework for prosocial decision-making in social dilemmas, including public goods games and related paradigms. It reviews evidence that extrinsic incentives (such as punishment) and trust signals independently increase cooperation. Neuroimaging studies show that punishment threats activate cognitive control regions and increase cooperative behavior, particularly among self-regarding or Machiavellian individuals. The review also highlights that individual differences (e.g., social value orientation, Machiavellianism) moderate the impact of punishment and incentives on cooperation. However, the paper does not directly report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and neural responses.
  prediction_guidance: This review provides strong theoretical and mechanistic support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation in public-goods-game-like environments, especially for self-regarding individuals. It suggests that the effect of punishment is likely to be more pronounced when extrinsic incentives are salient and among participants with self-regarding preferences. However, because the review does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its value for prediction is primarily in identifying moderators (e.g., individual differences, presence of incentives, trust signals) and in supporting the general mechanism by which punishment increases cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used to inform the likely direction and moderators of punishment effects, but not to calibrate the magnitude of efficiency changes.

- source: Simpson, B., & Willer, R. (2015). Beyond Altruism: Sociological Foundations of Cooperation and Prosocial Behavior. *Annual Review of Sociology, Vol 41*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper reviews sociological mechanisms that promote cooperation in social dilemmas, including public goods games. It finds that both formal (centralized) and informal (peer) punishment can increase cooperation, but the welfare effects are ambiguous due to the costs of sanctioning and potential for over- or under-punishment. Communication and endogenous rule formation improve outcomes. Centralized punishment (e.g., by elected leaders) is more effective and less prone to retaliation than peer punishment. However, the presence of sanctions and extrinsic incentives can undermine intrinsic motivation and trust, leading to lower cooperation when such mechanisms are removed. Reputation and status also promote cooperation, but can introduce ambiguity about motivations. Network structure (e.g., density, centrality, dynamic ties) shapes cooperation, with denser and more dynamic networks generally supporting higher cooperation, but also potentially undermining generalized trust.
  prediction_guidance: This review provides high-level theoretical and empirical synthesis relevant to predicting the effects of punishment in public goods games and similar environments. It supports the general finding that punishment increases cooperation, but cautions that the net effect on efficiency or group welfare depends on the costs and structure of punishment, the presence of communication, and whether enforcement is centralized or peer-based. The review suggests that communication and endogenous institution formation are important moderators, and that centralized punishment is more effective than decentralized peer punishment. However, it also warns that punishment can undermine intrinsic motivation and trust, potentially reducing efficiency in the long run. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent and moderated by game design features such as communication, institution formation, and enforcement structure, but does not provide quantitative estimates or direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Van Lange, P. A. M., Joireman, J., Parks, C. D., & Van Dijk, E. (2013). The psychology of social dilemmas: A review. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The review concludes that both punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemmas, including public goods games, but their effectiveness depends on factors such as trust, group size, decentralization, and cultural context. The paper notes that punishment can sometimes have adverse effects, such as reducing cooperation if sanctions are low or undermining intrinsic motivation. It also discusses the importance of communication, reputation, and structural solutions (e.g., leadership, local management) in sustaining cooperation. While the review references meta-analyses and empirical studies showing positive effects of punishment and reward on cooperation, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical and conceptual support for the idea that punishment (and reward) mechanisms can increase cooperation in public goods and related social dilemmas, and that their effects are moderated by structural and psychological factors. However, it does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze the impact of specific game design dimensions. For prediction tasks, this review supports the general expectation that enabling punishment will tend to increase cooperation, but the lack of direct efficiency data limits its value for precise quantitative prediction. The review is most useful for identifying moderators and mechanisms that may inform the interpretation of empirical results from other studies.

- source: Buchholz, W., Falkinger, J., & Rübbelke, D. (2014). NON-GOVERNMENTAL PUBLIC NORM ENFORCEMENT IN LARGE SOCIETIES AS A TWO-STAGE GAME OF VOLUNTARY PUBLIC GOOD PROVISION. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that voluntary contributions to a nongovernmental enforcement fund can sustain norm enforcement and increase public good provision (efficiency) in large societies, even when individuals are self-interested and there is no government. The positive effect of punishment/reward-based enforcement on efficiency is robust to increases in population size, provided the enforcement technology is nonrival. With rival enforcement, the positive effect diminishes as the population grows, but still remains above the no-enforcement baseline. The model also shows that the equilibrium level of public good provision is always below the first-best (Pareto-optimal) level, but is strictly higher than in the absence of enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment/reward mechanisms (via a funded enforcement agency) increases efficiency (public good provision) relative to the baseline voluntary contribution game without enforcement, especially when enforcement is nonrival. The model offers explicit comparative statics for how efficiency gains from punishment depend on player count, enforcement technology, and other design dimensions. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that the presence and effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms are key moderators of efficiency, and that the positive effect of punishment is robust to increases in group size if enforcement is nonrival, but attenuates with rival enforcement. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in PGG-like environments with norm enforcement.

- source: Griffin, C., & Belmonte, A. (2017). Cyclic public goods games: Compensated coexistence among mutual cheaters stabilized by optimized penalty taxation. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a cyclic public goods game with three species, the system without penalty taxation is unstable and leads to the dominance of a single species, with the interior coexistence point being a point of minimal total population growth (i.e., lowest efficiency). Imposing a penalty tax (punishment) on population growth can stabilize the interior fixed point and allow for coexistence of all species, but this tax is not redistributed and thus reduces overall efficiency further. The optimal tax is the minimal one that achieves stability, but any positive tax reduces total population growth below the already minimal level at the coexistence point. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include empirical or simulation data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in a cyclic public goods game with three types of mutual cheaters, introducing a penalty tax (punishment) can stabilize coexistence but always reduces total population growth (efficiency) compared to the no-punishment case. The penalty is not redistributed, so it is a pure cost. Thus, in similar game designs, enabling punishment (as a non-redistributive tax) is predicted to decrease efficiency relative to the control. The findings are most relevant for settings with cyclic/multispecies structure and non-redistributive punishment, and less so for standard two-strategy PGGs or for punishment that is redistributed. The results are directly informative for predicting the direction of efficiency change (less positive) when enabling punishment in such models.

- source: Asgharpourmasouleh, A., Sadeghi, A., & Yousofi, A. (2017). A Grounded Agent-Based Model of Common Good Production in a Residential Complex: Applying Artificial Experiments. *Sage Open*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The agent-based model simulates a common good game in a residential complex, with agent strategies grounded in qualitative fieldwork. The model shows that in blocks with more defectors, common good production and payoffs collapse. Artificial experiments demonstrate that (1) introducing punishers in place of defectors, (2) increasing all agents' desirability for the common good, and (3) combining both interventions, all increase common good production and total payoffs. The most dramatic improvement occurs when both punishers are introduced and desirability is maximized, resulting in all agents adopting punishing strategies and achieving the highest payoffs and common good production. Thus, punishment mechanisms, especially when agents value the common good, can transform a low-efficiency, low-payoff equilibrium into a high-efficiency, high-payoff one.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (and increasing the number of punishers) in a public-goods-like environment can increase efficiency and group payoffs, especially when agents also value the common good. The model is not a standard PGG but is structurally similar, with continuous contributions, punishment, and payoff functions analogous to PGGs. The results suggest that, all else equal, introducing punishment will increase efficiency, particularly in settings where baseline cooperation is fragile or low. The effect is amplified if agents' intrinsic motivation (desirability) for the common good is high. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low.

- source: Powers, S. T., & Lehmann, L. (2017). When is bigger better? The effects of group size on the evolution of helping behaviours. *Biological Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that as group size increases, the marginal benefits of helping, the effectiveness of reciprocity, and relatedness all tend to decrease, making cooperation less likely to evolve or be stable in large groups. Punishment can stabilize cooperation, but its effectiveness also declines with group size unless institutional mechanisms are in place. The authors argue that human societies have overcome these limitations through the evolution of institutions that coordinate sanctioning and information sharing, thereby enabling large-scale cooperation. The paper provides a conceptual framework for understanding how game design features (such as group size, punishment, and information structure) interact to affect the evolution and maintenance of cooperation, but does not provide quantitative predictions or new data on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding the qualitative effects of group size and institutional punishment on cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that, all else equal, increasing group size will tend to reduce the effectiveness of both reciprocity and punishment in sustaining cooperation, unless institutional mechanisms are present to coordinate sanctioning and information. The paper highlights the importance of considering diminishing marginal returns, monitoring costs, and second-order free-riding in large groups. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment will be less effective at increasing efficiency in larger groups unless institutional features (e.g., centralized punishment, coordinated monitoring) are present. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates or experimental data for efficiency outcomes, so its support is primarily conceptual and mechanistic.

- source: Schlüter, M., Tavoni, A., & Levin, S. (2016). Robustness of norm-driven cooperation in the commons. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent

  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that cooperation enforced by social sanctioning is robust under certain conditions but can collapse with increased resource abundance or small increases in resource variability. High resource variability or scarcity can promote cooperation. The effectiveness of sanctioning depends on the proportion of norm followers (social capital). When two groups are ecologically connected, resource leakage from a more cooperative group can destabilize cooperation in the less cooperative group. The model highlights the complex, nonlinear interplay between social and ecological factors in sustaining cooperation. However, the main outcome is the level of cooperation, not explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effects of punishment-like mechanisms (here, non-costly social disapproval) on cooperation in CPR/public goods settings, especially under varying resource conditions. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions used in the prediction task. The model's focus on non-costly, endogenous social sanctioning differs from standard experimental punishment treatments. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited and mainly contextual/mechanistic.

It suggests that punishment can increase cooperation, but may also have negative side effects. - source: Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2017). Helping in humans and other animals: a fruitful interdisciplinary dialogue. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that mechanisms such as punishment and reward, studied extensively in human public goods games, are also relevant in non-human animal cooperation. It reviews how laboratory studies (often using public goods or n-player social dilemma games) have inspired comparative research, and how features like punishment, partner choice, and communication can affect cooperation rates. The authors emphasize the need for more ecologically valid experiments and for understanding how laboratory findings translate to real-world helping and cooperation. The review notes that while punishment and reward can increase cooperation in public goods games, most evidence is about behavioral outcomes, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for how punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in public-goods-like games, but does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency effects. It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and moderators that may influence the effect of punishment on cooperation, but cannot be used to predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of ecological validity, the role of communication, and the need to consider real-world payoff structures.

- source: Wu, B., García, J., Hauert, C., & Traulsen, A. (2013). Extrapolating Weak Selection in Evolutionary Games. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
