When individuals have only coarse knowledge (e.g., know only the average neighborhood size), it is easier to sustain full cooperation, as targeting is not possible. The minimum discount factor required to sustain cooperation is lower under coarse knowledge than under precise knowledge, making full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) more robust. The model thus predicts that limited network knowledge facilitates the enforcement of cooperation through punishment, leading to higher group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining high efficiency in repeated social dilemmas depends not only on the presence of punishment but also on the information structure—specifically, the precision of network knowledge. In environments where individuals have only coarse knowledge of the network, punishment is more effective at sustaining full cooperation and thus maximizing efficiency. If a public goods game or similar environment allows only limited information about the network or about others' positions, the model predicts a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency. Conversely, if players have precise knowledge of the network, the positive effect of punishment on efficiency may be reduced. The paper does not provide direct empirical estimates but offers clear theoretical mechanisms and comparative statics relevant to prediction.

- source: Nasrallah, W. F., & Cheaib, K. A. (2016). An equilibrium model of how regulative and normative institutions influence micro-economic and organizational behavior. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The theta-pi model demonstrates that both punishment (regulative institutions) and empathy (normative institutions) can increase the efficiency of economic interactions by expanding the set of transactions that are mutually beneficial and deterring predation. The model provides explicit conditions under which markets are efficient (all cooperate), collapse (all defect or abstain), or are path-dependent/oscillatory. Punishment (pi) is especially effective in high-temptation, low-sucker-loss environments, while empathy (theta) is more effective when losses from being cheated are high. When some actors are exempt from punishment (elites), efficiency declines and can collapse if the exempt group is too large. Overlapping institutions and partial coverage can lead to complex dynamics, including cycles of cooperation and predation. The model quantifies the marginal value of increasing institutional strength and provides phase diagrams for different transaction types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (increasing pi) in a public-goods-like environment will increase efficiency, especially when the temptation to defect is high and the cost to victims is not overwhelming. The model clarifies that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its universality and the structure of payoffs, and that partial or selective enforcement can undermine efficiency. The findings suggest that, all else equal, increasing the probability or effectiveness of punishment will shift the equilibrium from market collapse or low efficiency to high efficiency, provided the institutional coverage is broad. The model also highlights that empathy-based mechanisms can substitute for punishment in some environments, but are less effective when temptation is high and losses are low. For prediction tasks, the model's phase diagrams and threshold conditions can be mapped to game design dimensions such as punishment cost, effectiveness, and universality, supporting the expectation that enabling punishment will generally increase efficiency in public-goods-like games.

- source: Wubs, M., Bshary, R., & Lehmann, L. (2016). Coevolution between positive reciprocity, punishment, and partner switching in repeated interactions. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, the evolutionary success of punishment, positive reciprocity, and partner switching depends on the number of rounds, group size, and the costs/effectiveness of punishment and switching. Punishment is favored in small and moderately sized groups, especially when punishment is efficient and its behavioral effect is long-lasting. Partner switching dominates in large groups or when the number of rounds is high, unless switching is costly. Positive reciprocity is rarely the dominant mechanism, except when punishment is less effective or more costly. The model does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but evolutionary success is determined by aggregate payoff, so the results indirectly inform about conditions under which punishment or other mechanisms would lead to higher average payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like environments. It suggests that punishment is more likely to improve group outcomes (in terms of evolutionary success/payoff) in small or moderately sized groups, especially when punishment is efficient and its behavioral effect is long-lasting. In large groups or with many rounds, partner switching may be more effective. The findings are most relevant for adjacent settings to public goods games, especially repeated dyadic interactions, and should be interpreted with caution when generalizing to standard PGGs. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, but the evolutionary dominance of strategies is determined by payoff, so the results can be used to infer the likely direction of punishment's effect on efficiency under different design dimensions.

- source: Patrzyk, P. M., & Takác, M. (2017). Cooperation Via Intimidation: An Emergent System of Mutual Threats can Maintain Social Order. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in agent-based models of repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with post-game fighting (punishment), aggressive strategies (harassers) become evolutionarily stable and promote high rates of mutual cooperation (CC) in small groups with strong deterrence. The mechanism is that agents develop reputations for toughness, deterring defection and reducing the need for actual fighting. In large groups, anonymity undermines this effect, and cooperation rates fall. When third-party (moralized) punishment is allowed, coalitions can enforce cooperation, but only under certain conditions (small groups, justified defection rules). The models suggest that punishment can increase group-level cooperation and, by proxy, efficiency, but only under specific structural conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that enabling punishment in repeated PD-like environments can increase cooperation rates (and thus efficiency) compared to control, especially in small groups with strong deterrence and information spread. The effect is weaker or absent in large, anonymous groups. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is reputation-based and where agents can observe and remember others' aggressive acts. The results are not from public goods games but from repeated PDs with post-game punishment, so transfer to PGGs should be cautious but is likely informative for adjacent settings. The paper does not report efficiency as a direct ratio of group payoff to maximum, but CC rates can be used as a proxy for high group welfare.

- source: Han, T. A., & Lenaerts, T. (2016). A synergy of costly punishment and commitment in cooperation dilemmas. *Adaptive Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that a strategy combining prior commitment and costly punishment (CPP) can achieve higher frequencies of cooperation than either mechanism alone, across a wide range of parameter values. The improvement is most significant when the cost of arranging commitments is high and the punishment impact is above a threshold. The model shows that commitment reduces the effect-to-cost ratio required for punishment to be effective, and punishment helps deal with free-riders who exploit commitment. The results are robust across different cost and punishment parameters, but are derived for the two-player, one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma, not for public goods games or group interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that combining commitment and punishment mechanisms can outperform either alone in promoting cooperation, especially when commitment is costly and punishment is sufficiently effective. However, the model is based on the two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game or group setting. The outcomes are reported as frequencies of strategies and risk-dominance, not as group efficiency or total payoff. Thus, while the findings suggest that punishment (especially when combined with commitment) can improve cooperation and, by implication, group outcomes, the results cannot be directly mapped to efficiency predictions in public goods games. The paper is most useful for understanding the interaction of punishment and commitment mechanisms and their parameter dependencies, but less directly informative for quantitative efficiency prediction in PGG-like environments.

- source: Zhu, Y. Y., Zhang, J. L., Sun, Q. L., & Chen, Z. Q. (2017). Evolutionary dynamics of strategies for threshold snowdrift games on complex networks. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment in threshold snowdrift games increases the evolutionary advantage of cooperation over defection, especially when punishment cost is low and the threshold is moderate. Larger thresholds generally promote cooperation, but too high or too low thresholds can be detrimental. Lower punishment cost (to the punisher) and higher punishment impact (on the defector) both facilitate cooperation. These effects are robust across well-mixed and networked populations, including single-layer and double-layer (interconnected) networks. However, the main reported outcomes are cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase cooperation rates in threshold public-goods-like games, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. Theoretical payoff difference (P_c - P_d) is analyzed, but simulation results focus on cooperation rates. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective when the cost to punishers is low and the penalty to defectors is high, and that network structure and threshold size moderate these effects. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment can improve cooperation under certain parameter regimes, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency.

- source: Cui, P. B., & Wu, Z. X. (2014). Selfish punishment with avoiding mechanism can alleviate both first-order and second-order social dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that introducing selfish punishment with an avoiding mechanism into a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can alleviate both first-order and second-order social dilemmas, depending on the punishment cost, fine, and identification probability. The presence of selfish punishers allows for the stable coexistence of cooperators and punishers, and under certain parameter regimes, reduces the prevalence of defectors and second-order free riders. Theoretical predictions using extended pair approximation and interaction web analysis match well with simulation results. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy type, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (especially when including mechanisms for selfish punishment and avoidance) can promote cooperation and reduce free riding in spatially structured social dilemmas. However, because the model is a prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and the main outcomes are strategy frequencies rather than group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment depends on the cost/fine ratio and the ability to identify defectors, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and qualitative moderators of punishment effects, rather than for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Roos, P., Gelfand, M., Nau, D., & Carr, R. (2014). High strength-of-ties and low mobility enable the evolution of third-party punishment. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that responsible third-party punishment (3PP) can evolve and sustain cooperation in structured populations when social-structural constraints are high (i.e., high strength-of-ties and low mobility). Under these conditions, clusters of responsible punishers can induce cooperation in their local neighborhoods, leading to higher local pay-offs and the spread of both cooperation and punishment. In contrast, in well-mixed or highly mobile populations, responsible 3PP does not evolve, and cooperation fails to emerge. The model shows that 3PP is pivotal for the emergence of cooperation and increased pay-off, as neither population structure alone nor direct punishment alone suffice. The findings are robust to variations in network structure and to the presence of antisocial punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and increasing pay-off (and thus efficiency) depends critically on the social structure of the population. Specifically, punishment is more likely to increase efficiency in games with high strength-of-ties (low average degree, i.e., players interact repeatedly with a small set of neighbors) and low mobility (players do not frequently change partners). In well-mixed or highly mobile settings, punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that game designs with fixed, repeated local interactions and low partner switching will see greater efficiency gains from punishment mechanisms, while designs with random matching or high mobility will not. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it uses a two-player cooperation game with third-party punishment), so the transfer to standard PGGs is not exact but likely informative.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., Nie, S. L., & Wang, L. (2015). Promote or hinder? The role of punishment in the emergence of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends critically on the pattern of punishment and the presence of anti-social punishment. Centralized (pool) pro-social punishment is more effective than decentralized (peer) punishment at promoting cooperation. When anti-social punishment is possible and both pro- and anti-social punishments are in the same pattern, the positive effect of punishment is largely neutralized. However, if pro-social punishment is centralized and anti-social punishment is decentralized, cooperation can still be promoted. The results are derived from theoretical evolutionary game models and focus on the stationary abundance of strategies, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative theoretical evidence about how punishment design (centralized vs decentralized, presence of anti-social punishment) can affect the evolutionary success of cooperation in social dilemmas. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that centralized punishment mechanisms are more robust to anti-social punishment and more likely to sustain cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that games with centralized punishment (pool) are more likely to see positive effects of punishment on efficiency, especially when anti-social punishment is possible. However, the results are based on evolutionary dynamics and strategy abundance, not direct efficiency measures, and the model is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2015). Emergence of parochial altruism in well-mixed populations. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that parochial altruism (in-group cooperation, out-group punishment) is favored by selection under certain parameter regimes, especially when the benefit of cooperation is high relative to its cost, and when the effectiveness of punishment (fine imposed) exceeds its cost. Intergroup conflict (punishment) can promote the evolution of in-group cooperation, and vice versa. High mutation rates are detrimental to the evolution of parochial altruism. Larger populations and intermediate selection strengths can also favor parochial altruism. The model provides analytical conditions for when parochial altruism is favored, but does not directly report on group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, by showing that punishment (when effective and not too costly) can promote the evolutionary success of parochial altruism (in-group cooperation and out-group punishment). However, the model is not a standard public goods game, but rather a two-group model with prisoner's dilemma for in-group and a punishment game for out-group interactions. The main outcomes are evolutionary frequencies of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under certain conditions, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency changes in standard PGGs. The findings are most relevant for understanding the parameter regimes where punishment is likely to support cooperative strategies, which may inform priors about efficiency effects in related experimental designs.

- source: Helbing, D., Yu, W. J., Opp, K. D., & Rauhut, H. (2014). Conditions for the Emergence of Shared Norms in Populations with Incompatible Preferences. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment (sanctioning) is a powerful mechanism for establishing shared norms in heterogeneous populations, even when preferences are incompatible. Behavior-based punishment is more effective than preference-based punishment in creating conformity. However, the resulting norm is not always efficient: sometimes a minority can establish an unpopular or suboptimal norm, and average payoff may not be maximized. Adaptive group pressure (where local majorities punish minorities) can lead to spontaneous norm emergence, but again, the content of the norm may be arbitrary and not welfare-maximizing. The model highlights the importance of path dependence, local interaction, and randomness in norm formation. Payoff (welfare) is explicitly tracked, and the model demonstrates that sanctioning can both increase and decrease average payoff depending on the scenario.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (payoff) in public-goods-like environments is highly context-dependent. Punishment can increase conformity and create shared norms, but does not guarantee higher efficiency: the emergent norm may be suboptimal. The model suggests that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its cost, effectiveness, and whether it is behavior- or preference-based. Local interaction and adaptive group pressure can facilitate norm emergence, but do not ensure welfare maximization. For prediction, this paper suggests that enabling punishment will often increase conformity but may have mixed or even negative effects on efficiency, especially if the norm that emerges is not aligned with group welfare. The model's adjacent structure to PGGs means its findings are suggestive but not directly quantitative for standard PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Boyd, R., & Mathew, S. (2015). Third-party monitoring and sanctions aid the evolution of language. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper develops a repeated signaling game (a variant of the Sir Philip Sidney game) to model the evolution of honest communication. It finds that when only direct reciprocity is possible and lies are hard to detect, honest signaling is not evolutionarily stable except in special cases. However, when third-party monitoring and punishment are introduced—so that multiple community members can observe, aggregate evidence, and sanction dishonesty—the range of parameters supporting honest signaling expands greatly. This is because third-party monitoring increases the probability of detecting lies and reduces false accusations, making it more likely that group-beneficial honest signaling (analogous to high efficiency) is maintained.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that adding third-party monitoring and punishment mechanisms to repeated social dilemmas (even when not strictly public goods games) can substantially increase the likelihood of group-beneficial outcomes, such as honest signaling or cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence of third-party punishment and monitoring should be expected to have a strongly positive effect on efficiency or group payoff, especially when direct detection of defection is difficult. The model is not a standard PGG, but the logic is closely related and the results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments where monitoring and punishment are possible.

- source: Larson, J. M. (2017). Networks and Interethnic Cooperation. *Journal of Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of groups to enforce cooperation through peer punishment depends critically on the structure of their internal communication networks. Specifically, the 'reach' or 'integration' of the network—how widely and quickly information about defections can spread—determines how easily cooperation can be sustained. Denser networks are not necessarily better; networks with high integration (no highly peripheral or bottleneck nodes) are optimal for supporting cooperation. The model also shows that there is an upper bound on the proportion of interethnic (cross-group) interactions that can be kept cooperative, determined by the network's reach. Improvements in communication speed, longer punishment phases, and more integrated networks all make cooperation easier to sustain. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, but rather the theoretical conditions for sustaining cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how network structure moderates the effectiveness of peer punishment in sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (and thus the likely efficiency gain from enabling punishment) depends not on network density but on integration/reach. However, the model is not a public goods game per se, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency. It is most useful for qualitative reasoning about the importance of network structure (especially communication reach) as a moderator of punishment's effect on cooperation.

- source: Morris, A., MacGlashan, J., Littman, M. L., & Cushman, F. (2017). Evolution of flexibility and rigidity in retaliatory punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that when punishment is costly, flexible (learning-based) victims are less likely to learn to punish, making them more vulnerable in repeated interactions. This leads to the evolution of rigid punishment strategies (i.e., always punish theft), even when such punishment is costly and not always effective. The learning dynamics of reinforcement learning agents show that thieves are more likely to adapt flexibly, while victims tend to persist in punishment. The evolutionary simulations confirm that the cost of punishment biases selection toward rigid punishment. The findings are primarily about the evolution and persistence of punishment strategies, not about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence about the evolution of punishment strategies in repeated dyadic games, focusing on the interplay between learning dynamics and evolutionary selection. While it does not directly address efficiency or group payoff in public goods games, it is informative about when rigid punishment is likely to evolve and persist, especially when punishment is costly. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper suggests that the cost of punishment and the learning dynamics of participants may influence the prevalence of rigid punishment, but it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency outcomes. Its relevance is mainly in understanding the conditions under which punishment is likely to be rigid or flexible, which may indirectly affect efficiency in related environments.

- source: Larson, J. M. (2017). WHY THE WEST BECAME WILD Informal Governance with Incomplete Networks. *World Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of peer punishment to sustain cooperation depends critically on the social network structure and the reach of gossip. Incomplete networks with peripheral members cannot sustain full cooperation: the most peripheral members are likely to be persistently cheated and ostracized, as their negative gossip does not reach enough punishers to deter defection. Shocks that increase the gains from defection or that create more peripheral positions (e.g., through rapid population growth or enclave settlement) lead to a ratcheting down of cooperation, with more members becoming persistent cheaters. The model generates hypotheses about when and where cooperation will break down, but does not provide quantitative predictions about group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how network structure and information transmission affect the limits of peer punishment in sustaining cooperation. It is relevant for understanding moderators of punishment effectiveness, especially the role of network incompleteness and peripheral positions. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that punishment will be less effective at increasing efficiency in settings with incomplete networks, high population turnover, or enclave settlement, but does not provide parameterized estimates or control-treatment efficiency comparisons.

- source: Wang, R. Y., & Ng, C. N. (2015). Can Centralized Sanctioning Promote Trust in Social Dilemmas? A Two-Level Trust Game with Incomplete Information. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a centralized sanctioning authority into a repeated trust game with incomplete information can increase trust and reciprocity, especially when actors believe the authority is likely to punish defectors. The model shows that the threat of centralized punishment relaxes the conditions under which trust is placed and honored, compared to the baseline trust game without an authority. The effect is strongest when there is optimism about the authority's willingness to punish. The model predicts that centralized sanctioning can mitigate the end-game effect (decline in cooperation in final rounds). However, the analysis is theoretical and does not provide explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that adding a centralized punishment mechanism to a trust-based social dilemma can increase trust and cooperation, especially under incomplete information. While the model is not a public goods game, it is adjacent and relevant for predicting the effect of centralized punishment in repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling centralized punishment is likely to increase cooperation and, by implication, group payoffs or efficiency, particularly when the punishment is sufficiently strong and actors believe the authority is likely to punish. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency or group payoff, so its value for direct efficiency prediction is limited to qualitative inference.

- source: Berenji, B., Chou, T., & D'Orsogna, M. R. (2014). Recidivism and Rehabilitation of Criminal Offenders: A Carrot and Stick Evolutionary Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The model demonstrates that neither punishment alone nor rehabilitation alone is optimal for reducing crime and recidivism. Instead, the best outcomes (highest ratio of reformed to unreformable individuals) are achieved by balancing punishment severity and rehabilitation resources, subject to a fixed total resource constraint. There is an optimal allocation of resources between punishment and rehabilitation, and both too little and too much of either can worsen outcomes. The model also shows that the duration and intensity of rehabilitation matter, with intermediate durations being most effective. These findings are robust across different initial conditions and parameter values. However, the model does not report efficiency or group payoff in the sense of public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. It supports the idea that the effect of punishment on group-level outcomes (here, the ratio of reformed to unreformable individuals) depends on the balance between punishment and rehabilitation, and that both are needed for optimal outcomes. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct predictive value for efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The main implication is that punishment's effect is non-monotonic and context-dependent, especially when resources are constrained and when rehabilitation is possible. The findings suggest that in analogous PGG settings, enabling punishment without considering complementary positive interventions or resource tradeoffs may not maximize efficiency.

- source: Huang, Y. K., & Inohara, T. (2015). Group-separations based on the repeated prisoners' dilemma games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper provides a theoretical framework for understanding how the ability to sustain cooperation via punishment (trigger strategies) in repeated pairwise prisoners' dilemma games depends on the discount factor and the players' mixed strategies. It shows that as the discount factor increases, more pairs can sustain cooperation, leading to larger groups of mutually cooperating players. The main findings are the mathematical characterization of the group-separation structure as a function of the discount factor, and the identification of intervals where complete-group-separations (where all within-group pairs can sustain cooperation) are possible. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants, as it does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is theoretical: it shows how the ability to sustain cooperation via punishment in repeated pairwise games depends on the discount factor and the heterogeneity of players' strategies. For prediction tasks, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) is sensitive to the discount factor and the similarity of players' strategies, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency effects. The model is also for pairwise repeated PDs, not for n-player public goods games.

- source: Blonski, M., & Spagnolo, G. (2015). Prisoners' other Dilemma. *International Journal of Game Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the infinitely repeated Prisoner's Dilemma, the riskiness of cooperation equilibria can be precisely characterized by comparing the Nash-products of cooperation and defection equilibria. The least risky cooperation equilibrium is always the grim trigger strategy. There exists a critical discount factor (delta*) above which cooperation can be supported without strategic risk, and below which all cooperation equilibria are risky. The more patient the players (higher discount factor), the more forgiving the punishment strategies can be while still supporting cooperation. The paper also introduces the concept of 'risk perfection', requiring that not only the equilibrium path but also all subgames (punishment phases) are not risky. The results generalize to asymmetric games and to repeated Cournot duopoly (collusion) games, which are structurally similar to public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on the relationship between punishment harshness, forgiveness, and the viability of cooperation in repeated games. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it shows that the ability of punishment to support cooperation depends on the discount factor and the riskiness of the equilibrium. In environments structurally similar to public goods games, the results suggest that harsher punishments (like grim trigger) are less risky and more likely to support cooperation, especially when players are less patient. As patience increases, more forgiving punishments become viable. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency depends on the strategic riskiness of the environment and the patience of players, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Christoforou, E., Anta, A. F., Georgiou, C., Mosteiro, M. A., & Sánchez, A. (2013). Crowd Computing as a Cooperation Problem: An Evolutionary Approach. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment for cheating in a repeated master-worker (principal-agent) setting with reinforcement learning leads to faster convergence to a state where all workers cooperate (i.e., provide correct answers), compared to no punishment. However, punishment is not strictly necessary for eventual correctness if other conditions (such as sufficient reward relative to aspiration and cost) are met. The system is robust to high tolerance for defectors, but as group size increases, high tolerance can reduce the probability of achieving full cooperation. The main outcomes are the probability of cheating and the system's convergence to correctness, not direct measures of group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it shows that punishment reduces the average cheating probability and accelerates convergence to a fully cooperative state. In settings where efficiency is closely tied to the rate of cooperation (e.g., if only correct answers generate group benefit), these results suggest that punishment would increase efficiency, especially in the short-to-medium term. However, the absence of explicit payoff or efficiency data limits the direct applicability of these findings to the prediction task. The effects of group size, tolerance, and aspiration are also informative: higher group size and higher tolerance to defectors can undermine the effectiveness of punishment in achieving full cooperation.

- source: Villatoro, D., Andrighetto, G., Conte, R., & Sabater-Mir, J. (2015). Self-Policing Through Norm Internalization: A Cognitive Solution to the Tragedy of the Digital Commons in Social Networks. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that agents capable of norm internalization (EMIL-I-A) achieve higher levels of cooperation (as measured by successful service transactions) than purely self-interested agents (IUMA) in a digital commons scenario. Internalizers flexibly adapt to environmental changes, internalizing and de-internalizing norms as conditions shift. The presence of punishment and sanction mechanisms supports norm salience and internalization, but the main driver of improved cooperation is internalization rather than external punishment alone. The model demonstrates that norm internalization can enable self-policing and robust cooperation in dynamic, decentralized environments. However, the outcomes are not reported in terms of group efficiency or total payoff as in standard public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of punishment and especially norm internalization mechanisms can increase cooperation and successful collective action in social dilemma-like environments. However, because the scenario is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that when agents internalize norms (potentially supported by punishment/sanction), cooperation is more robust and adaptive, but the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not isolated or quantified. The model's focus on norm salience and internalization may inform predictions about environments where norm-based motivations are strong, but it does not provide direct quantitative guidance for the efficiency impact of punishment in PGGs.

- source: Qu, X. L., Zhou, C. L., Cao, Z. G., & Yang, X. G. (2016). Conditional dissociation as a punishment mechanism in the evolution of cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Conditional dissociation, modeled as a punishment mechanism where players can break up with defectors and must wait before re-entering the game, promotes the evolution of cooperation in an iterated Prisoner's Dilemma. The effect is stronger when the punishment is more severe (longer waiting time) and when the population's lifespan is longer. Allowing mixed strategies (as opposed to only pure strategies) reduces the probability of ending up with a fully cooperative population. The model is clean, excluding other cooperation-promoting mechanisms, and shows that both intolerant cooperators and tolerant defectors can be stable outcomes. The main outcomes are the frequency of cooperative vs. defective strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (conditional dissociation) on cooperation in repeated pairwise social dilemmas. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that punishment mechanisms that impose a cost on defectors (and sometimes on punishers) can promote cooperation, especially when the punishment is more severe and when the game is longer. The absence of direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction, but the results support the general idea that increasing punishment severity (e.g., higher punishment cost or effectiveness) can increase cooperation, though possibly at the expense of overall efficiency if punishers also incur costs. The negative effect of mixed strategies on cooperation may also be relevant for predicting outcomes in more flexible or noisy environments.

- source: Chan, N. W. H., Xu, C., Tey, S. M., Yap, Y. J., & Hui, P. M. (2013). Evolutionary snowdrift game incorporating costly punishment in structured populations. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a costly punishing strategy into the snowdrift game on a square lattice leads to richer population dynamics and phase structures than in well-mixed populations. For certain parameter ranges (low cost-to-benefit ratio r and sufficiently high punishment impact β), punishers can suppress defectors and coexist with cooperators, or even dominate. The spatial structure (lattice) allows for stable coexistence of all three strategies (C, D, P) in some regions, a phenomenon not observed in well-mixed populations. The pair approximation analytic method only qualitatively matches the simulation results and fails to capture some spatially-induced phases. The main reported outcomes are the steady-state frequencies of each strategy, not group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment in structured populations, specifically in the context of the snowdrift game (which is adjacent to public goods games). It shows that costly punishment can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies and suppress defectors in spatially structured settings, but does not quantify the effect on group efficiency or total payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper suggests that spatial structure and the presence of punishment can qualitatively enhance cooperation, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes. The findings are most relevant for understanding how punishment and spatial structure interact to shape population composition, rather than for predicting efficiency changes in public goods games.

- source: Wu, Y., Zhang, B., & Zhang, S. H. (2017). Probabilistic reward or punishment promotes cooperation in evolutionary games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The introduction of a third-party agent who probabilistically rewards cooperators and punishes defectors increases the fraction of cooperators in both the prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games across different network structures. The effect is stronger as the reward/punishment magnitude increases and as the probability of encountering the third-party agent increases. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The results are robust across square lattice, Erdös-Rényi, and scale-free networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that probabilistic punishment (and/or reward) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games, but it does not report on efficiency or group payoff. The model is not a public goods game, but a two-player prisoner's dilemma or snowdrift game, so direct prediction of efficiency in PGGs is not possible. The findings may be useful for understanding the general effect of third-party sanctions on cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Vukov, J., Pinheiro, F. L., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2013). Reward from Punishment Does Not Emerge at All Costs. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in structured (scale-free network) populations, the introduction of peer punishment does not always promote cooperation. When the cost-to-impact ratio (q/p) is high, punishment can actually reduce the prevalence of cooperation compared to the no-punishment baseline. Only when the cost of punishing is sufficiently low relative to its impact does punishment enhance cooperation. The effect is mapped across a range of game parameters (T, S) and punishment parameters (p, q), showing regions where punishment is beneficial or detrimental for cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, but shows that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, on efficiency in adjacent games) is highly sensitive to the cost-effectiveness of punishment (q/p) and to the network structure. In structured populations, punishment can backfire unless costs are low. For prediction, this suggests that the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games may be negative or mixed if punishment is costly relative to its impact, especially in networked or heterogeneous settings. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the direct applicability of these findings to the prediction task.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Wang, Z. (2015). Role of mutual punishment in the snowdrift game. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that mutual punishment in the snowdrift game can either promote or inhibit cooperation depending on the temptation to defect and the punishment fine. For low temptation to defect, increasing punishment fine increases cooperation; for high temptation, it decreases cooperation; for intermediate temptation, there is a non-monotonic relationship. The main reported outcome is the steady-state fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The results are shown for both well-mixed and structured populations, with phase transitions in cooperation rates observed. The findings are relevant for understanding how mutual punishment (peer pressure) affects conformity and cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments with punishment. Its results are based on the snowdrift game, which is adjacent but not equivalent to the PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The mutual punishment mechanism is novel and may inform mechanism design, but the lack of payoff or efficiency analysis limits its direct applicability to the prediction task. The findings may be useful for understanding how punishment parameters affect cooperation rates, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Patrzyk, P. M., & Takác, M. (2017). Cognitive adaptations to criminal justice lead to paranoid norm obedience. *Adaptive Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that evolutionary pressures can lead to agents developing a cognitive bias that causes them to overestimate the probability of being observed and punished for norm violations. As a result, agents often cooperate (obey norms) even when they are not actually being observed, especially in environments with high uncertainty and/or strong punishment. The rate of cooperation increases with the magnitude of punishment, the probability of being observed, and the uncertainty in classifying situations as observed or not. However, much of this cooperation is 'irrational' in the sense that it is not based on accurate assessment of risk, but on evolved bias. The model does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that institutional punishment and cognitive bias can sustain high levels of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that increasing punishment magnitude and the probability of observation can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma settings, even when actual monitoring is imperfect. However, because the model is based on the Prisoner's Dilemma (not a public goods game) and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective in promoting cooperation, especially under uncertainty, but do not quantify the impact on group efficiency or surplus. The model also highlights the importance of cognitive biases in sustaining cooperation, which may be relevant for interpreting experimental results in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Sun, D. H., & Kou, X. L. (2014). Punishment Effect of Prisoner Dilemma Game Based on a New Evolution Strategy Rule. *Mathematical Problems in Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment (in the form of a nonzero payoff for mutual defection, parameter p) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can, under certain evolution rules, destabilize the all-defect equilibrium and promote the emergence and persistence of cooperation. The effect of punishment is nuanced: for some parameter ranges, higher punishment increases the fraction of cooperators, while in others, it stabilizes C-D structures and can reduce the final fraction of cooperators. The main reported outcomes are the fractions of cooperators and the stability of different local structures (C-C, C-D, D-D), not aggregate payoffs or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not use a public goods game or a direct variant. Its findings are about the dynamics of cooperation and the stability of strategy structures in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with punishment. While it provides insight into how punishment can affect the emergence and stability of cooperation, it does not provide quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its relevance to the prediction task is limited and indirect.

- source: Yang, H. X., Wu, Z. X., Rong, Z. H., & Lai, Y. C. (2015). Peer pressure: Enhancement of cooperation through mutual punishment. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that mutual (symmetric) punishment, where individuals punish neighbors with different strategies, can enhance cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. In well-mixed populations, cooperation can only be sustained if the initial density of cooperators is above a threshold, and if the punishment fine is above a critical value. In structured populations (square lattice), there is often an optimal, moderate punishment fine that maximizes cooperation, especially when the initial density of cooperators is not too low. Too weak or too harsh punishment suppresses cooperation. The main outcome is the steady-state fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform prediction of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with punishment. However, it provides indirect evidence that symmetric, mutual punishment can increase cooperation rates under certain conditions, especially with moderate punishment severity and sufficient initial cooperation. The results are based on the prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and do not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, the paper is only adjacent for the prediction task, and should be used with caution, mainly as qualitative support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation, but not necessarily efficiency.

- source: Wang, Z., Xia, C. Y., Meloni, S., Zhou, C. S., & Moreno, Y. (2013). Impact of Social Punishment on Cooperative Behavior in Complex Networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a punisher strategy (who pays a cost to reduce defectors' payoffs) in networked 2-person games (PD and SD) increases the fraction of cooperative strategies (cooperators and punishers) at equilibrium, across a range of network topologies (lattice, ER, scale-free) and punishment severities. The effect is robust: even small punishment fines can substantially increase cooperation. The paper analyzes the spatial and network organization of strategies, showing that punishers can form protective clusters that shield cooperators from defectors. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that social punishment increases the prevalence of cooperative strategies in 2-person networked games, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, the results are only indirectly informative: they suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but the actual payoff consequences (including the cost of punishment) are not analyzed. The game structure is not a public goods game but a 2-person PD/SD, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious.

- source: Niu, X. T., Xu, J. W., & Deng, Z. H. (2017). Conditional punishment resolves social dilemma in spatial network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that introducing and increasing the probability of social punishment (punishers who pay a cost to penalize defectors) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to a higher steady-state density of cooperators. When the willingness to punish is high, defectors are eliminated and cooperation dominates. The mechanism is shown to be robust across a range of temptation-to-defect values. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates and spatial patterns).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment increases cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma settings, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates may imply higher efficiency, but the cost of punishment and its effect on total group payoff are not analyzed. The model is also not a public goods game, but an adjacent two-player game with spatial structure. Use this paper as indirect support for the behavioral effect of punishment, but not for quantitative efficiency predictions.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhu, Y. Y., Chen, Z. Q., & Zhang, J. L. (2017). Punishment in the form of shared cost promotes altruism in the cooperative dilemma games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a multi-player Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment (where only cooperators punish and share the cost), the evolutionary outcome depends on the punishment parameters and group size. Higher punishment effectiveness (greater penalty to defectors) and lower cost to punishers promote the spread and fixation of cooperation. Larger group size and higher temptation to defect make cooperation harder to sustain. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation can dominate, coexist with defection, or be eliminated. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that punishment with shared cost can promote the fixation of cooperation under certain parameter regimes, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly. The effect of group size (player_count) and temptation to defect (analogous to MPCR) are also analyzed. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment with shared cost is likely to increase cooperation rates (and thus efficiency) when punishment is strong and not too costly, but the effect is weaker or negative when punishment is weak or costly, or when group size is large. However, because the model outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency, the translation to efficiency must be inferred.

- source: Perc, M., Donnay, K., & Helbing, D. (2013). Understanding Recurrent Crime as System-Immanent Collective Behavior. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that increasing punishment or inspection does not always reduce crime; instead, the system exhibits complex, sometimes counterintuitive dynamics, including cyclic dominance and abrupt phase transitions between different equilibria (e.g., dominance of criminals, coexistence of criminals and inspectors, or cyclic coexistence of all three strategies). The effectiveness of punishment depends critically on the temptation to commit crime, the cost and incentive for inspection, and the structure of social interactions. The model demonstrates that crime can be recurrent and robust to increased punishment, especially when spatial structure and local interactions are considered. However, the paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly translatable to predictions about group welfare or efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on crime (and by analogy, on cooperation in social dilemmas) is highly sensitive to the interplay of temptation, punishment cost, and network structure, and that simple linear predictions (e.g., more punishment always increases efficiency) are likely to fail. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting the potential for non-monotonic, phase-transition-like effects and the importance of considering spatial and network structure. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests caution in assuming monotonic or simple effects of punishment, especially in spatially structured or networked environments.

- source: Jordan, J. J., Hoffman, M., Bloom, P., & Rand, D. G. (2016). Third-party punishment as a costly signal of trustworthiness. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
