  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that TPP is used as a signal of trustworthiness when it is the only available signal, leading to greater trust and reciprocation in a subsequent trust game. When helping is also available as a signal, the use and signaling value of TPP are reduced, while helping remains a strong signal. The results support a reputational account of TPP, showing that people may punish to signal their character rather than solely to deter selfishness. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the signaling value and frequency of punishment depend on the availability and informativeness of alternative prosocial signals (like helping). However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoffs in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main relevance is in highlighting that the context and available signals can moderate the behavioral use of punishment, which may indirectly affect efficiency in other designs. For direct prediction of efficiency outcomes, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Bruner, J. P. (2013). POLICING EPISTEMIC COMMUNITIES. *Episteme-a Journal of Individual and Social Epistemology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that peer-policing can sustain high levels of honest reporting in a decentralized community if and only if punishment is opportunistic—i.e., if those who police and catch cheaters are rewarded. When the benefit of catching cheaters and the punishment for being caught are both high (with the benefit exceeding the punishment), the population stabilizes with a majority of honest reporters and a minority of policers and cheaters. If punishment is purely altruistic (costly to the punisher), honesty cannot be sustained and the population converges to all cheating. The model also shows that reducing the cost of policing (e.g., by rewarding replication) or increasing the punishment for cheating can further increase the prevalence of honesty. However, the model does not directly report on group efficiency or total welfare, focusing instead on the behavioral dynamics of honesty, policing, and cheating.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment can sustain prosocial behavior (honest reporting) in social dilemmas, but only if the incentive structure rewards punishers. While the model is not a direct public goods game, it is structurally similar and thus informative for predicting when punishment will be effective in PGG-like environments. The key implication is that punishment will only improve group outcomes (e.g., honesty, cooperation) if the cost-benefit structure makes policing individually rational—i.e., if the reward for punishing exceeds the cost and the punishment for being caught cheating exceeds the benefit of cheating. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict efficiency outcomes. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of the incentive structure for the effectiveness of punishment.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., Nie, S. L., & Wang, L. (2015). Emergence of parochial altruism in well-mixed populations of multiple groups. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that parochial altruism is more likely to emerge and be favored by selection when intergroup punishment is more effective (higher fine-to-cost ratio), when there are more groups (higher assortment), and when mutation rates are low. Affiliation (belonging to more groups) makes parochial altruism less likely for low mutation, but more likely for high mutation. The model provides explicit conditions (in terms of benefit, cost, punishment fine, and punishment cost) for when parochial altruism is favored. However, the main outcomes are about the evolutionary abundance of strategies, not about group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment can promote the emergence of cooperation-like strategies in group-structured populations, especially when punishment is effective and group assortment is high. However, because the model is theoretical and focuses on evolutionary stability of strategies rather than direct efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results suggest that punishment is more likely to be beneficial (in terms of promoting cooperation-like behavior) when punishment is effective and group structure is strong, but the translation to efficiency outcomes in experimental PGGs is indirect.

- source: Wang, X. Y., Zhang, L., Du, X. R., & Sun, Y. L. (2017). Evolving cooperation in spatial population with punishment by using PSO algorithm. *Natural Computing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment in spatial evolutionary games (n-player IPD and ISD) increases the proportion of cooperators and punishers, and decreases the proportion of defectors and loners, especially at lower cost-to-benefit ratios. The effect of punishment is stronger in the ISD game, where defection would otherwise dominate. Larger group sizes and higher cost-to-benefit ratios reduce cooperation. The network structure (regular, Moore, Von Neumann, small-world, complete) and neighborhood size also affect cooperation rates. The length of history record (memory) has a non-monotonic effect on cooperation. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial evolutionary games, and that the effect is moderated by group size, cost-to-benefit ratio, and network structure. However, because the outcomes are not efficiency or payoff-based, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that, in similar spatial games, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation, especially in smaller groups and at lower cost-to-benefit ratios, but the effect on efficiency is not directly established.

- source: Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2015). A double-edged sword: Benefits and pitfalls of heterogeneous punishment in evolutionary inspection games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous punishment (punishers who invest different amounts in sanctioning) can reduce the prevalence of second-order free-riders (ordinary people) compared to uniform punishment, but this does not always translate into lower crime rates. In some parameter regions, uniform punishment is as effective or more effective at reducing crime. The system exhibits complex phase transitions and cyclic dominance, which maintain the coexistence of criminals, punishers, and ordinary people. The main obstacle to eliminating crime is the ability of ordinary people to free-ride on the efforts of punishers. The results are presented as fractions of strategies in the population, not as efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, especially regarding the role of punishment heterogeneity and second-order free-riding. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict changes in efficiency when punishment is enabled. The findings suggest that the presence of second-order free-riders and the structure of punishment (uniform vs. heterogeneous) can have complex, non-monotonic effects on cooperation and norm enforcement, which may inform qualitative expectations about efficiency in related games. The results highlight the importance of considering strategy diversity and cyclic dominance in predicting the effects of punishment, but quantitative predictions about efficiency require additional evidence.

- source: Bausch, A. W. (2014). Evolving intergroup cooperation. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in-group policing is highly effective at promoting inter-group cooperation in an evolutionary Prisoner's Dilemma model, with high levels of CC outcomes (over 80% in many runs). In contrast, collective sanctions alone do not increase cooperation above baseline and can even reduce it when combined with in-group policing, especially when the sanction period is long. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that agent-level punishment is more effective than group-level punishment for sustaining cooperation. The findings are robust across a range of parameterizations, but are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than direct payoff or efficiency measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with group structure, agent-level punishment (in-group policing) is much more effective at sustaining cooperation than group-level punishment (collective sanctions). However, because the model does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game, its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited. The results are most useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment can promote or hinder cooperation, and for anticipating that group-level punishment may not translate into higher efficiency or payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of in-group policing (individualized punishment) is likely to have a more positive effect on cooperation than collective sanctions, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Shi, Y. T., Liu, B., Kou, X. L., & Han, X. (2014). Punishment and Feedback Mechanism for the Evolution Game on Small-World Network Based on Varying Topology. *Mathematical Problems in Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that a punishment and feedback mechanism—whereby neighbors of frequent defectors break links with them and form new links with each other—can help maintain a stable fraction of cooperators in a dynamic small-world network playing an n-round Prisoner's Dilemma. The mechanism is robust to the number of rounds played, but its effectiveness diminishes as the average degree (connectivity) and network size increase. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The model is not a public goods game but is related in its focus on cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. Its main relevance is in showing that punishment mechanisms based on breaking social ties can help sustain cooperation in networked Prisoner's Dilemma settings, but the outcome is measured as the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff. The model is adjacent to PGGs, and the findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how network structure and punishment interact to support cooperation, but they do not provide quantitative or directly transferable evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Li, H. Y. (2017). Evolutionary Game Analysis of Emergency Management of the Middle Route of South-to-North Water Diversion Project. *Water Resources Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: Theoretical and numerical analysis show that: (1) Local governments are more likely to show strong willingness to implement emergency policies if the profit from doing so exceeds the increased cost; (2) To avoid all local governments selecting weak cooperation, the central authority (MWR) should increase penalties for weak willingness, making the penalty greater than the cost saved by weak cooperation. The model demonstrates how the strength of punishment and the cost/benefit structure affect the evolution of cooperation, but does not directly quantify efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that increasing punishment (penalties) for non-cooperation can increase the willingness to cooperate in multi-agent settings similar to public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical results. The findings are most useful for understanding the mechanism by which punishment can shift equilibrium behavior toward greater cooperation, which may be associated with higher efficiency in analogous settings. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that higher punishment effectiveness and costliness of non-cooperation can promote cooperative equilibria, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency change.

- source: Li, K., Szolnoki, A., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2016). The coevolution of overconfidence and bluffing in the resource competition game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that in the absence of punishment, both overconfidence and bluffing evolve to high levels, with bluffing generally higher than overconfidence. Introducing punishment (with probability p) for revealed deception reduces both overconfidence and bluffing, with overconfidence being more sensitive to punishment. The structure of the interaction network (degree, heterogeneity) modulates these effects: higher degree or more heterogeneous networks promote bluffing and can make punishment less effective. System bias (delta) that increases conflict frequency also enhances the effectiveness of punishment. Players with higher real ability tend to evolve higher overconfidence and bluffing. The paper provides some analysis of average payoff for punished vs. non-punished players, showing that those with lower ability and lower payoff are punished more often, but does not report group-level efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and probability of punishment can reduce the prevalence of overconfidence and bluffing in resource competition games, especially overconfidence. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (deception levels) and not group efficiency or total payoff, the direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in public goods or similar games is limited. The model is adjacent to PGGs: it is a resource competition game with pairwise contests, not a standard PGG. The punishment mechanism is modeled as a reduction in deception traits rather than direct payoff reduction. The paper does provide some subgroup payoff analysis, suggesting that punishment targets and reduces the payoff of lower-ability, higher-deception individuals, but does not quantify overall group efficiency. Thus, the main decision support is qualitative: punishment reduces harmful behavioral biases, and its effectiveness depends on network structure and conflict frequency, but the effect on group efficiency is not directly established.

- source: Boehm, C. (2014). The moral consequences of social selection. *Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in mobile hunter-gatherer societies, group punishment—ranging from shaming and ostracism to capital punishment—has been a central mechanism for enforcing cooperation and curbing antisocial behaviors such as bullying, theft, and cheating. These sanctions are argued to have shaped the evolution of human conscience and moral norms, favoring individuals who are generous, honest, and humble. The author suggests that such punitive social selection has been critical for the emergence of egalitarian, cooperative societies, and for the evolutionary success of altruistic traits. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative analysis of efficiency or group payoffs in public goods game-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and ethnographic context for the role of punishment in promoting cooperation and suppressing free-riding in small-scale societies. It supports the idea that punishment can be a powerful tool for maintaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper offers indirect support for the positive role of punishment in sustaining cooperation, especially in small, face-to-face groups, but does not allow for parameter-specific predictions based on game design dimensions.

It predicts that the design and effectiveness of such rules depend on the distribution of enforcement power and the alignment of interests between enforcers and the group. The paper provides qualitative and historical examples but does not report experimental or quantitative findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes. It is relevant for understanding the mechanisms behind the emergence of punishment and cooperation rules, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment.

It highlights the importance of power asymmetries, shared interests, and group cohesiveness in shaping the effectiveness and group-beneficial nature of punishment rules. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The review draws on studies using ultimatum, dictator, and trust games, but does not focus on public goods games or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. - source: Singh, M., & Boomsma, J. J. (2015). Policing and punishment across the domains of social evolution. *Oikos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper proposes that punishment and policing behaviors are maintained when the inclusive fitness benefits to the punisher outweigh the costs, and when these benefits are greater than those from bystanding. The framework is applied to a range of biological and social systems, showing that power, relatedness, and reproductive options drive variation in who punishes. In systems with strong power asymmetries, dominants are more likely to punish; in systems with high relatedness and low individual reproductive prospects, policing is more likely to be corrective and group-beneficial. The paper highlights that the same inclusive fitness logic can explain punishment across diverse domains, but does not provide empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a conceptual framework for understanding the conditions under which punishment is likely to emerge and be maintained in social groups, emphasizing the roles of power, relatedness, and reproductive options. However, it does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. For the downstream prediction task, the paper may inform mechanistic expectations about when punishment is likely to be effective or who will implement it, but it does not supply data or direct evidence to calibrate efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions.

  findings: The paper argues that punishment is a fundamental mechanism for stabilizing cooperation in large groups, enabling the evolution and maintenance of pro-social norms and group-level adaptation. It synthesizes evidence from evolutionary theory, ethnography, and game theory to claim that humans' unique adaptive capacity is rooted in our ability to cooperate, which is supported by both formal and informal punishment of defectors. The paper does not present new empirical findings or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, but it does highlight the importance of punishment for sustaining cooperation and adaptive capacity in complex social systems.

  findings: The paper argues that human punishment is rooted in evolved psychological mechanisms, primarily retributive in nature, but that modern institutions have harnessed these mechanisms to promote large-scale cooperation. It reviews evidence that punishment can deter antisocial behavior and stabilize cooperation, referencing public goods game experiments, but also notes that punishment can sometimes reduce group payoffs due to cycles of retaliation. The author distinguishes between second-party and third-party punishment, and between individual and institutional punishment, emphasizing that institutions can professionalize and incentivize punishment in ways that align individual and group interests. The paper does not provide new empirical findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes in experimental games.

However, it provides useful theoretical context: it highlights that punishment can both stabilize cooperation and, under some conditions (e.g., cycles of retaliation), reduce group welfare. It also emphasizes the importance of institutional context and the alignment of individual and group incentives. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether punishment is institutionalized, the risk of retaliation, and the alignment of incentives, but the paper does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence.

- source: Syi (2014). Hidden patterns of reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper formalizes and compares four second-order action rules for direct reciprocity (e.g., Tit-for-tat, Contrite Tit-for-tat, Pavlov, Grim Trigger) and four second-order assessment rules for indirect reciprocity (e.g., Image Scoring, Simple Standing, Stern Judging, Shunning). It shows that these rules can be paired and exhibit similar patterns, especially in their relationship to punishment. The analysis highlights that moderate punishment policies (e.g., Simple Standing, Stern Judging) are most successful at maintaining cooperation, while extreme policies (e.g., Shunning, Grim Trigger) can undermine cooperation. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical evidence about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic insight into how different punishment and reciprocity rules may affect cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. Its value for the prediction task is limited to theoretical framing: it suggests that moderate, justified punishment rules are more likely to sustain cooperation (and thus potentially higher efficiency), while overly harsh or overly lenient rules may be less effective. However, these insights are not tied to specific game design dimensions or empirical efficiency data.

- source: Mariano, P., & Correia, L. (2015). THE GIVE AND TAKE GAME: ANALYSIS OF A RESOURCE SHARING GAME. *International Journal of Applied Mathematics and Computer Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in the Give and Take game, the existence of costly punishment (costs to both the punisher and the punished for the 'take' action) creates a strong incentive for cooperative, efficient sharing of the resource. When punishment costs are high enough, multiple Nash equilibria exist in which players alternate giving the resource, maximizing average payoffs and achieving Pareto optimality. If punishment is cheap or absent, exploitative strategies dominate and efficiency is lower. Simulations confirm that strategies which give the resource (cooperate) are more successful when punishment is costly, even if there is no bonus for giving. The game thus demonstrates that built-in, costly punishment can sustain efficient cooperation in resource sharing dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in two-player resource sharing games with built-in punishment, increasing the cost of punishment (to both punisher and punished) increases the efficiency of the game by supporting cooperative equilibria. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment with sufficiently high costs will increase efficiency relative to the control (no punishment) condition. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, turn-taking, indivisible resource games, but the mechanism—costly punishment deters exploitation and supports efficient cooperation—may generalize to adjacent public goods or resource sharing environments.
