  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in a repeated random matching game (asymmetric gift-giving, not a standard PGG), a reputation protocol with probabilistic punishment (parameterized by punishment probability beta) can induce full cooperation and achieve maximal efficiency (social welfare = b-c) in the long run, provided the discount factor and punishment strength are within certain bounds. If punishment is too weak or too strong, efficiency is lower. Theoretical analysis is supported by numerical simulations showing that the optimal protocol achieves much higher average social welfare than direct reciprocity (tit-for-tat) or less robust reputation schemes. The protocol's design is robust to stochastic perturbations and ensures convergence to the efficient equilibrium.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated random matching environments with binary contribution (gift-giving), the introduction of a well-calibrated reputation-based punishment protocol can move the system from zero efficiency (no cooperation) to full efficiency (all cooperate, social welfare maximized). The effect is robust to noise and initial conditions, provided the punishment probability is neither too low nor too high. While the game is not a standard PGG, the adjacent structure and explicit efficiency analysis make the results highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar environments. The findings suggest that, when punishment is enabled via a reputation protocol and properly tuned, efficiency can increase dramatically compared to the no-punishment baseline.

- source: Feinberg, Y., & Kets, W. (2014). Ranking friends. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of social interactions—specifically, the ranking profiles that determine the order in which players seek help—critically affects the ability to sustain full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) in repeated helping games. When punishment is available (either community or bilateral), full cooperation can be sustained if and only if incentive constraints are satisfied. These constraints are least restrictive (i.e., efficiency is most easily sustained) when expected costs are equalized across players (Latin square ranking profiles). Under bilateral punishment, even stricter symmetry (BFF) is required. The paper provides explicit formulas for the minimum discount factor required to sustain efficiency under each regime. Thus, the design of the interaction structure and the punishment regime jointly determine whether efficiency is achievable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated helping games with sequential requests and costly helping. It shows that the ability of punishment to sustain efficiency depends on the structure of interactions (ranking profiles) and the punishment regime. When the structure equalizes expected costs (Latin square), punishment is most effective at sustaining efficiency. If the structure is unbalanced, punishment is less effective, and efficiency may not be sustainable. The results are theoretical but provide explicit conditions and formulas that can be mapped to efficiency predictions in similar repeated public-goods-like environments, especially where helping is sequential and punishment is either community-wide or bilateral. However, the model is not a standard PGG, so direct quantitative predictions for standard PGGs should be made with caution.

- source: Nakamura, M., & Ohtsuki, H. (2014). Indirect reciprocity in three types of social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player simultaneous-move social dilemmas, the stability and efficiency of cooperation via indirect reciprocity depend on the structure of the social norm (reputation updating rule) and the underlying game. In Snowdrift and Prisoner's Dilemma, strong punishment of defectors (assigning bad reputations for defection against good players) is necessary to maintain high efficiency (i.e., stable cooperation with high group payoff). In Stag Hunt, such punishment is less critical, and norms that unfairly favor reciprocators (always assign good reputation to reciprocation) can enhance the robustness and efficiency of cooperation. The paper also shows that punishing unconditional cooperators (who help bad players) is important for maintaining efficient cooperation in all three games. The efficiency outcome (i.e., group payoff relative to full cooperation) is maximized under certain social norms, but the effect of punishment-like mechanisms is context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (via reputation loss) on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the underlying game structure and the details of the punishment mechanism (social norm). In games with strong temptation to defect (SG, PD), enabling punishment (bad reputation for defectors) increases efficiency by stabilizing cooperation. In coordination games (SH), such punishment is less necessary, and norms that favor reciprocators can be more effective. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency should be moderated by the type of social dilemma and the specific implementation of the punishment/reputation mechanism. The results are most directly relevant to two-player games, not standard multi-player PGGs, but the logic may transfer to small-group settings with reputation-based punishment.

- source: Yuan, Y., Wang, F. Y., & Zeng, D. (2016). Developing a cooperative bidding framework for sponsored search markets - An evolutionary perspective. *Information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated sponsored search auctions, if advertisers are limited to unconditional strategies (always cooperate or always compete), competitive strategies dominate, resulting in high bids and low efficiency (advertiser revenue is about 0.64 times the UBNE outcome, which is suboptimal). When contingent 'nice and retaliatory' (NR) strategies are available—where advertisers cooperate unless a rival deviates, in which case they punish by bidding competitively—stable cooperation emerges. This leads to lower bids (at the GEAE, the lowest-price equilibrium) and advertiser revenues at the socially optimal VCG level. The NR strategies are neutrally evolutionarily stable: they cannot be strongly invaded by competitive strategies, and cooperation persists. The effect is robust to incomplete information, as long as advertisers can monitor rivals' bids and retaliate. The findings are supported by both theoretical analysis and coevolutionary simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment-like contingent strategies (NR) in repeated, multi-player, auction-like public goods environments can substantially increase efficiency (group payoff) relative to a control condition where only unconditional strategies are available. The effect is robust across a range of parameterizations (number of players, rounds, etc.), and the mechanism is directly analogous to punishment in public goods games: punishment of defectors stabilizes cooperation and increases group welfare. For prediction tasks, the presence of contingent punishment strategies should be expected to increase efficiency from a low baseline (competitive equilibrium) to near the social optimum, provided players can observe and respond to others' actions. The results are most directly applicable to repeated, multi-player, auction-like games with observable actions and the possibility of contingent retaliation.

- source: Shinya, O., Yusuke, I., & Hiroki, T. (2016). The Condition for Generous Trust. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with uncertainty about partner intention and competence, neither pure punishment nor pure generosity alone can sustain high efficiency. Generous trust (forgiveness) becomes viable and can support efficient outcomes when uncertainty is moderate and is combined with moderate punishment. If uncertainty (incompetence) is too high, efficiency collapses. The agent-based simulation shows that a mix of matching generosity (forgiving in partner selection) and exit generosity (forgiving in relationship maintenance) strategies coevolve and support higher efficiency, but only above a threshold of partner competence. The results highlight the importance of balancing punishment and forgiveness to achieve efficient cooperation under uncertainty.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in trust-game-like environments depends critically on the level of uncertainty (competence) and the balance between punishment and forgiveness. In environments with moderate uncertainty, introducing moderate punishment alongside generosity increases efficiency compared to either strategy alone. However, if uncertainty is too high, even punishment cannot sustain efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the efficiency gain from punishment will be highest when the environment allows for some, but not excessive, uncertainty, and when punishment is not too strict or too lenient. The findings are most directly relevant to trust games and adjacent repeated dyadic games, rather than standard public goods games, but the mechanisms may transfer to PGG variants with similar uncertainty and partner selection features.

- source: Quiñones, A. E., van Doorn, G. S., Pen, I., Weissing, F. J., & Taborsky, M. (2016). Negotiation and appeasement can be more effective drivers of sociality than kin selection. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: Negotiation and appeasement mechanisms, modeled as responsive strategies where dominants can threaten aggression (punishment) and subordinates can appease by helping, lead to higher group productivity than kin-selected (unconditional) helping. The model shows that when negotiation is possible, even unrelated individuals can achieve high levels of help and group output, with low realized aggression. High relatedness, however, reduces the effectiveness of punishment/enforcement, leading to lower help and lower group productivity. Thus, punishment-like negotiation mechanisms are more effective at driving efficient cooperation than kin selection alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence of responsive punishment or enforcement mechanisms (negotiation) increases group efficiency in public-goods-like environments, compared to settings where only kin selection operates. The model is not a literal PGG, but the structure is closely analogous: individuals can help (contribute) or not, dominants can punish (aggression), and group output (productivity) is tracked. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment/negotiation will increase efficiency, especially when relatedness is low. The effect is robust across a range of parameter values. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment-enabled conditions on efficiency, and highlights that high relatedness may reduce the marginal benefit of punishment.

- source: Xu, J., Song, Y. B., & van der Schaar, M. (2014). Sharing in Networks of Strategic Agents. *Ieee Journal of Selected Topics in Signal Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that distributed rating-based sanctioning protocols can fully or nearly fully restore efficiency in repeated network sharing games with strategic agents, provided certain incentive compatibility conditions are met. When the network's connectivity is not too high and agents' discount factors are sufficient, the protocol achieves the social optimum (PoA=1). As network connectivity increases or agents become more impatient, efficiency declines but remains higher than with direct reciprocity mechanisms. The protocol is robust to imperfect monitoring and can be tuned for dynamic networks. Theoretical and simulation results show that the protocol outperforms Tit-for-Tat and similar direct punishment schemes, especially in heterogeneous or asymmetric networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that distributed, rating-based punishment mechanisms can substantially increase efficiency in repeated networked cooperation environments, often achieving the social optimum. The effect is most pronounced when the network is not too highly connected and agents are sufficiently patient. The protocol's performance is robust to imperfect monitoring and network dynamics. For prediction tasks, the presence of a distributed, indirect punishment mechanism (ratings affecting future access) is a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially compared to direct, bilateral punishment. The results are most directly applicable to networked repeated games with costly sharing, which are adjacent to but not identical to standard public goods games.

- source: O'Connor, C. (2016). The Evolution of Guilt: A Model-Based Approach. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that guilt, modeled as a behavioral trait leading to cooperation, apology, and reparative actions, can be individually adaptive in evolutionary games. In both stag hunt and prisoner's dilemma settings, guilt-proneness increases the likelihood of cooperation, which in turn raises individual and group payoffs. When punishment for defection is present, guilt-proneness further increases individual fitness by reducing the risk of being punished. The models show that costly or honest signals of apology (as a proxy for guilt) can stabilize cooperation and prevent the invasion of defectors or 'fakers.' The efficiency of the group (measured as average fitness or payoff) is higher in populations where guilt-proneness and apology are present, especially when punishment mechanisms exist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms, when combined with behavioral traits like guilt-proneness and apology, can increase efficiency (group payoff) in social dilemma games. While it does not directly model public goods games, the findings are adjacent and suggest that enabling punishment and supporting honest signals of intent (like apology) are likely to increase efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The models also highlight the importance of the cost and credibility of punishment and apology mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that introducing punishment (especially with some cost or honest signaling) will increase efficiency, provided that the game environment allows for reciprocity and repeated interaction.

- source: Handfield, T., Huang, P. H., & Simpson, R. M. (2016). Climate change, cooperation and moral bioenhancement. *Journal of Medical Ethics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that enhancing retributive (punishment) tendencies in social dilemma games (including public goods and prisoner's dilemma paradigms) is likely to lower group efficiency, even if it increases cooperation rates. The authors review theoretical models and cite experimental evidence indicating that harsh punishment can make groups poorer, and that those who punish often gain less than those who do not. The effect of punishment on efficiency is sensitive to the cost/benefit ratio but relatively insensitive to punishment severity. The paper also notes that anti-social punishment (punishing cooperators) can be highly destructive to cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and literature-based evidence that enabling or increasing punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is likely to reduce group efficiency, especially when punishment is harsh or misapplied. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, adding or intensifying punishment mechanisms may not increase and may even decrease efficiency, despite possible increases in cooperation rates. The effect is particularly negative when punishment is severe or when anti-social punishment is possible. The paper is less informative about the precise quantitative effect sizes but is clear about the direction of the effect on efficiency.

- source: Furuzono, Y., Yamakawa, T., Ohnishi, S., & Kaneko, T. (2013). Combined effects of social pressure from the inside and outside of fishing communities promote cooperation for stock management. *Ecological Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a coupled socioeconomic-bioeconomic model of a fishing community, where individuals choose to cooperate with management or not, and non-cooperators experience psychological punishment ('social pressure') that depends on both internal (community-based) and external (outside community) sources. The model shows that when both internal and external social pressures are sufficiently strong, the system can reach full cooperation, resulting in sustainable fish stocks and maximum sustainable yield (MSY)—a proxy for group efficiency. If only one type of social pressure is strong, or both are weak, the system may stabilize at lower cooperation and lower stock levels. The model highlights the importance of both community relationships and external societal pressure in promoting cooperation for resource management.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that psychological punishment mechanisms (social pressure) can increase group efficiency in common-pool resource settings analogous to public goods games, especially when both internal and external sources are present. While the model is not a standard PGG and does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, it shows that the presence and strength of punishment-like mechanisms (social pressure) are critical moderators of group outcomes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling both internal and external punishment/sanction mechanisms is likely to increase efficiency, while the absence or weakness of either reduces the effect. The model also suggests that the effect of punishment is moderated by group size (player count), as social pressure per individual decreases with larger groups.

- source: Pin, P., & Rogers, B. W. (2015). Cooperation, punishment and immigration. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the punishment (expulsion) rate for immigrant defectors increases the overall cooperation rate in society, but decreases the cooperation rate among citizens. This is due to an equilibrium substitution effect: as immigrants are more strongly incentivized to cooperate, the overall level of cooperation rises above its 'natural' level, making defection more attractive for citizens. The model shows that the net effect of punishment is smaller than would be predicted if equilibrium effects were ignored. The paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but expected payoffs for different agent types are derived. The results are robust to heterogeneity in agent preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment targeted at a subset of players (immigrants) can increase overall cooperation rates, but may also induce more defection among the untargeted group (citizens), reducing the net effect on cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff may be less positive than expected if substitution effects are present. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its quantitative value for predicting efficiency is limited. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it is based on a networked prisoner's dilemma with binary (all-or-nothing) choices and dynamic linking, rather than a standard PGG. The findings are most relevant for understanding equilibrium behavioral responses to targeted punishment policies.

- source: Verma, P., Nandi, A. K., & Sengupta, S. (2017). Bribery games on inter-dependent regular networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the spread of honest officers (and thus reduction in bribery) is favored over a larger region of parameter space compared to mixed populations. However, the effect of network connectivity is non-monotonic: increasing the degree of the citizen network initially helps honest strategies, but beyond an optimal point, further increases hinder the spread of honesty. Similarly, the degree of the interaction network (citizen-officer links) has an optimal value for maximizing the prevalence of honest officers. Asymmetry in population sizes (more citizens per officer) makes it harder to eradicate bribery. The cost of complaining and the bribe amount are key parameters: lower cost of complaining and lower bribe amounts favor honest strategies. The main dependent variables are the fixation probability of honest officers and the phase space area where honest strategies dominate, not direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it analyzes how network structure, punishment asymmetry, and behavioral parameters affect the prevalence of honest (non-corrupt) strategies. In public-goods-like environments with asymmetric punishment, structured populations can reduce corruption, but only up to an optimal level of network connectivity. The findings suggest that, for similar games, enabling punishment (asymmetric, targeting only one side) in a structured population can increase the prevalence of socially beneficial behavior, but the effect depends on network connectivity and behavioral costs. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that punishment can improve outcomes (in terms of honest behavior) in structured settings, but warns that too much connectivity can undermine this effect. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured, the findings should be used as indirect evidence.

- source: Safarzynska, K. (2013). The coevolution of culture and environment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that costly resource conservation (including punishment of defectors) can evolve in structured populations facing renewable resource constraints and environmental shocks. Groups with many defectors are more likely to overharvest and collapse, while groups with more cooperators and punishers are more resilient. Punishment reduces the within-group advantage of defection, but is itself costly. Environmental shocks (weather) accelerate the elimination of non-cooperative groups. Between-group interactions (resource-conflict and harvest-sharing) modulate the evolution of cooperation: resource-conflict increases the frequency of resource conservation by increasing collapse of defector-heavy groups, while harvest-sharing reduces group collapse and allows more defection. Larger group size has a non-monotonic effect: above a threshold, larger groups are more likely to collapse, which can favor the evolution of conservation. Policies that increase the cost of extraction or penalize defectors promote conservation. However, the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome, focusing instead on behavioral strategy frequencies and group survival.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that punishment and resource conservation can evolve and persist in group-structured resource dilemmas, especially under environmental variability and when group collapse is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The model's outcomes are mainly about the frequency of cooperative/punishing strategies and group survival, not efficiency as defined for the prediction task. Thus, while the mechanisms discussed (e.g., punishment reducing defection, group collapse selecting for cooperation) are relevant for understanding when punishment might improve group outcomes, the paper does not provide data or results that can be directly mapped to efficiency predictions for specific game design dimensions.

- source: Li, Q., Iqbal, A., Perc, M., Chen, M. Y., & Abbott, D. (2013). Coevolution of Quantum and Classical Strategies on Evolving Random Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when agents can punish by reducing link weights, cooperation can dominate for a wider range of temptation values, especially when the network structure is updated frequently. Stronger punishment (lower minimum link weight) increases the range where cooperation dominates. As the temptation to defect increases, quantum strategies eventually dominate. The number of neighbors (network degree) has little effect if all agents have equal degree. The network evolves to have higher clustering and small-world properties. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings are about strategy prevalence, not direct payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (reducing link weights) on the prevalence of cooperation in an evolving network Prisoner's Dilemma. It suggests that stronger punishment and more frequent network updating support cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only adjacent: it supports the idea that punishment can sustain cooperation under some conditions, but does not quantify efficiency gains or losses. The game is not a public goods game, but a networked PD with quantum strategies, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious.

- source: Mameli, M. (2013). Meat made us moral: a hypothesis on the nature and evolution of moral judgment. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that the evolution of moral judgment is rooted in emotional dispositions that support the enforcement of group-beneficial norms through punishment and meta-punishment. It draws on ethnographic and evolutionary accounts of hunter-gatherer societies, where collective punishment of cheaters and bullies (those who disrupt cooperation and resource sharing) is common and is enforced through both direct and reputational means (e.g., gossip, ostracism, disapproval). The author suggests that these mechanisms enabled stable cooperation in small groups, but the analysis is conceptual and evolutionary rather than empirical or quantitative. The paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and meta-punishment mechanisms can stabilize cooperation in small, egalitarian groups, especially in contexts analogous to public goods dilemmas (e.g., meat sharing after cooperative hunting). However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff outcomes with or without punishment. Its relevance is mainly in supporting the plausibility of punishment as a stabilizer of cooperation, especially when punishment is collective, reputational, and embedded in group norms. For prediction tasks, this paper may inform the expected direction of punishment effects in small, highly interactive, reputation-sensitive groups, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific guidance.

- source: Riehl, C., & Frederickson, M. E. (2016). Cheating and punishment in cooperative animal societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that in animal societies, cheating (adaptive non-cooperation that increases the cheater's fitness at the expense of others) is rare, and when it does occur, punishment is also rare or not clearly evolved as a response to cheating. Most uncooperative individuals have lower, not higher, fitness than cooperators, and variation in cooperation is often due to condition or context rather than strategic cheating. Where punishment or policing is observed (e.g., worker policing in social insects, aggression toward non-helping subordinates), it is often not clearly linked to increased group efficiency or evolved specifically to suppress cheating. The review concludes that cooperation is usually stable because it is directly or indirectly beneficial, not because punishment enforces it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, especially in the context of game design dimensions. It suggests that, in natural animal societies, punishment is not a robust or general mechanism for increasing group efficiency or suppressing cheating, and that cooperation is often stable for other reasons. For prediction tasks, this implies that the presence of punishment mechanisms may not reliably increase efficiency unless cheating is truly adaptive and punishment is both targeted and effective. The review also highlights the importance of context, such as group size, relatedness, and the direct benefits of cooperation, which may moderate the effect of punishment.

- source: Abdulkadiroglu, A., & Bagwell, K. (2013). Trust, Reciprocity, and Favors in Cooperative Relationships. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with private information, efficiency (total group payoff) can be increased above the autarky (Nash) level through favor-exchange relationships (dynamic reciprocity) or through symmetric punishment (negative reciprocity). The optimal equilibrium structure depends on the parameters: when information asymmetries are low, symmetric punishment can support higher efficiency than favor-exchange. Hybrid equilibria (honeymoon period followed by favor-exchange or punishment) can further improve efficiency in some cases. The paper provides explicit formulas for the efficiency achieved under each regime. Immediate reciprocity (punishment for non-reciprocation) is not always necessary; dynamic reciprocity (future favor) is often sufficient. The model is not a PGG but is closely related in its focus on cooperation, trust, and efficiency under repeated interaction and private information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical predictions for how efficiency in repeated cooperation games is affected by the availability and structure of punishment and favor-exchange mechanisms. While not a PGG, the model is adjacent and the efficiency formulas and comparative statics are informative for predicting efficiency in environments where punishment (symmetric, infrequent) is possible versus when only favor-exchange is available. The results suggest that, in some parameter regions, introducing symmetric punishment can increase efficiency above favor-exchange alone, especially when information asymmetries are low. However, the model is limited to two players and does not include standard PGG features like group size or marginal per-capita return. The findings are most useful for understanding the theoretical potential of punishment to enhance efficiency in repeated cooperation with private information, but direct quantitative prediction for PGGs would require caution.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., Santos, F. C., & Lenaerts, T. (2013). Good Agreements Make Good Friends. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that prior commitment strategies, where players agree in advance to cooperate and specify compensation for defection, can achieve higher levels of cooperation and average payoff than traditional costly punishment, provided the arrangement cost is not too high. Sharing the cost of commitment further increases the viability and efficiency of these strategies. The efficiency gains are robust across a range of compensation and arrangement cost parameters, and commitment-based cooperation is less susceptible to antisocial punishment. The findings are derived from analytical risk-dominance conditions and evolutionary simulations, focusing on the stationary distribution of strategies and average payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that mechanisms analogous to prior commitment with compensation (distinct from standard punishment) can yield higher efficiency than costly punishment, especially when the cost of arranging the agreement is low or shared. While the model is based on the one-shot PD rather than a public goods game, the efficiency results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments with similar cost/impact structures. The findings imply that, for games with low punishment/arrangement costs and high punishment/compensation effectiveness, enabling commitment or cost-sharing mechanisms should increase efficiency relative to standard punishment. The paper also highlights the importance of cost-to-impact ratios and the potential for free-riding if arrangement costs are high and not shared.

- source: Inaba, M., Takahashi, N., & Ohtsuki, H. (2016). Robustness of linkage strategy that leads to large-scale cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that a linkage strategy—where defectors in a social dilemma are excluded from indirect reciprocity benefits—can be evolutionarily stable and maintain high group efficiency, but only when there are implementation errors in the social dilemma and no perception errors. In the absence of errors, linkage and non-linkage strategies are neutral, allowing non-linkage strategies to drift in and eventually enable defectors to invade, collapsing cooperation and efficiency. Perception errors (misjudging others' actions) undermine the stability and efficiency of linkage strategies, as they disrupt the ability to assortatively cooperate and exclude defectors. The results highlight that the effectiveness of exclusionary sanctions (linkage) for maintaining efficiency is highly sensitive to the type and prevalence of errors in the system.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-like exclusion mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that exclusionary sanctions (linkage) can increase efficiency, but only if players can reliably observe and agree on others' actions (i.e., low perception error) and if there is some noise in implementation (so that linkage strategies can distinguish themselves from non-linkage cooperators). If perception errors are common, the efficiency benefit of punishment/exclusion is undermined. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion from future benefits rather than direct costly punishment, and where the information environment is well-specified. The results are less directly transferable to standard PGGs with explicit costly punishment, but the mechanisms and moderators (error types, information) are highly relevant for prediction.

- source: Pérez-Escudero, A., Friedman, J., & Gore, J. (2016). Preferential interactions promote blind cooperation and informed defection. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games where one player can gather information before cooperating or defecting, aversion to information gathering (i.e., a preference for 'blind' cooperation) can be sustained either by punishment (ending the game if the player looks) or by preferential interactions (continuing only with reliable partners). In homogeneous populations, punishment is required to enforce blind cooperation and thus higher payoffs. In heterogeneous populations, preferential interactions can achieve similar or greater efficiency without punishment. The model also predicts that if detecting information gathering is costly, punishment-based mechanisms break down in homogeneous populations, but preferential interactions remain effective in heterogeneous ones. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore positive in homogeneous populations but redundant or unnecessary in heterogeneous ones.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated cooperation games depends on population heterogeneity. In homogeneous populations, punishment (ending the game after information gathering or defection) is necessary to enforce cooperation and increase efficiency. In heterogeneous populations, preferential partner selection can achieve high efficiency without punishment. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should consider the degree of population heterogeneity and the possibility of preferential interactions. If the environment is homogeneous and punishment is strong, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency. If the environment is heterogeneous and allows for preferential interactions, punishment may have little or no additional effect on efficiency.

- source: Harbaugh, R., & To, T. (2014). Opportunistic discrimination. *European Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with two groups, the ability to sustain efficient, cooperative outcomes depends on the punishment strategy adopted by individuals. Solidarity-based punishment (where all individuals punish any opportunism) supports high efficiency and deters cheating. However, if individuals only punish opportunism against their own group (discriminatory trigger), the minority group is more likely to be cheated, leading to lower efficiency for the minority and lower overall surplus. The model also shows that selective legal enforcement (protecting only the majority) can reduce the effectiveness of reputational punishment, making the minority worse off. Only anti-discrimination enforcement (penalizing group-based opportunism) robustly increases efficiency and reduces discrimination. The results highlight that the structure of punishment and enforcement mechanisms critically shapes efficiency and the distribution of payoffs across groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency depends on whether punishment is coordinated across groups (solidarity) or limited to within-group (discriminatory). In environments where punishment is only triggered by harm to one's own group, efficiency gains from punishment are limited, especially for minorities. The model also warns that adding legal enforcement in a way that only protects the majority can reduce overall efficiency for minorities by undermining reputational incentives. For prediction tasks, this suggests that game designs enabling broad, group-independent punishment (solidarity) will yield higher efficiency gains from punishment, while designs that fragment punishment or allow selective enforcement may see limited or even negative effects on efficiency, especially for minority subgroups.

- source: Hilbe, C., Traulsen, A., & Sigmund, K. (2015). Partners or rivals? Strategies for the iterated prisoner's dilemma. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper provides a comprehensive theoretical analysis of memory-one strategies in the IPD, showing which strategies can enforce mutual cooperation (maximal efficiency), competitive advantage, or linear payoff relationships (ZD strategies). It proves that partner strategies (including Grim and Tit-for-Tat under certain conditions) can enforce mutual cooperation, and that generous ZD strategies can promote cooperation by aligning payoffs. The existence and effectiveness of these strategies depend on the discount factor (patience) and the payoff matrix. The paper does not analyze the effect of enabling or disabling punishment as a separate game feature, but it shows that strategies implementing contingent punishment (e.g., Grim) are critical for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how strategy design in repeated games can enforce efficient outcomes, but it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling punishment as a separate game feature in public goods games. Its results are most relevant for two-player repeated games (IPD) and show that strategies implementing contingent punishment (like Grim or Tit-for-Tat) are necessary for sustaining cooperation and efficiency. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment in PGG-like environments, the paper suggests that the ability to implement credible punishment (via strategy) is essential for efficiency, but it does not address institutional punishment mechanisms or group settings directly. Thus, its value is mainly theoretical and mechanistic, not empirical or directly predictive for PGGs with explicit punishment options.

- source: Fujiwara-Greve, T., Okuno-Fujiwara, M., & Suzuki, N. (2015). Efficiency may improve when defectors exist. *Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with endogenous partner choice and no information flow, the most efficient Nash equilibrium is not the symmetric trust-building equilibrium (where all players initially defect to build trust), but rather a bimorphic equilibrium where some players always cooperate and others always defect and leave. This equilibrium is locally stable and, under a 'small stake' condition (when the temptation to defect is not too large), is more efficient than any equilibrium with only trust-building strategies. The efficiency gain comes from allowing a higher share of cooperators, even though some are exploited by defectors. The paper provides explicit conditions for when this efficiency dominance holds and characterizes the parameter regions where the presence of defectors improves efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environments with endogenous partner choice and no information flow, the introduction of unconditional defectors can increase equilibrium efficiency compared to trust-building (punishment-based) equilibria, especially when the temptation to defect is not too large (small stake condition) and the effective discount factor (survival rate) is high. The efficiency of the equilibrium is explicitly characterized as a function of the payoff parameters and the share of cooperators/defectors. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not feature explicit costly punishment, it is highly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated games with endogenous partner selection and limited punishment options. The results suggest that, under certain conditions, the presence of defectors can be efficiency-improving, and that efficiency does not always monotonically increase with the strength or prevalence of punishment strategies.

- source: Ghachem, M. (2016). The institution as a blunt instrument: Cooperation through imperfect observability. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that institutional tagging, even when imperfect, can substantially increase the efficiency (payoff) of cooperation in social dilemmas like the Prisoner's Dilemma. The key mechanism is that tagged cooperators are more likely to be chosen for interaction, leading to non-uniform interaction rates that favor cooperators. The model provides analytical conditions under which cooperation is favored by selection, is risk dominant, or is more abundant in the mutation-selection equilibrium. The positive effect on efficiency is robust to moderate costs of tag production and search. The effect is highly sensitive to the institution's error rates, especially the rate at which defectors are mistakenly tagged as cooperators. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but institutional tagging serves a similar function by enabling partner selection based on observed tags.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that institutional mechanisms which increase the observability of cooperation (even imperfectly) can increase group efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. While not a public goods game per se, the findings are adjacent and likely transferable to PGG-like settings where partner choice or reputation is possible. The model suggests that, in environments where institutions can credibly tag cooperators and individuals can use these tags to select partners, enabling such mechanisms will increase efficiency relative to a control with no tagging. The effect is stronger when the institution is more accurate (especially in avoiding false positives for defectors), when more of the population is screened, and when individuals can search more for partners. The results are less directly applicable to settings where punishment is the main intervention, but the underlying logic (institutional information enabling discrimination) is relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment-like or reputation-based interventions.

- source: Ochea, M. I. (2013). Evolution of repeated prisoner's dilemma play under logit dynamics. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with a finite set of memory-one strategies, the long-run average payoff (efficiency) and the prevalence of cooperation depend critically on the mix of strategies present and the game parameters. In some ecologies, strategies like Pavlov (WSLS), which can be interpreted as implementing endogenous punishment (by defecting after being defected upon), can dominate and sustain high payoffs, especially when unconditional cooperators are absent. However, the introduction of unconditional cooperators (AllC) can destabilize cooperation and lead to the dominance of defectors (AllD), reducing efficiency. The dynamics can be complex, with cycles and chaos emerging for intermediate values of the intensity of choice parameter. The paper provides explicit formulas for average payoffs and analyzes bifurcations in the system as parameters change. Overall, the effect of 'punishment-like' strategies on efficiency is mixed and highly sensitive to the strategic environment and parameter values.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like strategies (e.g., Pavlov/WSLS) on efficiency in repeated social dilemma games is highly context-dependent. In environments without unconditional cooperators, such strategies can sustain cooperation and high payoffs, but their effectiveness is undermined when unconditional cooperators are present. The results suggest that the presence or absence of certain strategy types (especially unconditional cooperators) and the values of key parameters (benefit of cooperation, error rates, intensity of choice) are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of enabling punishment (or punishment-like strategies) on efficiency cannot be determined solely by the presence of punishment, but must account for the broader strategic ecology and parameter settings. The findings are most directly relevant to repeated PD-like environments, but may inform predictions in PGG-like settings by analogy.

- source: Shibayama, S. (2015). Academic commercialization and changing nature of academic cooperation. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the prevalence of commercial actors (who do not cooperate gratis) in an academic community undermines the reputation mechanism that sustains indirect reciprocity. This leads to a decline in unconditional cooperation, a rise in demand for direct rewards (e.g., coauthorship, barter), and an increase in abstention from cooperation. The overall effect is a reduction in group efficiency and mean payoff, as the system moves away from the collectively optimal state. Centralized rewarding can partially restore cooperation and efficiency, but only if the reward is carefully calibrated; excessive rewards can backfire by incentivizing defectors. The model provides formal predictions and phase diagrams showing how the basin of attraction for efficient cooperation shrinks as commercialization increases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and design of punishment, reward, and reputation mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While not a direct PGG or lab experiment, the model is closely related and analyzes efficiency outcomes as a function of game design parameters (e.g., cost/benefit of cooperation, reward size, reputation availability, prevalence of defectors). For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling effective punishment or reward (especially centralized, carefully sized rewards) can increase efficiency, but that the presence of many defectors or commercial actors will reduce the effectiveness of reputation-based cooperation and lower efficiency. The model also highlights the importance of the matching rate for rewards and the availability of reputation information. However, the absence of empirical or experimental data means that quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Mani, A., Rahwan, I., & Pentland, A. (2013). Inducing Peer Pressure to Promote Cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing peer pressure mechanisms—where peers are incentivized to exert costly pressure on each other to reduce negative externalities—can lead to higher social surplus (efficiency) than traditional Pigouvian mechanisms, especially in large, sparse networks. Theoretical results show that peer pressure reduces equilibrium action levels (i.e., reduces over-consumption or under-contribution), but not all the way to the social optimum unless subsidized. Social mechanisms that reward peers for their neighbors' good behavior are more cost-effective than direct rewards to individuals. The model predicts that the efficiency gain from peer pressure increases as the cost of exerting pressure decreases and as social ties strengthen. An illustrative experiment on physical activity supports these predictions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling peer-based sanctions or rewards (peer pressure) can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of peer pressure is moderate and the network is sparse. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing peer-based punishment or reward mechanisms should increase efficiency relative to control, and that the effect is stronger when the cost of peer pressure is lower and when social ties are stronger. The model is not an exact PGG, but is closely related and the efficiency results are directly relevant. The paper does not provide direct empirical estimates for standard PGG parameters, but the qualitative direction is clear: peer pressure mechanisms are more efficient than no intervention, and often more efficient than centralized punishment/reward, especially at low budgets.

- source: Al-Dhanhani, A., Mizouni, R., Otrok, H., & Al-Rubaie, A. (2014). A game theoretical model for collaborative groups in social applications. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of reciprocity and punishment strategies in repeated social dilemmas (modeled as asynchronous iterated prisoner's dilemma) has a strong effect on group efficiency and the distribution of gains between cooperative and selfish (free riding) users. Classical Tit for Tat leads to deadlock and declining payoffs. Generous Tit for Tat avoids deadlock and increases group gain, but allows free riders to benefit disproportionately. Reputation-based Tit for Tat reduces free rider gain but can also reduce cooperative user gain, especially if the reputation threshold is set high or the initialization phase is short. Group reputation-based Tit for Tat (indirect reciprocity) achieves the highest cooperative user gain and lowest free rider gain, thus maximizing group efficiency and survivability. The presence of free riders always reduces group efficiency, but the right punishment/reciprocity mechanism can mitigate this loss.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of punishment/reciprocity mechanisms (especially those based on group reputation and indirect reciprocity) can significantly improve group efficiency in repeated social dilemma environments, even in the presence of free riders. While the model is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related and the efficiency outcomes are directly comparable. The findings suggest that enabling punishment or exclusion based on group-level reputation (rather than only direct reciprocity) is likely to increase efficiency relative to no punishment or to simple direct reciprocity. The results are most relevant for settings with repeated interactions, observable histories, and the ability to track and act on group-level reputation. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or direct mappings to all design dimensions, but the qualitative direction is clear: more sophisticated, group-based punishment/reciprocity mechanisms are more effective at sustaining efficiency.

- source: Gao, L., Li, Y. T., & Wang, R. W. (2015). The shift between the Red Queen and the Red King effects in mutualisms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in multiplayer snowdrift games modeling mutualisms, the introduction and increase of a collective reward mechanism can shift the allocation of mutualistic benefits from favoring the faster-evolving species (Red Queen effect) to favoring the slower-evolving species (Red King effect). Asymmetries in the number of interacting partners or in payoffs can also shift the balance of surplus allocation. The model predicts that, depending on the reward intensity and asymmetries, the efficiency (total surplus generated and its division) can be increased or made more unequal. The model is theoretical and does not provide empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the presence and intensity of a reward mechanism (analogous to a positive incentive or bonus for cooperation) can substantially alter the efficiency and allocation of surplus in multiplayer social dilemmas. While it does not directly model punishment or public goods games, its results suggest that reward mechanisms can increase group efficiency and shift the distribution of payoffs, especially in the presence of asymmetries. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that enabling a reward mechanism (with sufficient intensity) can increase efficiency and alter the distribution of payoffs, but the effect depends on the details of the reward and the structure of the game (e.g., group size, asymmetries). The findings are most relevant for settings with reward rather than punishment, and for snowdrift-like rather than public-goods-like games.

- source: Sasaki, T., Okada, I., & Nakai, Y. (2017). The evolution of conditional moral assessment in indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the 'Staying' norm, which prescribes abstaining from moral assessment when a donor interacts with a recipient of bad reputation, is more effective at establishing and maintaining cooperation than the four most studied social norms in indirect reciprocity. Under Staying, discriminators (conditional cooperators) are more likely to invade populations of defectors, and the evolutionary dynamics favor higher average payoffs and more efficient group outcomes. The model demonstrates that the range of initial conditions leading to efficient, cooperative equilibria is wider under Staying, and that the strict application of moral judgment (as in other norms) is less effective for promoting efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity games (a close relative of public goods games), the design of the assessment/punishment norm can have a large effect on group efficiency. Specifically, conditional assessment (Staying) leads to higher efficiency than unconditional assessment (Scoring, etc.), especially when assessment errors are low. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the details of the punishment/assessment mechanism (e.g., whether punishment is conditional or unconditional, and how errors are handled) are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. The findings support the idea that more nuanced, context-sensitive punishment/assessment rules can yield higher efficiency than strict, always-on punishment rules.

- source: Bird, R. B., & Power, E. A. (2015). Prosocial signaling and cooperation among Martu hunters. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In populations of SLA agents without memory, those with low penalty sensitivity dominate, leading to high defection rates and low average payoffs. (2) SLA strategies are not stable against invasion by TFT or ALLD; TFT can dominate and lead to higher population payoffs. (3) Adding memory to SLA agents enables them to learn to cooperate with TFT, increasing cooperation and average payoffs, but this effect is limited to interactions with TFT; among themselves, SLA agents still defect. (4) The penalty parameter (analogous to punishment sensitivity) is a key moderator: higher penalty sensitivity leads to more cooperation and higher payoffs, but is outcompeted by less sensitive types in evolutionary dynamics. (5) The model predicts that, absent memory or external cooperative strategies, populations of simple learning agents will evolve toward defection and low efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environments without explicit punishment mechanisms, agents with low sensitivity to penalties (i.e., low punishment cost or low punishment effectiveness) evolve toward defection and low efficiency. The findings suggest that, in analogous public goods games, the presence and effectiveness of punishment (or penalty sensitivity) are critical for sustaining cooperation and high efficiency. However, the model does not include explicit peer punishment as a game design feature, so direct quantitative predictions for the effect of enabling punishment in public goods games are limited. The results do support the qualitative prediction that increasing the effectiveness or salience of punishment (or penalty) responses can increase efficiency, but such strategies may be evolutionarily unstable without additional mechanisms (e.g., memory, reputation, or external enforcement).

- source: Garay, J., Csiszár, V., & Móri, T. F. (2014). Under multilevel selection: When shall you be neither spiteful nor envious?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations with multilevel selection, the evolutionary stability of strategies depends on the relative strength of group-level survival effects versus within-group competition. When group survival (due to predation or intergroup conflict) is highly sensitive to group size (i.e., total fecundity), strategies that maximize group fecundity (neutral or donator) are favored, leading to higher group efficiency. When group-level effects are weak, spiteful or envious strategies can dominate, reducing group efficiency. The model shows that increasing the benefit of donation or the cost of spite/envious behavior further promotes efficient, cooperative strategies. The results are robust to parameter changes and apply to both predation and intergroup conflict scenarios.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in environments where group-level survival or payoff is highly sensitive to group size or total contribution, mechanisms that increase group efficiency (such as donation or cooperation) will be favored over spiteful or envious (punitive) strategies. While the model does not directly implement a public goods game with explicit punishment, it is adjacent: 'spiteful' and 'envious' strategies are analogous to costly punishment, and 'donator' is analogous to cooperation or reward. The findings suggest that increasing the importance of group-level outcomes (e.g., by increasing the marginal per-capita return or the impact of group payoff on survival) will make punishment less stable and cooperation more stable, thus increasing efficiency. However, the model is not a direct PGG and does not include explicit punishment technology or cost parameters as in standard PGG experiments.

- source: Forges, F., Horst, U., & Salomon, A. (2016). Feasibility and individual rationality in two-person Bayesian games. *International Journal of Game Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in two-person Bayesian games with a single informed player, the existence of a uniform punishment strategy (UPS) for the uninformed player guarantees the existence of feasible, individually rational (i.e., efficient/welfare-maximizing) solutions. These solutions can be implemented as Nash equilibria of appropriately designed noncooperative games. The set of such payoffs coincides with the set of joint plan equilibrium payoffs in the infinitely repeated game. When UPS does not hold, feasible, individually rational solutions may not exist. The results generalize to cases where both players have private information, under certain conditions. The paper provides theoretical foundations for how punishment strategies can support efficient outcomes in games with incomplete information, but does not provide empirical estimates or experimental results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the ability to punish (specifically, the existence of a uniform punishment strategy) is crucial for achieving efficient, individually rational outcomes in two-person Bayesian games with incomplete information. For the downstream prediction task, the main implication is that the presence and effectiveness of punishment mechanisms (punishment cost, punishment tech, and the ability to credibly threaten punishment) are key moderators of whether efficiency gains from punishment are possible. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical estimates, and its results are not specific to public goods games or experimental designs. It is most useful for understanding the theoretical boundary conditions under which punishment can support efficiency, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency changes in experimental PGG-like environments.

- source: Sasaki, T., Okada, I., & Nakai, Y. (2016). Indirect reciprocity can overcome free-rider problems on costly moral assessment. *Biology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with costly assessment, cooperation is not stable unless there is a mechanism to sanction or exclude those who evade the cost of assessment. By introducing a pre-assessment stage that labels non-payers as 'nasty' and excludes them from receiving help, the model shows that a cooperative equilibrium (where everyone pays for assessment and helps others) can be locally stable if the benefit of helping exceeds the cost of assessment. This mechanism is analogous to pool punishment in public goods games and is effective under leading social norms. The findings are theoretical but directly address efficiency and the conditions for stable cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that institutional mechanisms to sanction or exclude cost-evaders (such as pre-assessment or pool punishment) can significantly improve efficiency in social dilemma games with costly monitoring or punishment. For prediction tasks, the presence of a pre-assessment or institutionalized punishment mechanism is likely to shift efficiency from low (collapse of cooperation) to high (stable cooperation), provided the net benefit of cooperation is positive. The results are robust across different social norms and suggest that the design of the punishment/sanctioning mechanism is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Ognedal, T. (2016). Morality in the market. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Increasing the fraction of honest sellers can reduce or increase social surplus depending on the initial prevalence of honesty and the elasticity of demand. When honesty is rare, more honesty can increase production costs and reduce welfare; when honesty is common, more honesty can improve efficiency. (2) Sanctions (punishment) are a more reliable tool for improving efficiency, as they do not distort the ranking of private and social costs. (3) The effects of honesty among buyers and sellers are asymmetric: honesty among sellers affects production efficiency, while honesty among buyers affects allocation efficiency. (4) Policies that increase individual compliance (e.g., moral suasion) may have no effect or even negative effects on market-level efficiency, depending on market structure and the distribution of honesty. (5) The relationship between individual-level compliance and market-level efficiency is complex and non-linear.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency is more reliably positive than the effect of increasing intrinsic motivation (morality/honesty). In environments analogous to public goods games with market entry/exit and endogenous prices, punishment that is uniform across agents improves efficiency by aligning private and social costs, while increasing the fraction of intrinsically honest agents can have mixed or even negative effects on efficiency, especially when honesty is rare. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment (extrinsic sanctions) is more likely to increase efficiency than relying on intrinsic motivation, and that the effect of punishment may depend on the distribution of compliance and market structure. However, the model is not a direct PGG or experimental result, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
