  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that trust is essential for enabling players to leave secure networks and engage in market transactions that offer higher potential payoffs but also greater risk. The model shows that the willingness to transact in markets depends on the expected probability of encountering trustworthy partners, the relative payoffs from network versus market transactions, and the cost of being cheated. When trust is high and/or the gains from market transactions are large relative to networks, players are more likely to leave networks and realize higher efficiency (total payoff). Conversely, when trust is low or the cost of being cheated is high, players remain in networks, foregoing potential efficiency gains. The model highlights the distinction between trust (needed for market transactions) and assurance (provided by networks through repeated interaction and informal sanctions).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how efficiency (expected group payoff) in environments with and without trust/assurance mechanisms depends on the structure of payoffs, risk, and expectations about others' behavior. While it does not directly model public goods games or experimental punishment, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where players can choose between secure, repeated interactions (with informal sanctions) and riskier, anonymous interactions (requiring trust). The model suggests that efficiency gains from moving to market-like environments depend on the level of trust and the relative payoffs, and that mechanisms providing assurance (such as punishment or repeated interaction) can substitute for trust in supporting cooperation. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment in public goods games, so its relevance for predicting the effect of enabling punishment is indirect and primarily theoretical.

- source: Rand, D. G., Tarnita, C. E., Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Evolution of fairness in the one-shot anonymous Ultimatum Game. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Theoretical and simulation results show that, in finite populations with stochastic evolutionary dynamics, fairness (both higher offers and nonzero rejection thresholds) can be favored by natural selection in the Ultimatum Game, even without reputation or repeated interaction. The favored strategies depend on mutation rate and selection strength. The behavioral experiment supports the prediction that greater uncertainty about others' success and less behavioral consistency are associated with higher offers and/or demands. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total welfare, or surplus, and the Ultimatum Game structure is only adjacent to public goods games with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the evolution of costly punishment and fairness in strategic interactions, but its findings are not directly transferable to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The Ultimatum Game is a two-player bargaining game with a binary accept/reject punishment mechanism, not a multi-player public goods game with explicit punishment options. The main outcomes are behavioral (offer and demand), not group efficiency. Thus, while the paper supports the idea that costly punishment (rejection) can be favored and lead to fairer outcomes, it does not provide quantitative evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments. Use this paper for mechanism insight, not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A., Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2015). Apology and forgiveness evolve to resolve failures in cooperative agreements. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated social dilemmas modeled as noisy iterated Prisoner's Dilemma, the ability to make commitments and to retaliate (revenge) after commitment breakdown increases the prevalence of cooperative strategies. When apology and forgiveness are added, cooperation is further boosted, but only if apology is sufficiently costly (sincere). If apology is too cheap, fake committers exploit the system, reducing cooperation. The model shows that the optimal apology cost is above the cost of cooperation but below the penalty for breaking commitment. The results are robust to noise and repeated interaction length. While the model is not a public goods game, it is adjacent, and the mechanisms studied (commitment, retaliation, apology, forgiveness) are relevant to understanding how punishment-like mechanisms affect cooperation and group payoff in repeated social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. It shows that in repeated, noisy social dilemmas, mechanisms analogous to punishment (revenge/retaliation) and costly apology/forgiveness can increase cooperation and, by implication, group payoff/efficiency, especially when the cost and effectiveness of these mechanisms are well-calibrated. The findings suggest that enabling punishment-like responses after norm violations (commitment breakdown) and allowing for costly apology/forgiveness can improve efficiency, but only if the costs are set appropriately. The results are most transferable to repeated games with commitment and noise, and less so to one-shot or standard PGGs. The paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions for PGG efficiency, but supports the idea that punishment and compensation mechanisms can be beneficial for group outcomes under certain conditions.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A., Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2017). When agreement-accepting free-riders are a necessary evil for the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in one-shot games, the coexistence of proposers and acceptors can be necessary to keep defectors out of the population, especially when the cost of commitment must be paid before knowing if the other will accept (prior commitments). In repeated games, proposers alone can sustain cooperation, and the presence of acceptors can actually reduce cooperation by making it easier for defectors to invade. The introduction of an apology-forgiveness mechanism (where apologies after defection can restore cooperation) can be exploited by acceptors if the apology cost is too low, leading to lower cooperation. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and the stationary distribution of strategies, with average payoff as the fitness criterion, but do not directly report group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it does offer adjacent theoretical insights about how commitment mechanisms (which can be seen as a form of pre-emptive contract or compensation for defection) affect the prevalence of cooperation and the exclusion of defectors. The findings suggest that the design of commitment mechanisms (timing of costs, ability to recognize intentions, presence of apology-forgiveness) can strongly moderate the success of cooperation-promoting strategies and thus the average payoff in the population. However, because the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma and not a public goods game, and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning as in PGGs, its direct predictive value for the downstream task is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding how alternative mechanisms to punishment (commitments, compensation, apology) might affect cooperation and payoff in related social dilemmas.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2015). Reputation Effects in Public and Private Interactions. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of cooperative strategies in indirect reciprocity depends critically on the observability of private actions (q), the frequency of public interactions (p), and the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c). Honest cooperation (leading to full group efficiency) is only stable when private actions are sufficiently observable and b/c is high. When private actions are rarely observed or b/c is low, only partial cooperation or defection is stable, resulting in lower group efficiency. The model provides explicit analytical thresholds for these transitions. The presence of public observation increases the incentive for cooperation, but if private actions are unobserved, individuals may defect in private, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how observability (analogous to information feedback or monitoring in public goods games) and the benefit-to-cost ratio moderate the effect of reputation-based incentives on group efficiency. While not a direct PGG or punishment study, its results suggest that increasing the probability that actions are observed (or reputationally consequential) increases the likelihood of stable, high-efficiency cooperation. In prediction tasks, similar mechanisms (e.g., transparency, monitoring, or reputation updating) should be expected to increase the efficiency gains from reputation or sanctioning systems, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high. However, if observability is low, the efficiency impact of such systems will be limited.

- source: Phillips, T., Li, J. W., & Kendall, G. (2014). The Effects of Extra-Somatic Weapons on the Evolution of Human Cooperation towards Non-Kin. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Simulating a population of 40 players using 25 well-known IPD strategies, the authors compare two conditions: a standard evolutionary IPD and a 'weapons use' IPD where repeated defections trigger a risk of elimination (interpreted as a proxy for lethal punishment or high-cost disputes). Cooperative strategies, on average, survive significantly longer under weapons use, while non-cooperative strategies survive for fewer generations. The 'Always Cooperate' strategy, typically the least successful, becomes the most successful cooperative strategy as the risk of elimination increases. The model suggests that high costs for repeated defection (analogous to punishment) can shift evolutionary success toward cooperation, even in the absence of explicit monitoring or repeated interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that increasing the cost of within-group conflict (modeled as a risk of elimination after repeated defections) can increase the evolutionary success of cooperative strategies and decrease that of non-cooperative strategies. While the model is not a public goods game and does not report group efficiency per se, the findings are relevant for predicting that introducing or increasing the severity of punishment in repeated social dilemmas is likely to increase the relative payoff or evolutionary success of cooperation. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment is severe and unavoidable, and less so to standard PGGs with explicit, voluntary punishment stages. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of specific game design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, chat), but supports the general principle that costly sanctions can promote cooperation and group-level success.

- source: Angourakis, A., Santos, J. I., Galán, J. M., & Balbo, A. L. (2015). Food for all: An agent-based model to explore the emergence and implications of cooperation for food storage. *Environmental Archaeology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The model demonstrates that cooperation in food storage is most likely to emerge and persist when (1) cooperative storage is at least as efficient as household storage, (2) there is moderate to high dependency on storable food, and (3) social norms regulating access to the cooperative stock are neither too strict nor too lax. Too strict enforcement (high reciprocity requirement) can undermine cooperation by excluding agents who fail to contribute due to bad luck, while too lax enforcement allows free-riding to undermine the common stock. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff in the public goods sense, but shows that cooperation increases the probability of avoiding shortages and, under certain conditions, can lead to surplus. The findings suggest that moderate sanctioning (via access control) is optimal for sustaining cooperation in common-pool resource settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff in a public goods game with explicit punishment, it models a closely related common-pool resource dilemma where access to the common resource is conditional on past cooperation (a form of sanction). The results suggest that intermediate levels of sanctioning (not too strict, not too lax) maximize cooperation and system performance (in terms of shortage avoidance and potential surplus). For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment or exclusion mechanisms that are too harsh may reduce efficiency by discouraging recovery from bad luck, while too little enforcement allows free-riding. The model also highlights the importance of storage efficiency and dependency on the resource, which may map onto MPCR and baseline efficiency in public goods games. However, direct quantitative predictions of efficiency with and without punishment are not available from this model.

- source: Shutters, S. T., & Hales, D. (2013). Tag-Mediated Altruism is Contingent on How Cheaters Are Defined. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the sustainability of tag-mediated altruism is highly sensitive to how cheaters are defined. When cheaters are defined as agents who can never donate (strong cheaters), altruism collapses completely and rapidly. With medium cheaters (who can revert to altruism via mutation), donation rates are very low. Only with ultraweak or weak cheaters do substantial donation rates persist. The paper also finds that parameters such as neighborhood size, number of pairings, and mutation rate affect donation rates, but the presence of strong cheaters overwhelms these effects. The authors suggest that additional mechanisms, such as punishment or multi-level selection, may be necessary to sustain cooperation in the presence of strong cheaters, but these are not modeled directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or close variants. It does not implement punishment or sanctions, nor does it report group efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it is informative in showing that, in tag-based donation models, the presence of strong cheaters (analogous to unpunished defectors) eliminates cooperation, and that additional mechanisms (such as punishment) may be required to sustain altruism. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that in environments where strong, unpunished defection is possible, efficiency will be low unless additional mechanisms (like punishment) are present. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct manipulation of punishment, so its value for prediction is mainly conceptual and indirect.

- source: Huang, K. K., Zheng, X. P., Li, Z. J., & Yang, Y. Q. (2015). Understanding Cooperative Behavior Based on the Coevolution of Game Strategy and Link Weight. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that allowing link weights between players to coevolve with strategies in a spatial prisoner's dilemma can significantly enhance the fraction of cooperators, especially at intermediate values of link weight amplitude. The mechanism works by reinforcing links to successful neighbors and weakening links to less successful ones, which leads to the formation and expansion of cooperator clusters. The effect is non-monotonic: too little or too much link weight adaptation is less effective than an intermediate level. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods or punishment games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It uses a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a PGG) and studies the coevolution of strategies and link weights (not explicit punishment or sanctions). The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency or payoff. However, the findings may be contextually informative for understanding how dynamic network structures or adaptive relationships can promote cooperation, which could be relevant for models where network adaptation or endogenous partner selection is a feature. For the core prediction task, this paper offers only adjacent, indirect support.

- source: Vukov, J. (2014). Accurate reactions open up the way for more cooperative societies. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that agents with more accurate and detailed memory (mem-1 strategies) can achieve much higher levels of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma than agents with only reactive memory. This effect is robust across a wide range of temptation-to-defect values, provided that the copying process is accurate (low error/noise). When copying errors increase, cooperation collapses. The paper interprets these results as showing that cognitive sophistication enables more stable and widespread cooperation, which in turn is associated with higher average payoffs, though payoff is not the primary outcome. There is no explicit punishment or sanctioning institution; instead, retaliation and forgiveness are embedded in the strategy space.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms. Instead, it analyzes how cognitive sophistication in memory and strategy (allowing for retaliation and forgiveness) affects cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The findings suggest that mechanisms enabling accurate, context-sensitive responses (such as retaliation or forgiveness) can promote cooperation and, by implication, higher payoffs, but this is not directly mapped to efficiency or to institutional punishment. The results may be useful for understanding the role of memory, strategy complexity, and error in sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment interventions on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Tanimoto, J., & Sagara, H. (2015). How the indirect reciprocity with co-evolving norm and strategy for 2 x 2 prisoner's dilemma game works for emerging cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that stable cooperation via indirect reciprocity is more robust in environments with a stronger dilemma (higher temptation to defect), as this encourages the evolution of norms that punish second-order free-riders. In contrast, weaker dilemmas allow permissive norms to dominate, leading to fragile cooperation. Allowing agents to interact with others holding different norms undermines indirect reciprocity unless the image score is evaluated by the focal agent. Introducing a small action error (randomness) can help robust, punishing norms emerge by preventing the early dominance of overly permissive norms. The main dependent variable is the cooperation fraction; average payoff is shown but not analyzed in depth.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the role of norm-based punishment (targeting second-order free-riders) in sustaining cooperation in social dilemma environments. However, it does not use a public goods game, does not implement explicit costly punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. The findings suggest that mechanisms which allow for the punishment of second-order free-riders (whether via norms or explicit punishment) may be important for robust cooperation, especially in more challenging (stronger dilemma) environments. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the structure of sanctioning (including norm evolution and the ability to punish non-punishers) can be critical, but it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Godino, I. B. I., Santos, J. I., Galán, J. M., Caro, J., Alvarez, M., & Zurro, D. (2014). Social Cooperation and Resource Management DynamicsAmong Late Hunter-Fisher-Gatherer Societies in Tierra del Fuego (South America). *Journal of Archaeological Method and Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The ABM shows that cooperation (publicly calling others to share a resource) is stable when social capital (reputation) is valued in the fitness function and when the visibility of actions (vision) is high, making it easier to detect defectors. When only material gain matters, defection dominates. The system tends to polarize into either high cooperation or high defection regimes, with intermediate states rare. The model highlights the importance of indirect reciprocity and the public observability of actions for sustaining cooperation. However, the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms increasing the visibility of actions and the value of social reputation can promote cooperation in public-goods-like dilemmas. It suggests that punishment via social exclusion or reputation loss (rather than explicit costly punishment) can sustain cooperation if actions are observable and social capital is valued. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of observability and social capital as moderators of cooperation, which may inform the interpretation of similar dimensions in empirical studies.

- source: Cui, G. H., Wang, Z., Ren, J. K., Lu, K., & Li, M. C. (2016). Promotion of cooperation induced by discriminators in the spatial multi-player donor-recipient game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The introduction of a discriminative cooperation strategy in a spatial multi-player donor-recipient game can maintain high levels of cooperation and prevent the dominance of defectors, especially when the cost-to-benefit ratio is low and the cost of information collection is small. Discriminators act as conditional cooperators who deny resources to defectors, which functions as a form of indirect punishment. The model shows that, under certain parameter regimes, all three strategies (cooperation, defection, discrimination) can coexist in a cyclic dominance pattern, and the ratio of donated to total resources can remain high. However, the model does not directly report group efficiency or total welfare in the sense of public goods games, and the structure is not a standard PGG.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that conditional, discriminative strategies (akin to targeted punishment or exclusion) can sustain cooperation and high resource donation in spatial multi-player settings, especially when the cost of such discrimination is low. However, because the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, its findings should be transferred to PGG-like prediction tasks with caution. The main implication is that mechanisms which allow players to conditionally withhold benefits from defectors (even at a cost) can be effective in maintaining cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency in PGGs is not directly established.

- source: Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., Paiva, A., & Santos, F. C. (2017). Structural power and the evolution of collective fairness in social networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the structural power of a network—a measure of how often individuals appear in each other's groups—strongly predicts the evolution of fairer strategies in Multiplayer Ultimatum Games. Networks with high SP (such as regular rings or modular community structures) promote higher average offers and acceptance thresholds, leading to more equitable outcomes. The mechanism is that repeated interactions within overlapping groups allow individuals to exert more influence and enforce fairness, analogous to effective punishment. However, the paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral measures of fairness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency in public-goods-like environments with punishment, by showing that network structure (specifically, high structural power) can promote fairer behavioral outcomes in group bargaining games. However, because the model is based on the Multiplayer Ultimatum Game (not a public goods game), does not include explicit costly punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that structural features enabling repeated peer influence may enhance the effectiveness of fairness enforcement (analogous to punishment), but do not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or other design dimensions.

- source: Powers, S. T., van Schaik, C. P., & Lehmann, L. (2016). How institutions shaped the last major evolutionary transition to large-scale human societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that the ability to create and enforce institutional rules, including sanctioning mechanisms, is a key driver of large-scale human cooperation and division of labor. Institutions can transform the default equilibrium of social dilemmas (such as public goods games) to more cooperative, higher-payoff outcomes by enabling coordination, information sharing, and self-enforcing sanctioning. Theoretical arguments and historical/anthropological examples are used to support the claim that institutions can increase group efficiency and welfare, but the paper does not provide new empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or institutional design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that enabling punishment or sanctioning mechanisms within an institutional framework can increase efficiency and group payoffs in public-goods-like environments, especially as group size increases. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes as a function of game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its value for prediction is in supporting the plausibility and mechanism of positive effects of institutions (including punishment) on efficiency, but it does not inform the magnitude or specific moderators of these effects.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., & Delton, A. W. (2016). Are Humans Too Generous and Too Punitive? Using Psychological Principles to Further Debates about Human Social Evolution. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper argues that human generosity and punitive behavior, even in one-shot or group contexts, can be explained by evolved psychological mechanisms that are sensitive to uncertainty and error costs, rather than requiring group selection. Agent-based simulations cited by the authors show that cooperation and punishment can evolve under individual selection, with quantitative variation in punitive tendencies supporting group cooperation. However, the paper does not report direct empirical or experimental results on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for why punishment and cooperation may be observed in public goods game-like environments, even when classic evolutionary theory would not predict them. However, it does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in such games. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency outcomes, this paper is mainly useful for understanding possible psychological mechanisms and for interpreting why punishment may be present, but it does not inform the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes due to punishment under specific game design dimensions.

- source: Forber, P., & Smead, R. (2016). The Evolution of Spite, Recognition, and Morality. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that mechanisms enabling recognition and conditional behavior can support both altruism and spite, but with important asymmetries. Recognition based on exogenous signals (e.g., greenbeard effects) is more evolutionarily stable with spite than with altruism, because mimics threaten altruism more than spite. In contrast, reciprocity based on past behavior (as in repeated games) is more conducive to the evolution of altruism than spite. The models show that reciprocal altruism evolves readily in repeated games, while reciprocal spite is rare. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, but rather the evolutionary stability and frequency of these behaviors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its models are adjacent, focusing on the evolutionary conditions for the emergence and stability of conditional harming (spite) and helping (altruism) behaviors, and the coevolution of recognition mechanisms. While it discusses repeated games and conditional strategies, it does not report or predict group efficiency, welfare, or payoff outcomes. The main relevance is conceptual: it suggests that the evolutionary stability of punishment-like behaviors depends on the mechanism of recognition and the type of conditionality (signal-based vs. reciprocity), but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency effects in PGG-like games.

- source: McNally, L., & Jackson, A. L. (2013). Cooperation creates selection for tactical deception. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that conditional cooperation mechanisms (including punishment and reward) can select for the evolution of tactical deception, as deceivers can exploit conditional cooperators by hiding their defection. Theoretical results show that, under certain conditions (notably negative frequency dependence in deception detection), cooperation and deception can coexist at equilibrium. Comparative analysis in primates supports the prediction that more cooperative species exhibit more tactical deception. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, nor does it experimentally manipulate punishment or public goods game parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its theoretical model is based on the iterated prisoner's dilemma and focuses on the evolutionary dynamics of deception and cooperation, not on payoff or efficiency outcomes. While it discusses punishment as one of several conditional cooperation mechanisms, it does not analyze the quantitative impact of punishment on group efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it offers only indirect, contextual support for the idea that enforcement mechanisms can have complex evolutionary consequences, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

- source: Sterelny, K. (2016). Cooperation, Culture, and Conflict. *British Journal for the Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation evolved in two major transitions: first, among Pleistocene foragers, where cooperation was individually advantageous and stabilized by mutualism, reciprocation, and punishment of freeriders (both shirkers and bullies); and second, in the Holocene, where larger, more hierarchical societies required new mechanisms (including group selection and intergroup conflict) to maintain cooperation. Punishment is discussed as a key mechanism for controlling freeriding and maintaining cooperation, but the analysis is conceptual and evolutionary rather than experimental or quantitative. The paper references experimental public goods games and their findings (e.g., punishment stabilizes cooperation), but only as background for theoretical arguments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and norm enforcement may affect cooperation and, by extension, efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. However, it supports the idea that punishment is more likely to stabilize cooperation (and thus efficiency) in small, transparent, repeated-interaction groups, and that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on social structure, group size, and transparency. For prediction tasks, this paper is best used as background for mechanism hypotheses rather than as a source of direct predictive evidence.

The model predicts that individuals will use both costly and costless signals to communicate their type (altruistic or spiteful), and that the availability of communication can affect the use of costly signals like punishment or reward. The paper discusses, but does not empirically test, the implications for public goods games, including the idea that punishment may serve as a signal of ill will and that communication may substitute for punishment. No direct findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes are provided.

While it suggests that communication opportunities may reduce the use of costly punishment (and vice versa), and that punishment can serve as a signal of ill will, it does not provide data or direct analysis of efficiency outcomes. - source: Schimit, P. H. T., Santos, B. O., & Soares, C. A. (2015). Evolution of cooperation in Axelrod tournament using cellular automata. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial evolutionary Prisoner's Dilemma, the success of cooperative strategies depends strongly on the fitness function used for strategy updating. When fitness functions penalize aggressive (high 'caused death') strategies, cooperation rates and the prevalence of cooperative strategies increase. When there is no such penalty, defectors dominate. However, even with punishment-like strategies or fitness penalties, extreme punishment is not always effective at promoting cooperation. Tit-for-tat is not the most successful strategy in this spatial, evolutionary context. The model does not directly measure group efficiency or total group payoff, but higher cooperation is associated with longer average lifetimes (a proxy for individual fitness). The results are not directly transferable to public goods games with explicit costly punishment, but suggest that the structure of incentives for aggression and retaliation can shape cooperation outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides only indirect and adjacent evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The model is a spatial evolutionary Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, and does not implement explicit costly punishment as a player choice. Instead, some strategies are retaliatory, and some fitness functions penalize aggression. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and strategy prevalence, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that penalizing aggression (analogous to punishing defectors) can increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not directly measured. For prediction tasks, this paper may inform the qualitative expectation that punishment or penalties for defection can promote cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency in public goods games cannot be inferred from these results.

- source: Yukalov, V. I., Yukalova, E. P., & Sornette, D. (2013). Utility Rate Equations of Group Population Dynamics in Biological and Social Systems. *Plos One*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The authors develop a system-theoretic, utility-based model for the evolution of group fractions (cooperators, defectors, regulators) in self-organized societies. They show analytically that, under resource constraints, neither defectors nor regulators can exceed about 10% of the population in stable equilibrium. The model demonstrates that cooperation can persist without explicit punishment mechanisms, provided resources are finite. When regulators (punishers) are present, their fraction is also limited by resource costs. The model is applied to real-world data from countries, but only to estimate group fractions and stability, not efficiency or payoff. No direct predictions or measurements of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus are made.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar experimental designs. It offers a theoretical perspective that, in resource-constrained societies, both defectors and regulators (punishers) are limited in equilibrium, and cooperation can be stable even without explicit punishment. However, the model does not map onto the game design dimensions or efficiency outcomes used in experimental PGG research. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, but may provide indirect, high-level context about the limits of punishment and defection in self-organized systems.

The paper argues that synergy (e.g., from division of labor) is often the most important factor for the evolution and stability of cooperation, and that these insights apply to both biological and economic systems. The paper references simulation and empirical studies (by the author and others) that support the importance of synergy, but does not provide new data or direct quantitative results. The discussion includes mention of cost/benefit ratios, payoff matrices, and the role of punishment in norm enforcement, but these are not analyzed in a public-goods-game-like experimental context.

However, it offers theoretical arguments that social synergy (non-additive benefits from cooperation) and assortation (homophily) are key moderators of cooperation and group outcomes, potentially more important than kinship or direct punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that game designs which enable or amplify synergy (e.g., through division of labor or mechanisms that create win-win outcomes) are likely to see higher efficiency, regardless of the presence or absence of punishment. The paper does not provide specific guidance on how punishment, its cost, or its implementation affect efficiency in controlled experimental settings.

  findings: The paper argues that the stability of waiting lines among strangers is best explained by the human tendency toward strong reciprocity: people are inclined to cooperate when they see others doing so and to punish norm violators, even at personal cost. It draws on public goods game experiments to support this model, noting that people will punish defectors even when it is costly. The paper describes how informal sanctions (verbal objections, shaming, and sometimes violence) deter line-cutting and maintain order. It also discusses cultural and contextual variation in queue norms, exceptions to the rules, and the role of service providers and technology. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoffs, nor does it manipulate or systematically analyze game design dimensions.

However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments.   outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the term 'altruism' is used inconsistently across disciplines, leading to confusion and overstated claims, especially when transferring findings between fields like experimental economics and evolutionary biology. It argues for a clear taxonomy distinguishing psychological, reproductive, behavioural, and preference altruism. The authors show that much of the experimental economics literature (including public goods games with punishment) is best understood in terms of behavioural or preference altruism, not reproductive altruism. The paper cautions against conflating outcome-based and motivation-based definitions, and against assuming that findings about punishment or cooperation in economic games directly inform evolutionary or moral questions.

However, it is useful for clarifying that many studies in experimental economics (including those on punishment in public goods games) are about behavioural or preference altruism, and that their outcomes (such as increased cooperation or punishment behavior) should not be conflated with broader evolutionary or moral claims. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that careful attention should be paid to the definitions and outcome measures used in empirical studies, and that efficiency or payoff outcomes are distinct from measures of cooperation or punishment frequency.

- source: Waldeck, R. (2013). Segregated Cooperation. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a prisoner's dilemma with internalized norm adherence (modeled as a private emotional cost for defecting), local interaction structures (small neighborhoods) can lead to persistent, segregated clusters of cooperators, resulting in higher average fitness (payoff) for cooperators compared to defectors. This effect is robust to various simulation parameters (error rate, number of active players, population size) but is highly sensitive to the distribution of emotional adherence and the cost of cooperation. When the basin of attraction for defection is large (e.g., high cost of cooperation or unfavorable emotion distribution), cooperation collapses even in local structures. No explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present; all norm enforcement is via internalized emotion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods or PD games, as it does not include any explicit punishment mechanism. Instead, it models norm adherence as an internalized emotional cost. The findings are relevant for understanding how local interaction structure and heterogeneity in norm adherence can affect efficiency and cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of enabling punishment. Thus, this paper should not be used to predict the efficiency impact of punishment interventions, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in environments with internalized norm enforcement and local interactions.

- source: Ren, Y. Z., Li, M. C., Xiang, Y., Cui, Y. R., & Sakurai, K. (2013). Evolution of cooperation in reputation system by group-based scheme. *Journal of Supercomputing*.

  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: Simulations show that introducing group competition (group selection) in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environment leads to higher cooperation rates and significantly higher average individual payoffs compared to individual selection alone. The effect is robust across different benefit-to-cost ratios and group sizes. Higher benefit-to-cost ratios and smaller group sizes (with constant group number) further enhance cooperation and average payoff. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, but demonstrates that group-based structuring alone can improve efficiency in reputation systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that group selection mechanisms (group competition) can substantially increase efficiency (average payoff) in repeated social dilemma environments similar to public goods games, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward. While it does not directly manipulate or analyze punishment, it is informative for predicting efficiency in designs where group structure or group-level selection is present. The findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing group competition or selection can be expected to increase efficiency relative to individual selection. However, the results do not directly inform the effect of punishment mechanisms, so their predictive value for punishment-enabled vs. control efficiency is limited unless group selection is a relevant design feature.

  findings: The study finds that allowing for fraternal (other-regarding) player types in spatial social dilemmas leads to the coexistence of egoist and fraternal strategies, often in spatially ordered patterns. The presence of fraternal players reduces the parameter region where only defectors survive, thus supporting higher levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs. The model demonstrates that spatial structure and personality diversity can promote cooperation and mitigate the tragedy of the commons, even without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms.

However, the model is not a public goods game and does not include punishment or reward treatments. The findings suggest that, in environments where player types or preferences can vary, the introduction of pro-social types can have a positive effect on efficiency, analogous to the effect of some institutional interventions. For direct prediction of punishment effects in PGGs, this paper is only weakly informative, but it supports the general principle that mechanisms reducing pure self-interest can improve efficiency.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2016). Imperfect information facilitates the evolution of reciprocity. *Mathematical Biosciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of imperfect information on the evolution of reciprocity (and thus on group efficiency/payoff) depends on the structure of payoffs. When payoffs are linear, imperfect information always makes reciprocity (cooperation) less likely to evolve, reducing group efficiency. However, when payoffs are nonlinear (synergetic effects), imperfect information can sometimes make reciprocity more likely to evolve, increasing group efficiency. The analysis is theoretical, based on evolutionary stability conditions for tit-for-tat (TFT) versus always-defect (ALLD) strategies in the iterated prisoner's dilemma with mistakes and imperfect information. The results do not generalize directly to public goods games or to settings with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information structure and payoff nonlinearity can affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency in repeated dyadic games. However, it does not address public goods games, punishment, or sanctions, and thus is only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The main lesson is that the effect of information and error structure on efficiency can be non-monotonic and depend on payoff nonlinearity, but this does not directly inform the effect of punishment in multi-player public goods games.

- source: Xu, M., Zheng, D. F., Xu, C., & Hui, P. M. (2017). Three-strategy N-person snowdrift game incorporating loners. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a loner strategy into the N-person snowdrift game leads to three possible steady states: all cooperators (AllC), all loners (AllL), or a mixed state of cooperators and defectors (C+D). The AllC state, which corresponds to maximum efficiency, is only possible in a very small region of parameter space (low cost/benefit ratio r and high loner payoff L). Most of the parameter space results in either a C+D state (intermediate efficiency) or an AllL state (lower efficiency, as loners receive a fixed, suboptimal payoff). The presence of loners can help eliminate defectors in the transient, enabling the possibility of an AllC state, but this is rare. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. However, it does provide informative theoretical results about how the availability of an outside option (loner strategy) affects group efficiency and the conditions under which full cooperation can be achieved. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative about punishment effects, but it does highlight that the structure of available strategies (e.g., presence of loners) and the payoff parameters can strongly affect efficiency outcomes. The absence of punishment or reward means it cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment, but it may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in adjacent game structures.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

In structured populations, higher network degree (more neighbors) makes cooperation harder to sustain. The analysis provides explicit formulas for the critical benefit-to-cost ratio as a function of group size, thresholds, and network structure. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

However, it provides detailed theoretical results on how group size, thresholds, and network structure affect the baseline efficiency and cooperation in a closely related multi-player game. These results may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in control conditions without punishment, but cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

The model does not include punishment or sanctions, and thus does not address their effects.

However, it does provide theoretical support for the importance of voluntary participation and payoff structure in sustaining efficient cooperation.

- source: Lewis, H. M., Vinicius, L., Strods, J., Mace, R., & Migliano, A. B. (2014). High mobility explains demand sharing and enforced cooperation in egalitarian hunter-gatherers. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The simulation demonstrates that enforced demand sharing (forced equal sharing of food among all camp members) combined with high mobility enables populations to survive in unpredictable environments, maintain high group efficiency (measured by survival, energy surplus, and life expectancy), and buffer against the presence of free riders. In contrast, loner (non-sharing) populations and sedentary (non-mobile) demand-sharing populations are much more likely to collapse, especially under harsh environmental conditions. The model explicitly excludes punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, showing that high efficiency and cooperation can be maintained without punishment, as long as mobility is high. The findings are directly relevant to understanding the efficiency of enforced cooperation in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the effect of adding punishment to a public-goods-like environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as punishment is not present in the model. Instead, it shows that enforced cooperation (demand sharing) combined with high mobility can maintain high efficiency and group survival without the need for punishment. For the downstream prediction task—predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency—this paper suggests that, in some environments, enforced cooperation and mobility may substitute for punishment, but it does not provide direct evidence on the marginal effect of punishment. The model is adjacent to PGGs, but the absence of a punishment condition means it cannot be used to predict the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Smaldino, P. E., Schank, J. C., & McElreath, R. (2013). Increased Costs of Cooperation Help Cooperators in the Long Run. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model demonstrates that in spatially structured populations where survival requires occasional cooperation, increasing the cost of living or the cost of unreciprocated cooperation can promote the long-term survival and dominance of cooperators. This occurs because defectors, while initially advantaged, eventually die out when they cannot find enough cooperators to exploit, allowing cooperator clusters to recover and expand. However, if costs become too high, the population may collapse entirely. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the frequency of cooperators, not explicit group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant to understanding how environmental adversity and the cost structure of cooperation can affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatially structured populations, but it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency outcomes or the impact of punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions involving punishment.

- source: Molleman, L., Pen, I., & Weissing, F. J. (2013). Effects of Conformism on the Cultural Evolution of Social Behaviour. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of conformism on the evolution of cooperation depends on the game structure. In social dilemmas, conformism can make cooperation a stable equilibrium if it is already common, but in group-structured populations, conformism hinders the spread of cooperation because it prevents rare cooperative strategies from invading. In evasion games, conformism erodes polymorphic equilibria and leads to fixation of a single strategy. The main outcomes are fixation probabilities and average payoffs at equilibrium, which are related to efficiency. The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms, and does not directly model public goods games, but the results are relevant for understanding how social learning biases affect group-level cooperation and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms. However, it does provide theoretical insight into how social learning rules (payoff-based vs. conformist) affect the spread and stability of cooperation and group payoffs in two-strategy games, including social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant: it suggests that mechanisms which stabilize cooperation (such as conformism) may not always increase group efficiency, especially in group-structured populations. However, since punishment is not modeled, and the game structure is not a PGG or its direct variant, this paper should be considered only for background or mechanistic context, not for quantitative prediction.

- source: Podobnik, B., Jusup, M., Wang, Z., & Stanley, H. E. (2017). How Fear of Future Outcomes Affects Social Dynamics. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of benevolence (and thus group-level efficiency or welfare) in an open society depends on the interplay between insiders' tolerance, the assimilation rate of outsiders, and the network structure of interactions. As the fraction of outsiders increases, benevolence (measured by the fitness ratio R) generally decreases. If insiders' tolerance is too low or assimilation is too slow, the system can experience a sudden breakdown of cooperation (herd behavior), leading to antagonism and loss of mutual benefit. If tolerance and assimilation are balanced, a stable mutualistic equilibrium can be maintained. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it does provide indirect insight into how the withdrawal of benevolence (analogous to defection or exclusion) can destabilize cooperation and reduce group welfare. The model's outcomes are determined by tolerance and assimilation parameters, not by punishment or reward. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2014). Randomness in the network inhibits cooperation based on the bounded rational collective altruistic decision. *Journal of Physics A-mathematical and Theoretical*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial PDG with sequential strategies and bounded rational collective altruistic decision-making, high levels of cooperation can be achieved when the network is regular (low randomness). As network randomness increases, the average frequency of mutual cooperation decreases, especially when some groups have low connectivity. Groups with fewer connections ('straying groups') are less able to evolve cooperative strategies and act as obstacles to overall cooperation. Changes in the number of groups or the number of strategies per group do not significantly affect cooperation, but lower connectivity and longer strategy sequences can slow or inhibit the evolution of cooperation. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or analyzed.

It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related mechanisms. Its findings about network structure and bounded rationality may be contextually relevant for understanding cooperation dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on cooperation frequency rather than payoff or efficiency limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

  findings: The study finds that in a spatial continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma with limited resources, lower total resource and intermediate temptation to defect can promote higher average cooperation (measured by the average characteristic parameter, CP). However, the model does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The results are primarily about the evolution of cooperation and survival under resource constraints.

- source: Wang, X. W., Nie, S., Jiang, L. L., Wang, B. H., & Chen, S. M. (2017). Role of delay-based reward in the spatial cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing delay-based rewards (where the reward for cooperation is discounted by the time delay before it is received) can significantly increase the level of cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, especially on square lattice networks. The effect is strongest when the difference in reward between different delays is intermediate (i.e., not too small or too large). The cooperation level is maximized at a specific discounting factor (K ≈ 1.0). The effect is weaker in random and small-world networks. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is focused on reward (not punishment), and the main outcome is cooperation level, not efficiency or group payoff. The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. Therefore, its findings are only weakly relevant for the downstream prediction task, and should not be used as primary evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

These cycles occur for a wide range of mutation structures and parameter values, and can be sparked by arbitrarily small mutation rates and complexity costs. However, the analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment, reward, or explicit efficiency outcomes.

- source: He, J. Z., Zhao, Y., Cai, H. J., & Wang, R. W. (2013). Spatial games and the maintenance of cooperation in an asymmetric Hawk-Dove game. *Chinese Science Bulletin*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial asymmetric Hawk-Dove game, the probability of cooperation by strong players increases as the benefit-to-cost ratio decreases, while weak players tend to defect. When the benefit-to-cost ratio is sufficiently small, cooperation by strong players dominates. The degree of asymmetry also affects the distribution of strategies: higher asymmetry reduces defection by strong players but increases defection by weak players. The system can exhibit chaotic or oscillating patterns in strategy frequencies, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is near a threshold. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main finding is that in a spatial Ultimatum Game, when proposers allocate more chips to less successful opponents (negative correlation), the population evolves toward higher average offers and acceptance thresholds, interpreted as greater fairness. When more chips are allocated to more successful opponents (positive correlation), fairness is reduced. The effect is robust across different network topologies and is amplified by broader group interaction. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or punishment, and is not a public goods game, but provides adjacent evidence about how resource allocation rules affect fairness-related behavioral outcomes.

- source: Lu, P., & Wang, F. (2015). Heterogeneity of inferring reputation probability in cooperative behaviors for the spatial prisoners' dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that the mean probability with which agents can correctly identify and imitate the most reputable neighbor ('inferring reputation probability') has a nonlinear effect on cooperation: increasing the mean from low values (<0.5) strongly increases cooperation, but further increases stabilize or even reduce cooperation. Heterogeneity in this probability (standard deviation) has effects that depend on the mean: it can reduce cooperation when the mean is low, but promote cooperation when the mean is high. All results are in terms of cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings on cooperation rate in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma with heterogeneous imitation of reputation are only weakly relevant to the prediction task, and only as very indirect context for how heterogeneity in social learning might affect cooperation. It should not be used as primary evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the weight of other-regarding preferences (alpha) promotes cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with synchronous updating. For low alpha, cooperation collapses; for high alpha, cooperation dominates. At intermediate alpha, a 'ping-pong effect' emerges, with large fluctuations in the fraction of cooperators. The study analyzes the micro-dynamics of strategy switching but does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes, and the game structure is a spatial prisoner's dilemma with a self-questioning update rule, not a public goods game. The findings about other-regarding preferences and cooperation rates may be contextually informative for understanding how social preferences affect cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Sekiguchi, T. (2013). General conditions for strategy abundance through a self-referential mechanism under weak selection. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that, under weak selection, the self-referential updating rule (where players are more likely to abandon their strategy if dissatisfied with their payoff) leads to a greater difference in the stationary frequencies of favored versus unfavored strategies than the standard Moran process. This holds for both symmetric and asymmetric games. When mutation rates are small, the two updating rules yield nearly identical results. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not address efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or sanctions. The results are relevant for understanding how different evolutionary updating rules affect the abundance of strategies, but not for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or punishment games.
